# (1917-1930), which was made from the same Greek. Checked and built by tools/modern_enneads.py.

#   @treatise <Ennead.treatise> | <title>   a treatise, with its summary on the lines after
#   @ch <chapter> | <short title>            a chapter of the Greek (I.1.1), with its gist on the lines after
#   @older <n> <n> ...                       MacKenna's chapters shown with it, where his numbers are not the Greek's
#                                            (by default his chapter of the same number)
#   @p                                       a paragraph of the modern version
#   @greek <first words>                     where the paragraph begins in the chapter's Greek
#                                            (the first paragraph of a chapter begins with the chapter)
#   !flag <MacKenna's words> | <why>         a hard place, or where MacKenna's English and the Greek differ;
#                                            his words are found, word for word, in his chapters shown with it
#   !note <text>                             a note on the paragraph
# Lines starting with # are comments.

# Porphyry's Life of Plotinus (milestones E5 and E6), set first, as Porphyry set it before his edition. Its Greek is Volkmann's
# vol. I (1883), proofread from the page images (sources/neoplatonists/proofread/porphyry-life-plotinus.txt), its chapters
# numbered as Volkmann numbers them ("Life.1"); MacKenna's English of it opens his vol. I (1917). In chapters 4 to 6 Porphyry
# lists the treatises by title and first words; the numbers and Ennead places in square brackets are the Workshop's.

@treatise Life | Porphyry, On the life of Plotinus and the order of his books
Porphyry, Plotinus' pupil, wrote this life of his teacher about thirty years after his death and set it before his edition of the treatises, the Enneads. He begins with Plotinus' death and works back: his illness and last words, his birth, his search for a teacher until he found Ammonius at Alexandria, his years in Rome. Then he lists the fifty-four treatises in the order Plotinus wrote them, with their first words, and describes the circle around him: his pupils, the senators, the women and the orphans in his care, his way of writing and of teaching, his gift for reading character, the attack of a rival by magic, and the god that was found to be his guardian daemon; his way of teaching and writing, his answers to the Gnostics and to the charge of copying Numenius, the judgement of the critic Longinus, and Apollo's oracle on where his soul had gone. Last he explains how he arranged the treatises by subject into six sets of nine, the Enneads, and lists them.

@ch Life.1 | Ashamed to be in a body
Plotinus seemed ashamed of being in a body, and would not speak of his family or his country, or sit for a portrait: the body is already an image, and a portrait would be an image of an image. Amelius had a painter, Carterius, watch him at the meetings and paint him from memory.

@p
Plotinus, the philosopher of our own time, seemed like a man ashamed to be in a body. Because of this frame of mind he could not bear to talk about his family, his parents or his homeland. He thought it so far beneath him to sit for a painter or a sculptor that, when Amelius asked him to allow a likeness of him to be made, he said: "Is it not enough to carry the image that nature has wrapped around us? Must I also agree to leave behind an image of the image, to last longer, as if it were one of the works worth looking at?"
!note "An image of the image": Plato's Republic X (597e-598b) puts the painter's work third from the truth, the imitation of an appearance. For Plotinus the body is already an image (εἴδωλον) that nature has put around us.

@p
@greek ὅθεν ἀπαγορεύοντος
So, since he refused and would not sit for it, Amelius, who had a friend, Carterius, the best of the painters of that time, had him come in and attend the meetings (anyone who wished could come to the meetings), and trained him, by paying closer and closer attention, to take in more striking impressions from what he saw. Then, as Carterius drew the likeness from the image stored in his memory, and Amelius helped him correct the sketch towards a resemblance, Carterius' talent made a portrait of him very like him, without Plotinus knowing.

@ch Life.2 | Illness, death and birth
Plotinus refused remedies he thought unfitting; when the plague killed the men who massaged him, a disease of the throat grew on him, with hoarseness, failing sight and sores, and he left Rome for Campania. He died there at sixty-six, with only Eustochius present, saying that he was trying to bring the divine in us back to the divine in the All. He never told his birthday, but kept Plato's and Socrates'.

@p
He often suffered from a disease of the bowels, but he would not put up with an enema, saying that it was not fitting for an old man to undergo such treatments; nor would he take the antidotes made from wild beasts, saying that he did not accept food from the bodies even of tame animals. He kept away from the baths, and had himself rubbed down every day at home; but when the plague grew heavy and the men who rubbed him died, he gave up this treatment, and little by little the savage form of the throat disease took hold of him. While I was with him nothing of this kind showed itself yet; but after I had sailed away the illness grew so fierce, as our companion Eustochius, who stayed with him until his death, told me when I came back, that his voice lost its clearness and its ring as he grew hoarse, his sight was clouded, and his hands and feet were covered with sores. So, since his friends avoided meeting him, because he had the habit of greeting everyone mouth to mouth, he left the city and went to Campania, and stayed at the estate of Zethus, an old companion of his who had died. What he needed was provided from Zethus' estate, and brought from Minturnae, from Castricius' lands; for Castricius had his property at Minturnae.
!flag in a short while he contracted malign diphtheria | The Greek says "little by little" (κατ’ ὀλίγον) the disease grew on him, not "in a short while"; and its name is κυνάγχη ("dog-choke"), the ancient name for a severe inflammation of the throat. "Malign diphtheria" is a modern diagnosis put into Porphyry's mouth.
!flag addressing everyone by word of mouth | The Greek ἀπὸ στόματος πάντας προσαγορεύειν is most naturally "greeting everyone mouth to mouth", with a kiss, which is why his friends, afraid of his sores, kept away; "by word of mouth" makes it speech.
!note The "antidotes from wild beasts" (θηριακαὶ ἀντίδοτοι) are theriacs, compound remedies against poison made with the flesh of wild animals, above all of vipers. Porphyry himself wrote a book On abstinence from animal food.

@p
@greek μέλλων δὲ τελευτᾶν
When he was about to die, as Eustochius told us, since Eustochius, who lived at Puteoli, was slow in coming to him, he said: "I am still waiting for you." And saying that he was trying to bring the divine in us back up to the divine in the All, as a snake passed under the bed on which he lay and slipped into a hole in the wall, he breathed his last. He was, as Eustochius said, sixty-six years old, and the second year of Claudius' reign was coming to its end. When he died I, Porphyry, happened to be staying at Lilybaeum, Amelius at Apamea in Syria, and Castricius in Rome; only Eustochius was there.
!note The last words are read differently in the manuscripts and by modern editors: some read them as Plotinus' charge to those present ("try to bring back..."), some read "the god in us" for "the divine in us". The modern version keeps Volkmann's text: "saying that he was trying" (φήσας πειρᾶσθαι).
!note The second year of the emperor Claudius (Claudius II) is AD 270, in the modern reckoning.

@p
@greek ἀναψηφίζουσι δὲ ἡμῖν
Counting back sixty-six years from the second year of Claudius' reign, the time of his birth falls in the thirteenth year of the reign of Severus. But he did not tell anyone the month in which he was born, nor his birthday, since he did not think it right to sacrifice or give a feast on his own birthday; and yet on the traditional birthdays of Plato and Socrates he sacrificed and gave his companions a feast, when those of the companions who were able also had to read out a discourse before those who had come together.
!note The thirteenth year of the emperor Septimius Severus is AD 205, in the modern reckoning. The birthdays of Socrates and Plato were kept on the sixth and seventh of the Athenian month Thargelion (Plutarch, Table Talk VIII.1).

@ch Life.3 | Ammonius, the Persian campaign, and Rome
What Plotinus told of himself: as a boy of eight he still went to his nurse for milk until he was shamed out of it. At twenty-eight he turned to philosophy, found the teachers of Alexandria wanting until he heard Ammonius, and stayed with him eleven years; he joined Gordian's campaign to learn the wisdom of Persia and India, escaped to Antioch, and at forty came to Rome. He kept his promise not to publish Ammonius' teaching, taught without writing for ten years, and Amelius joined him.

@p
These things, however, he often told us about himself in conversation. He kept going to his nurse, though he was already going to school to a teacher of letters, until he was eight years old, and baring her breasts was eager to suck; but once, hearing someone say that he was a wretched child, he was ashamed and stopped.
!flag he was told he was a " perverted imp," | The Greek ἀτηρὸν παιδίον is "a naughty (or wretched) child", a nurse's scolding; "perverted" is MacKenna's.

@p
@greek εἰκοστὸν δὲ καὶ ὄγδοον
In his twenty-eighth year he set out for philosophy, and was introduced to the teachers who had a name at Alexandria at that time; and he came away from their lectures downcast and full of sorrow, so that he told one of his friends what he was going through. The friend, understanding what his soul wanted, took him to Ammonius, whom he had not yet tried. He went in, heard him, and said to his companion: "This is the man I was looking for." And from that day he stayed with Ammonius continually, and gained such a mastery in philosophy that he was eager to get experience as well of the philosophy practised among the Persians and of the philosophy that flourishes among the Indians.
!flag At twenty he was caught by the passion for philosophy | The Greek says "in his twenty-eighth year" (εἰκοστὸν δὲ καὶ ὄγδοον ἔτος): MacKenna drops the eight. The count matters, since Porphyry then has him study eleven years with Ammonius and join Gordian's army at thirty-nine.
!note Ammonius (called Saccas by later writers) left no writings; what he taught is known only through his pupils, of whom Plotinus is the greatest.

@p
@greek Γορδιανοῦ δὲ τοῦ βασιλέως
When the emperor Gordian was about to march against the Persians, he gave himself to the army and went in with it, being already in his thirty-ninth year; for he had stayed studying with Ammonius for eleven whole years. When Gordian was killed in Mesopotamia, he escaped with difficulty and came through safely to Antioch. And when Philip had taken power, being forty years old, he went up to Rome.
!note Gordian III marched against the Persians in 242 and died in Mesopotamia in 244; Philip the Arab followed him (modern dates).

@p
@greek Ἐρεννίῳ δὲ καὶ Ὠριγένει
Erennius, Origen and Plotinus had made an agreement to reveal nothing of the teachings of Ammonius, which he had made clear to them in his lectures; and Plotinus kept to it: he met with some of those who came to him, but kept Ammonius' teachings undisclosed. Erennius was the first to break the agreement, and Origen followed Erennius, who had gone first. But Origen wrote nothing except the treatise On the daemons and, under Gallienus, That the king is the only maker. Plotinus went on for a long time writing nothing, but made his lectures from his time with Ammonius; and so he went on for ten whole years, meeting with some people but writing nothing. His lectures, since he urged those who were with him to ask questions themselves, were full of disorder and much idle talk, as Amelius told us.
!note This Origen is the Platonist, Plotinus' fellow pupil under Ammonius; whether he is the same man as Origen the Christian writer, who also heard Ammonius, is a question modern scholars still argue.
!note ποιητής is both "maker" and "poet": the title can mean that the king of all is the only maker of the world.

@p
@greek προσῆλθε δὲ αὐτῷ ὁ Ἀμέλιος
Amelius came to him when he was in his third year in Rome, in the third year of Philip's reign, and stayed until the first year of Claudius' reign, so that he was with him twenty-four whole years. When he came he already had a training from his time with Lysimachus, and in hard work he surpassed all those of his day, since he had copied and collected almost all the works of Numenius and learned most of them by heart. Making notes from the meetings, he put together about a hundred books of notes, which he has given to Ustillianus Hesychius of Apamea, whom he adopted as his son.
!flag presented to Hostilianus of Apamea | The Greek names him Ustillianus Hesychius (Οὐστιλλιανῷ Ἡσυχίῳ) of Apamea; MacKenna gives one name, in another form.
!note Numenius of Apamea was a Platonist of the second century, known now only from fragments; in chapter 17 Porphyry answers those who said that Plotinus took his ideas from him.

@ch Life.4 | Porphyry arrives; the first twenty-one treatises
Porphyry came to Rome from Greece in the tenth year of Gallienus, aged thirty, and found Amelius in his eighteenth year with Plotinus, who was fifty-nine. Plotinus had begun writing in the first year of Gallienus, and had written twenty-one treatises, given out only to a few after careful judgement. They had no titles of their own; Porphyry lists them with the titles that won out and their first words.

@p
In the tenth year of Gallienus' reign I, Porphyry, coming from Greece with Antonius of Rhodes, found Amelius in his eighteenth year with Plotinus, though he had not yet dared to write anything except his notes, which had not yet come to a hundred in number. In the tenth year of Gallienus' reign Plotinus was about fifty-nine years old. And I, Porphyry, was thirty years old myself when I first met him.
!note The tenth year of Gallienus is AD 262-263, in the modern reckoning.

@p
@greek ἀπὸ μέντοι τοῦ πρώτου ἔτους
From the first year of Gallienus' reign, however, Plotinus had been urged to write on the subjects that came up, and by the tenth year of Gallienus' reign, when I, Porphyry, first came to know him, he is found to have written twenty-one books, which I also got hold of, though they had been given out to few. For the giving out was not yet easy, nor done lightly, nor simply and readily, but with every care in judging those who received them.
!flag the distribution was grudging and secret | The Greek says only that the books were not given out easily or lightly, "but with every care in judging those who received them"; "secret" is MacKenna's.

@p
@greek ἦν δὲ καὶ τὰ γεγραμμένα ταῦτα
These were the writings; and since he did not give them titles himself, each person gave each a title of his own. The titles that won out are these; and I shall set down the first words of the books as well, so that each of the books named can easily be recognised from its first words:
[1, I.6] On beauty. It begins: "Beauty is mostly in sight."
[2, IV.7] On the immortality of the soul. It begins: "Whether each of us is immortal."
[3, III.1] On destiny. It begins: "All the things that come to be."
[4, IV.2] On the being of the soul. It begins: "The being of the soul."
[5, V.9] On intellect, the forms, and what is. It begins: "All human beings, from the time they come to be."
[6, IV.8] On the descent of the soul into bodies. It begins: "Often, waking up."
[7, V.4] How what comes after the first comes from the first; and on the One. It begins: "If there is anything after the first."
[8, IV.9] Whether all souls are one. It begins: "Is it so, that just as the soul."
[9, VI.9] On the Good, or the One. It begins: "All the things that are."
[10, V.1] On the three first realities. It begins: "What is it, then, that has made the souls."
[11, V.2] On the coming to be and the order of what comes after the first. It begins: "The One is all things."
[12, II.4] On the two kinds of matter. It begins: "What is called matter."
[13, III.9] Various considerations. It begins: "Intellect, he says, sees the forms that are in."
[14, II.2] On the circular movement. It begins: "Why does it move in a circle."
[15, III.4] On the daemon allotted to us. It begins: "Of some, the realities."
[16, I.9] On going out of life reasonably. It begins: "You shall not lead it out, lest it go out."
[17, II.6] On quality. It begins: "Are being and substance."
[18, V.7] Whether there are forms of individuals too. It begins: "Whether there is a form of the individual too."
[19, I.2] On the virtues. It begins: "Since evils are here."
[20, I.3] On dialectic. It begins: "What art, or method."
[21, IV.1] How the soul is said to be between the undivided and the divided being. It begins: "In the intelligible world."
!note The numbers and Ennead places in square brackets are the Workshop's, not Porphyry's: the place of each book in his count by date of writing, and where he set it in his edition (the Workshop numbers the Enneads as First1KGreek's text does, in which IV.1 is the short treatise that begins "In the intelligible world"). MacKenna leaves out the first words, and adds his own Ennead places; he gives IV.1 for both the fourth book and the twenty-first.
!flag On our Tutelary Spirit (II. 2) | The fifteenth book, "On the daemon allotted to us", is III.4 in the Enneads, not II.2, which is the fourteenth, "On the circular movement". The Ennead places are MacKenna's additions.
!note The title of the fifteenth book recalls Plato's Phaedo (107d): the daemon "allotted" to each in life leads the soul after death. The sixteenth echoes the Phaedo too (61c-62c): we must not take ourselves out of life.

@p
@greek ταῦτα μὲν οὖν εἴκοσι καὶ ἓν ὄντα
These, then, twenty-one in all, are found to have been written when I, Porphyry, first came to him; Plotinus was then in his fifty-ninth year.

@ch Life.5 | Six years with Plotinus: twenty-four more
In the six years Porphyry was with him, questions raised at the meetings, and the urging of Amelius and Porphyry, led Plotinus to write twenty-four treatises, among them the great works on being everywhere whole, on the soul, on intelligible beauty, against the Gnostics, and on the kinds of being. With the first twenty-one, they make forty-five.

@p
I was with him this year and five years after it (for I, Porphyry, had come to Rome a little before the tenth year, while Plotinus was keeping the summer months free of lectures, though meeting people otherwise in conversation). In these six years, as many questions were examined in the meetings, and Amelius and I asked him to write, he wrote two books, [22-23, VI.4-5] That being, one and the same, is everywhere whole. The first of these has the beginning: "Is it the soul that is everywhere"; the second's beginning is: "The one and the same everywhere." Next he wrote two more: one [24, V.6], That what is beyond being does not think, and what is the first thinker and what the second, which begins: "One thing thinks something other"; the other [25, II.5], On the potential and the actual, which begins: "Something is said to be potential."
!flag Unity (II. 4, 5) | The two books "That being, one and the same, is everywhere whole" are VI.4 and VI.5 of the Enneads, not II.4 and II.5 (which are "On matter" and "On the potential and the actual").

@p
@greek περὶ τῆς τῶν ἀσωμάτων ἀπαθείας
[26, III.6] On the impassibility of the bodiless. It begins: "Saying that perceptions are not affections."
[27, IV.3] On the soul, first. It begins: "About the soul, all the questions we must raise."
[28, IV.4] On the soul, second. It begins: "What, then, will it say."
[29, IV.5] On the soul, third; or, How we see. It begins: "Since we put off."
[30, III.8] On contemplation. It begins: "Playing, then, at first."
[31, V.8] On intelligible beauty. It begins: "Since we say."
[32, V.5] That the intelligibles are not outside intellect, and on the Good. It begins: "Intellect, the true intellect."
[33, II.9] Against the Gnostics. It begins: "Since, then, it has appeared to us."
[34, VI.6] On numbers. It begins: "Is multiplicity."
[35, II.8] How things seen far off look small. It begins: "Is it that things seen far off."
[36, I.5] Whether being happy lies in length of time. It begins: "Being happy."
[37, II.7] On complete blending. It begins: "About what is called."
[38, VI.7] How the many forms came to be, and on the Good. It begins: "Sending into coming to be, the god."
[39, VI.8] On what is willed. It begins: "Is it, even in the case of the gods."
[40, II.1] On the cosmos. It begins: "Saying that the cosmos always."
[41, IV.6] On perception and memory. It begins: "Saying that perceptions are not imprints."
[42, VI.1] On the kinds of being, first. It begins: "About the things that are, how many and which."
[43, VI.2] On the kinds of being, second. It begins: "Since, about the so-called."
[44, VI.3] On the kinds of being, third. It begins: "About substance, how it seems."
[45, III.7] On eternity and time. It begins: "Eternity and time."
!flag After these come the following twenty | Porphyry simply goes on with the list; the heading, and the count of twenty, are MacKenna's.
!flag On the Impassibility of the Bodiless (III. 5) | This book is III.6 of the Enneads; III.5 is "On love", the fiftieth.

@p
@greek ταῦτα τὰ εἴκοσι καὶ τέτταρα ὄντα
These twenty-four, which he wrote in the six-year period of my, Porphyry's, presence, taking their subjects from problems that came up at the time, as we have shown from the headings of each of the books, together with the twenty-one from before our stay, make forty-five in all.

@ch Life.6 | The last nine, and the strength of the books
While Porphyry was in Sicily, Plotinus sent him five more treatises, and, shortly before his death, four: fifty-four in all. Their strength follows the time of writing: the first twenty-one are slighter, the middle twenty-four the most complete, the last nine weaker, and the last four weaker still.

@p
While I was living in Sicily (for I had withdrawn there about the fifteenth year of Gallienus' reign), Plotinus wrote five books and sent them to me:
[46, I.4] On happiness. It begins: "Living well and being happy."
[47, III.2] On providence, first. It begins: "To chance."
[48, III.3] On providence, second. It begins: "What, then, do we think about these things."
[49, V.3] On the knowing realities and what is beyond. It begins: "Must what thinks itself be manifold."
[50, III.5] On love. It begins: "About love, whether it is a god."

@p
@greek ταῦτα μὲν οὖν τῷ πρώτῳ ἔτει
These, then, he sent in the first year of Claudius' reign; at the beginning of the second, when he died soon after, he sent these:
[51, I.8] What evils are. It begins: "Those who ask where evils come from."
[52, II.3] Whether the stars act. It begins: "The movement of the stars."
[53, I.1] What is the living being, and what is the human being. It begins: "Pleasures and pains."
[54, I.7] On the first good, or on happiness. It begins: "Could anyone say that another."
!flag On the First Good ; or, On Happiness (I. 8) | The last book, "On the first good", is I.7 of the Enneads; I.8 is "What evils are", the fifty-first.

@p
@greek ταῦτα μετὰ τῶν τετταράκοντα πέντε
These, with the forty-five of the first and second writing, make fifty-four. And as they were written, some in his earlier age, some in his prime, and some when his body was worn out by illness, so the books have their strength. The first twenty-one are of slighter power, not yet having enough greatness for full vigour; those that came out in the middle period show his power at its height, and the twenty-four, except for the short ones, are the most complete; but the last nine were written when his power was already failing, and the last four more so than the five before them.
!flag early manhood, vigorous prime, worn-out constitution | The Greek κατὰ πρώτην ἡλικίαν is "in his earlier age": by Porphyry's own count Plotinus was about forty-eight to fifty-nine when he wrote the first twenty-one, not in "early manhood".
!note Modern readers have doubted this judgement, which ranks the books by Porphyry's own years with Plotinus: the first twenty-one include On beauty (I.6) and On the Good, or the One (VI.9).

@ch Life.7 | The circle: pupils, senators, friends
Plotinus had many hearers and some close followers: Amelius, who wanted to be called Amerius; the doctors Paulinus, Eustochius and Zethus; the critic and poet Zoticus; Castricius Firmus; senators, among them Rogatianus, who gave up his property and office and was cured of his gout, Plotinus' model for philosophers; Serapion, who could not give up money; and Porphyry, whom he asked to correct his writings.

@p
He had many hearers, and as followers who were with him for the sake of philosophy, Amelius from Etruria, whose proper name was Gentilianus, though he himself wanted to be called Amerius, with an r, saying that it suited him to be named from amereia, being without parts, rather than from ameleia, carelessness. He had also a doctor, Paulinus of Scythopolis, whom Amelius used to call Mikkalos, "little one", a man full of things he had misheard. And he had as a companion a doctor from Alexandria, Eustochius, who came to know him towards the end of his life and stayed, caring for him, until his death; giving his time to Plotinus' teachings alone, he took on the character of a true philosopher.
!flag from Amereia, Unification, | ἀμέρεια is "being without parts", indivisibility, which the intelligible has; MacKenna's "Unification" is near the sense, but not the word.

@p
@greek συνῆν δὲ καὶ Ζωτικὸς
Zoticus, too, was with him, a critic and a poet, who made corrected editions of Antimachus and turned the story of Atlantis into a poem, very poetically; his sight failed him, and he died a little before Plotinus' death. Paulinus also died before Plotinus. He had also a companion Zethus, an Arab by birth, who married the daughter of Theodosius, a companion of Ammonius. He too was a doctor, and Plotinus loved him dearly; and since he was a man of civic affairs, with leanings towards civic life, Plotinus tried to hold him back from it. He treated him as one of his own, so that he would even withdraw to his country place six miles from Minturnae, the fields that Castricius, called Firmus, had owned. Castricius was of all the men of our time the greatest lover of beautiful things; he revered Plotinus, served Amelius in everything like a good servant, and was devoted to me, Porphyry, in everything as to a true brother. He too, then, revered Plotinus, though he had chosen the civic life.
!flag the political career in which he stood high | The Greek says only that Zethus was a man of politics, "with political leanings" (ῥοπὰς ἔχοντα πολιτικάς), which Plotinus tried to check; that he "stood high" in it is MacKenna's.
!note Antimachus of Colophon was an epic poet of about 400 BC; the story of Atlantis is told by Plato in the Timaeus (20d-25d) and Critias.

@p
@greek ἠκροῶντο δὲ αὐτοῦ
Not a few members of the Senate also attended his lectures, of whom Marcellus Orrontius and Sabinillus worked hardest at philosophy. There was also Rogatianus, of the Senate, who had gone so far in turning away from this life that he gave up all his property, sent away all his servants, and gave up his rank as well; when he was about to go out as praetor, with the attendants waiting, he would neither go out nor take any care of the office. He would not even choose a house of his own to live in, but went round to some of his friends and acquaintances, dining and sleeping there, and eating only every other day. And from this renunciation, and this carelessness about the needs of life, though he had had gout so badly that he was carried in a chair, he recovered his strength; and though he had not been able to stretch out his hands, he came to use them much more easily than those who live by the crafts of their hands. Plotinus welcomed him, and kept praising him above all, holding him up as a good pattern to those who do philosophy.
!flag only one meal a day | The Greek σιτεῖσθαι παρὰ μίαν is "to eat every other day", skipping a day between meals; MacKenna's "one meal a day" is a milder fast.

@p
@greek συνῆν δὲ καὶ Σεραπίων
There was also Serapion of Alexandria, at first a rhetorician, and later given to philosophical discussions as well, but he could not give up his weakness for money and money-lending. He had me too, Porphyry of Tyre, among his closest companions, and he asked me to correct his writings.

@ch Life.8 | How he wrote
Plotinus never reread what he had written, since his sight was poor, and he cared nothing for handwriting or spelling, only for the thought. He worked out a whole argument in his head and then wrote it down as if copying from a book; he could talk with someone and keep his train of thought, and go straight on afterwards. He was always attentive to himself, and slept little.

@p
For once he had written something, he could never bear to take it up a second time, nor even to read it through once, because his sight did not serve him for reading. He wrote without forming the letters beautifully, without dividing the syllables clearly, and without caring for spelling: he held only to the sense [and the matter]; and he kept on doing the thing we all marvelled at to the end.
!flag in these habits, to our general surprise, he remained unchanged to the very end | What "we all marvelled at" (ὃ πάντες ἐθαυμάζομεν) is what follows, "for he would complete the argument in himself...": his writing straight from a finished thought, not his careless habits.

@p
@greek συντελέσας γὰρ παρ’ ἑαυτῷ
For he would complete the inquiry within himself from beginning to end, and then, putting what he had thought into writing, he would write out what he had set in order in his soul so continuously that he seemed to be copying from a book. Even when he was talking with someone and keeping the conversation going, he was at his own inquiry, so that he could meet what the conversation needed and at the same time keep his thought on what he was examining unbroken. When the person he was talking with had gone, he did not go over what he had written, because, as we have said, his sight was not enough for rereading; he would join on what came next, as if he had left no time between, while he was in conversation.
!flag by someone entering on business | The Greek says only "even when talking with someone and keeping the conversation going"; the visitor "entering on business" is MacKenna's.

@p
@greek συνῆν οὖν καὶ ἑαυτῷ ἅμα
So he was present at once to himself and to others, and he would never relax his attention to himself, except in sleep; and sleep was kept away by the smallness of his food (often he would not even touch bread) and by his continual turning towards intellect.

@ch Life.9 | Women, orphans, and a gentle man
Gemina, her daughter and Amphiclea were devoted to him and to philosophy. Dying parents left their children and property in his care, so his house was full of boys and girls; he checked their accounts, saying their property must be kept safe until they took up philosophy, yet never relaxed his mind's tension towards intellect. In twenty-six years in Rome he settled many disputes and made no enemy.

@p
He also had women who were greatly devoted to him: Gemina, in whose house he lived, and her daughter, also Gemina, named like her mother, and Amphiclea, who had become the wife of Ariston, the son of Iamblichus; they were greatly devoted to philosophy.
!note This Iamblichus is not the philosopher, who was younger; who he was is not known.

@p
@greek πολλοὶ δὲ καὶ ἄνδρες
Many men and women of the noblest families, when they were about to die, brought him their children, boys and girls alike, and entrusted them to him with the rest of their property, as to a holy and divine guardian. So his house was full of boys and girls. Among them was Polemon, whose education he cared for, and he would often listen to him even when he was composing verses. He also put up with going over the accounts brought to him by those in charge of the children's affairs, and saw to it that they were exact, saying that, until the children took up philosophy, they must keep their possessions and incomes untouched and safe. And yet, though he provided for so many people the cares and attentions of life, he never relaxed, while he was awake, his tension towards intellect.
!flag his intention towards the Supreme | The Greek is τὴν πρὸς τὸν νοῦν τάσιν, his "tension towards intellect"; MacKenna's "the Supreme" sets it higher, at the One.

@p
@greek ἦν δὲ καὶ πρᾷος
He was gentle, too, and available to all who had any acquaintance at all with him. So, though he spent twenty-six whole years in Rome and settled many people's disputes with one another, he never made an enemy of any of the men in civic life.
!note Twenty-six years: from 244, when he came to Rome at forty, to 270 (modern dates).

@ch Life.10 | Olympius' magic, the god-daemon, and the gods
Olympius, a jealous rival, tried to harm Plotinus by magic, but the attack turned back on him. An Egyptian priest called up Plotinus' guardian daemon in the temple of Isis, and a god appeared, not a daemon; hence Plotinus' book On the daemon allotted to us. Asked to join the sacrifices, he said: "They must come to me, not I to them."

@p
Among those who claimed to do philosophy, Olympius of Alexandria, who had been for a short time a pupil of Ammonius, behaved with contempt towards him from love of being first; and he went so far in attacking him that he even tried to bring a star-stroke on him by magic. But when he found the attempt turning back on himself, he said to his friends that the power of Plotinus' soul was great, so great that it could throw back the attacks made on it onto those trying to harm him. Plotinus, though, felt Olympius' attempt, and said that at that moment his body was being pulled tight like a drawstring purse, his limbs being squeezed against one another. And Olympius, after often being in danger of suffering something himself rather than doing anything to Plotinus, stopped.

@p
@greek ἦν γὰρ καὶ κατὰ γένεσιν
For Plotinus had something more than others even from birth. An Egyptian priest who had come up to Rome, and had been introduced to him through a friend, wishing to give a proof of his own wisdom, asked Plotinus to come and see the daemon who was with him, his own daemon, as it is called, made visible. Plotinus readily agreed, and the calling up was made in the temple of Isis: that was the only pure place, they say, that the Egyptian could find in Rome. When the daemon was called to show itself, a god came, and not one of the race of daemons; so the Egyptian said: "Blessed are you, for you have a god as your daemon, and the one who is with you is not of the lower race." But they could neither ask it anything nor see it present any longer, because the friend who was watching with them strangled the birds he was holding as a safeguard, whether from envy or from some fear. So, having one of the more divine daemons as his companion, he himself kept raising his own divine eye towards it. It is for this reason that he wrote a book, too, On the daemon allotted to us, where he tries to give the causes of the differences between the companions of each.
!note The book is III.4. Plato's Timaeus (90a) calls the highest part of the soul the daemon God has given each of us; in the Republic's myth of Er (617e, 620d-e) each soul chooses a life and is given a daemon as its guardian.

@p
@greek φιλοθύτου δὲ γεγονότος
When Amelius had become fond of sacrifices, and went round the temples at the new moon and at the feasts, and once asked to take Plotinus with him, Plotinus said: "They must come to me, not I to them." From what thought he spoke so loftily, we could neither understand by ourselves nor did we dare to ask him.

@ch Life.11 | Reading character; Porphyry saved
Plotinus picked out the slave who had stolen a widow's necklace, and foretold how the children in his care would turn out. When Porphyry thought of taking his own life, Plotinus came to him, told him it came from melancholy, not from intellect, and sent him away to Sicily, where Porphyry gave up the thought, but was not with Plotinus when he died.

@p
He had so great an abundance of insight into character that once, when a costly necklace was stolen from Chione, who lived with him with her children, passing her widowhood with dignity, and the servants were gathered before Plotinus' eyes, he looked at them all and said, "This is the one who stole it," pointing out one of them. The man was whipped, and for a long time at first denied it, but later confessed, brought back what he had stolen and gave it back.

@p
@greek προεῖπε δ’ ἂν καὶ
He would also foretell, of each of the children who were with him, how he would turn out; so of Polemon, what he would be like: that he would be a lover, given to love, and short-lived, as indeed happened.

@p
@greek καί ποτε ἐμοῦ Πορφυρίου
And once he noticed that I, Porphyry, was thinking of taking myself out of life; and he came to me suddenly while I was staying in the house, said that this desire did not come from a state of intellect, but from some melancholic illness, and told me to go away on a journey. I obeyed him and went to Sicily, since I heard that a man of note, Probus, was living near Lilybaeum; and I both gave up that desire and was kept from being with Plotinus until his death.
!flag to my house where I had secluded myself | The Greek says only that Plotinus came to Porphyry "while I was staying in the house" (ἐν τῷ οἴκῳ διατρίβοντι); that he had shut himself away is MacKenna's.
!note Plotinus' own book On going out of life (I.9, the sixteenth he wrote) teaches that the soul must not be led out of the body by force.

@ch Life.12 | Platonopolis
The emperor Gallienus and his wife Salonina honoured Plotinus, and he asked them to rebuild a ruined city of philosophers in Campania, to be called Platonopolis and governed by Plato's laws, where he would retire with his companions. Envy at court stopped it.

@p
The emperor Gallienus and his wife Salonina honoured and revered Plotinus greatly. Making use of their friendship, he asked them to raise up again a city of philosophers that was said to have existed in Campania, but was otherwise in ruins, and to give the country round it to the city once it was settled; those who would live there were to use Plato's laws, and it was to be given the name Platonopolis; and he promised that he would withdraw there himself with his companions. And the philosopher's wish would have come about very easily, had not some of those around the emperor stopped it, from envy, or resentment, or some other wicked cause.
!note Plato's Laws describe the founding of a new city in Crete, Magnesia; the Republic (473c-d) holds that cities will have no rest from evils until philosophers rule.

@ch Life.13 | How he taught
In the meetings Plotinus spoke well and found the point quickly, though he mispronounced some words; when he spoke, his intellect lit up his face. He would answer questions for days: when a visitor wanted a set lecture instead of Porphyry's questions, he said that without solving the questions there would be nothing to write.

@p
In the meetings he was able to put things into words, and most powerful at finding and thinking out what fitted the matter; but he made mistakes in some words: he would not say anamimnēsketai ("he recollects") but anamnēmisketai, and other wrongly stamped words, which he kept to in his writing too. When he spoke, the showing of his intellect lit up his face with its light: he was lovely to look at, but then most beautiful to see; a fine sweat ran down him, gentleness shone through him, and he showed kindness towards questions, and vigour.
!flag a slight moisture gathered on his forehead | The Greek says only that "a fine sweat ran down" him (λεπτός τις ἱδρὼς ἐπέθει); the forehead is MacKenna's.
!flag He was always as ready to entertain objections as he was powerful in meeting them | The Greek lists what showed in his face as he spoke: his gentleness, his kindness towards questions (τὸ προσηνὲς πρὸς τὰς ἐρωτήσεις) and his vigour (τὸ εὔτονον). MacKenna turns this into a sentence of his own about objections and his power in meeting them.
!note The two words: ἀναμιμνήσκεται is the right form; Plotinus said ἀναμνημίσκεται, swapping the syllables. Porphyry's point is that the master of thought was no master of grammar.

@p
@greek τριῶν γοῦν ἡμερῶν
For three days, for example, when I, Porphyry, asked him how the soul is present with the body, he kept on demonstrating, so that when a man named Thaumasius came in, who dealt in general discourses and said that he wanted to hear him speak in a way fit for books, and could not bear Porphyry answering and asking, Plotinus said: "But if we do not solve the difficulties when Porphyry asks, we shall not be able to say anything at all for the book."
!flag a man called Thomasius | The Greek name is Θαυμάσιος, Thaumasius.
!note Volkmann reads ἀποκρινομένου, "answering"; the modern version keeps it. The question how the soul is present with the body is the subject of Plotinus' IV.3, the first of the books On difficulties about the soul (done beside Taylor).

@ch Life.14 | How he wrote, and what was read in his school
His writing was brief and full of thought; the Stoics and Aristotle's Metaphysics are hidden in it; he knew the mathematical sciences. In his school commentaries on Plato and Aristotle were read, but he spoke from his own thought, in Ammonius' spirit. He called Longinus a scholar, not a philosopher, and blushed when Origen came to hear him.

@p
In writing he was concise and full of thought, brief, and richer in ideas than in words, mostly speaking with inspiration and passion. Mixed into his writings, though hidden, are the doctrines of the Stoics and the Peripatetics; and Aristotle's Metaphysics too is packed into them. No theorem, as they call it, of geometry escaped him, nor of arithmetic, nor of mechanics, optics or musical theory; but he was not set on working these out himself.
!flag He followed his own path rather than that of tradition | There is nothing for this in Volkmann's Greek, which goes straight from "speaking with inspiration and passion" to the Stoic and Peripatetic doctrines.
!flag is condensed in them, all but entire | The Greek says only that the Metaphysics "is packed into" his writings too (καταπεπύκνωται); "all but entire" is MacKenna's.
!flag Geometry, Mechanics, Optics and Music | MacKenna leaves out arithmetic (ἀριθμητικόν), which the Greek names after geometry.
!note "The Peripatetics" are Aristotle's school. Plotinus' VI.1 to VI.3, On the kinds of being, examine Aristotle's categories and the Stoics' at length.

@p
@greek ἐν δὲ ταῖς συνουσίαις
In the meetings the commentaries were read out to him: they might be by Severus, or Cronius, or Numenius, or Gaius, or Atticus, and among the Peripatetics by Aspasius, Alexander, Adrastus and any that came to hand. But nothing was simply said from them: he was original and different in his study, and brought the intellect of Ammonius to his inquiries. He took the matter in quickly, and after giving the sense of a deep theorem in a few words, he would rise. When Longinus' On principles and his Lover of antiquity were read to him, he said: "Longinus is a scholar, but not a philosopher at all."
!flag Longinus' work On Causes | The title is περὶ ἀρχῶν, On principles (or "On first principles"), not "On causes".
!flag a few words sufficed him to make clear the significance of some profound theory and so to pass on | The Greek ἀνίστατο is "he rose": having given the sense in a few words, he stood up and ended the meeting.
!note Severus, Cronius, Numenius, Gaius and Atticus are Platonists of the second century; Aspasius, Alexander (of Aphrodisias) and Adrastus are commentators on Aristotle. Longinus, the critic and scholar who taught Porphyry at Athens, appears again in chapters 19 to 21. "Lover of antiquity" (φιλάρχαιος) is the title of one of his books.

@p
@greek Ὠριγένους δὲ ἀπαντήσαντός
Once when Origen came to the meeting, Plotinus went red and wanted to rise; and when Origen asked him to speak, he said that one's eagerness flags when the speaker knows that he is speaking to people who already know what he is going to say; and so, after talking a little, he got up and left.
!note This Origen is the Platonist, Plotinus' fellow pupil under Ammonius (chapter 3).

@ch Life.15 | A poem, a bad speech, and the astrologers
When Porphyry read a mystical poem at Plato's feast, Plotinus called him poet, philosopher and hierophant. When a speaker defended Alcibiades' offer in the Symposium, Plotinus had Porphyry answer him. He read the Platonist Eubulus' writings through Porphyry, studied astrology closely, and refuted it.

@p
When at Plato's feast I read a poem, The sacred marriage, and someone said that Porphyry was mad, because much of it was said in the language of the mysteries, with inspiration and in a hidden way, Plotinus said so that all could hear: "You have shown yourself at once poet, philosopher and hierophant."
!note "Plato's feast" (Πλατώνεια) is the feast Plotinus gave on Plato's birthday (chapter 2). The hierophant is the priest who shows the sacred things at the Mysteries of Eleusis.

@p
@greek ὅτε δὲ ὁ ῥήτωρ Διοφάνης
When the orator Diophanes read a defence of Alcibiades in Plato's Symposium, laying it down that for the sake of learning virtue a pupil must give himself to a teacher who desires him, even for sexual union, Plotinus several times leapt up to leave the gathering, but held himself back; and after the audience broke up he told me, Porphyry, to write a reply. Since Diophanes would not give me the book, I recalled his arguments from memory, wrote the reply, and read it before the same hearers gathered again, and I pleased Plotinus so much that at the meetings he kept saying: "Shoot like that, and you may become a light to men."
!flag should submit to the teacher without reserve | The Greek says the pupil should give himself "to the teacher who desires him" (ἐρῶντι ... τῷ καθηγεμόνι); "without reserve" is MacKenna's, and he leaves out the teacher's desire.
!flag during the very reading he repeatedly quoted | The Greek says that Plotinus "kept saying it at the meetings" (κἀν ταῖς συνουσίαις συνεχῶς ἐπιλέγειν), afterwards and often, not during the reading.
!note In the Symposium (217a-219d) Alcibiades tells how he offered himself to Socrates in exchange for his wisdom, and was refused. Plotinus' line is Homer's Iliad 8.282, Agamemnon to the archer Teucer ("shoot like that, and you may become a light to the Danaans"), with "men" for "Danaans".

@p
@greek γράφοντος δὲ Εὐβούλου
When Eubulus, the Platonic successor, wrote from Athens and sent writings on some Platonic questions, Plotinus had them given to me, Porphyry, and asked me to examine them and report to him what was written. He paid attention to the tables of the stars, though not at all as a mathematician, but to the forecasts of the casters of nativities more closely; and having caught out how little their promise could be trusted, he did not hesitate to refute it in many places in his writings too.
!flag the principles of Astronomy | The Greek κανόνες are "tables" (the astronomers' tables of the stars' movements), used to cast nativities; it is these that Plotinus studied "not at all as a mathematician".
!note The "successor" (διάδοχος) is the head of the Platonic school at Athens, Plato's successor. Plotinus refutes the astrologers in II.3, Whether the stars are causes, and III.1, On destiny.

@ch Life.16 | The Gnostics
In his time there were sectarians, Christians, with revelations under the names of Zoroaster, Zostrianus and others, who said Plato had not reached the depth of intelligible being. Plotinus refuted them at the meetings and in the book Against the Gnostics; Amelius and Porphyry wrote against their revelations.

@p
In his time there were many others, Christians, and among them sectarians who had set out from the ancient philosophy, the followers of Adelphius and Aquilinus, who owned a great many writings of Alexander the Libyan, Philocomus and Demostratus the Lydian, and brought out revelations of Zoroaster, Zostrianus, Nicotheus, Allogenes, Mesus and others of that kind; they deceived many, and were deceived themselves, saying that Plato had not come near the depth of intelligible being.
!flag works by Alexander of Lydia, by Philocomus, by Demostratus and by Lydus | Volkmann's Greek reads "Alexander the Libyan" (τοῦ Λίβυος) and "Demostratus the Lydian" (τοῦ Λυδοῦ): MacKenna makes Alexander a Lydian, and the Lydian a fourth author, "Lydus".
!note The Greek can also be read "many Christians, and others": the sentence has been taken both ways. The sectarians (αἱρετικοί) are those whom Plotinus' book calls Gnostics.
!note A modern finding, not in Porphyry: books called Zostrianus and Allogenes survive in Coptic, among the manuscripts found at Nag Hammadi in Egypt in 1945.

@p
@greek ὅθεν αὐτὸς μὲν
So he himself made many refutations at the meetings, and also wrote the book which we have titled Against the Gnostics, and left the rest to us to judge. Amelius went on as far as forty books writing against the book of Zostrianus; and I, Porphyry, have made many refutations of the book of Zoroaster, showing that the book is spurious and recent, made up by those who founded the sect, so that it would be thought that the doctrines they had chosen to honour were those of the ancient Zoroaster.
!note Against the Gnostics is II.9 (the thirty-third book by date), done beside Taylor. Volkmann sets ὅπως, "so that", in round brackets.

@ch Life.17 | Numenius, and Amelius' letter
Some in Greece said Plotinus was passing off Numenius' work as his own. Amelius answered in a book, and dedicated it to Porphyry as "Basileus", the Greek for his Syrian name Malchus, "king". Porphyry gives the covering letter.

@p
Since people in Greece were saying that he was passing off Numenius' work as his own, and Trypho, the Stoic and Platonist, reported this to Amelius, Amelius wrote a book which he titled On the difference between the doctrines of Plotinus and Numenius, and dedicated it to me, as Basileus. Basileus was the name I, Porphyry, had: in my native tongue I was called Malchus, which was my father's name too, and Malchus means basileus ("king") if one wants to turn it into Greek. So Longinus, dedicating his On impulse to Cleodamus and to me, Porphyry, wrote at its head "Cleodamus and Malchus"; and Amelius, translating the name, as Numenius turned Maximus into Megalos, writes Basileus for Malchus:
!flag it is equivalent to Porphyry (Purple-robed) | Not in the Greek: Porphyry says only that Malchus, his native name, means "king". That "Porphyry" ("purple") also suits a king is MacKenna's addition.
!note Porphyry was from Tyre, in Phoenicia; malk is "king" in the Semitic languages spoken there. Maximus is Latin for "greatest", Megalos Greek for "great".

@p
@greek Ἀμέλιος Βασιλεῖ εὖ πράττειν
"Amelius to Basileus, greetings. For the sake of those highly praised men themselves who, as you say, have dinned into your ears that our companion's doctrines go back to Numenius of Apamea, I would never have let out a word, you may be sure. For plainly this too comes from the fine speech and fluency they pride themselves on: saying now that he is a great babbler, now that he is a plagiarist, and thirdly that what he passes off as his own is the poorest stuff there is, plainly to mock him when they speak against him.
!flag he bases the universe on the meanest of existents | The Greek ὑποβαλλόμενος is the same word as in "passing off" Numenius' work: the third charge is most naturally that what he passes off is the poorest of things. MacKenna reads it as "putting under", a foundation for the universe.

@p
@greek σοῦ δὲ τῇ προφάσει
"But since you think we should make use of this occasion, both to have what we hold more ready to hand for remembering, and to come to know more fully, for the sake of so great a companion as Plotinus, teachings that have long been noised abroad, I have done as you asked; and now I come giving you what I promised, worked out, as you know yourself, in three days. You must judge them fairly and kindly, as things neither set in order nor picked out by laying them beside those writings, but recalled from our reading long ago and set down here now in the order in which each first came to mind; all the more since the thought of the man whom some bring in as agreeing with us is not at all easy to grasp, because, as it may seem, he is carried now one way, now another about the same things.
!flag a writer who, like Plotinus, now arraigned for the opinion we also hold | A hard place. Who "the man" is the Greek does not say plainly: most likely Numenius, whose thought some say agrees with ours. MacKenna takes him to be Plotinus.

@p
@greek ὅτι δέ, εἴ τι τῶν
"That you will kindly set right anything from our own hearth that may have been put out falsely, I know well. I have been driven, it seems, as the tragedy says somewhere, busybody that I am, to set straight and to disown what departs from our leader's doctrines. Such, then, was my wish to please you in every way. Farewell."
!flag A busy man and far from the teachings of our master I must needs correct and recant | A hard place. The "distance" (διαστάσει) is most naturally the distance of the charge from Plotinus' doctrines, which Amelius sets straight and disowns; MacKenna makes Amelius himself far from the master's teachings.
!note The tragedy Amelius quotes is not identified here.

@ch Life.18 | Porphyry's recantation
Porphyry gave the letter to show that some thought Plotinus a babbler, because he taught like someone in conversation, without show. Porphyry himself at first wrote against him that the intelligibles are outside intellect; after three rounds with Amelius he changed his mind, and was trusted with Plotinus' books.

@p
I have been led to put in this letter not only to show that people at that time thought he was boasting of Numenius' doctrines as his own, but also that they thought him a great babbler and despised him, because they did not understand what he said, and because he was clean of all the stage-show and the puffing of the sophist: in the meetings he was like someone conversing, and did not quickly make plain to anyone the logical necessities in his argument.

@p
@greek ἔπαθον δ’ οὖν
I, Porphyry, felt the same when I first heard him. So I wrote a reply and brought it to him, trying to show that the intelligibles exist outside intellect. He had Amelius read it to him, and when it had been read he smiled and said: "It is for you, Amelius, to solve the difficulties he has fallen into through not knowing what we hold." Amelius wrote a book of no small size against Porphyry's difficulties; I wrote again in reply to what he had written; Amelius answered that too; and at the third round I, Porphyry, at last understood what was said, changed my mind, wrote a recantation and read it in the school. From then on I was trusted with Plotinus' books, and I stirred the teacher himself to the ambition of setting out his views in order and writing them at greater length. And I made Amelius, too, eager to write.
!flag From that time on I put faith in Plotinus' writings | The Greek ἐπιστεύθην with "the books" is "I was entrusted with Plotinus' books": the books were put in his care (as chapter 24 shows), not that he came to believe in them.
!note Plotinus' own treatise on the question is V.5, That the intelligibles are not outside intellect (the thirty-second by date).

@ch Life.19 | Longinus' letter
What Longinus thought of Plotinus: in a letter asking Porphyry to come from Sicily to Phoenicia with Plotinus' books, he says his copies are full of errors and asks for good ones; he does not accept most of Plotinus' hypotheses, but admires his writing above all.

@p
What opinion Longinus too held of Plotinus, mostly from what I told him in my letters, a part of a letter he wrote to me will show; it runs like this. Asking me to come down from Sicily to him in Phoenicia, and to bring Plotinus' books, he says:

@p
@greek καὶ σὺ μὲν ταῦτά
"Send these whenever you like; or rather, bring them; for I would never stop asking you to put the road to us before any other, if for no other reason (for what wisdom could you come expecting from us?), then for the sake of old friendship and the air, which is very mild for the bodily weakness you speak of. Whatever else you may happen to expect, expect nothing new from me, nor even those of my older writings that you say you have lost. For there is such a dearth of copyists here that, by the gods, all this time I have been getting together what was missing of Plotinus' books, and have only just managed it by taking my copyist away from his usual work and setting him to this alone.

@p
@greek καὶ κέκτημαι μὲν ὅσα
"I have, I think, all of them, with those you have now sent; but I have them only half complete, for they are full of mistakes, and not a few. I thought our companion Amelius would correct the copyists' slips; but he had more pressing things to do than such close work. So I do not know how to deal with them, though I am very eager to look into the books On the soul and On being, for these above all are corrupt. I would very much like the copies made with care to come to me from you, just to compare them and then send them back. But again I say the same: do not send them, but rather come yourself, bringing these and anything else that escaped Amelius. For everything he brought I got for myself with care: how could I not want to have the notes of a man worthy of all reverence and honour?
!note The books On the soul are IV.3 to IV.5, On difficulties about the soul; On being (περὶ τοῦ ὄντος) is most likely VI.4 and VI.5, That being, one and the same, is everywhere whole.

@p
@greek τοῦτο γὰρ οὖν καὶ παρόντι
"I have told you this before, surely, both when you were with me and when you were far away and staying at Tyre: that I do not much accept most of his hypotheses, but that I greatly admire and love the character of his writing, the density of his thoughts, and the philosophical ordering of his questions, and I would say that those who seek must count his books among the most highly esteemed."

@ch Life.20 | Longinus on the philosophers of his time
Longinus, the sharpest critic of the time, at first thought little of Plotinus. The preface of his On the end sorts the philosophers he had known into those who wrote and those who only taught, and says that only Plotinus and Amelius wrote with a method of their own and are worth reading.

@p
I have set this out at some length to show what the judgement on Plotinus was of the most critical man of our time, who examined the work of nearly all his contemporaries; though at first, from the ignorance of others, he was scornful of him. He thought that the copies he got from Amelius were faulty because he did not understand the man's usual way of putting things; for if any were corrected, Amelius' copies were, being taken from the originals.

@p
@greek ἔτι δὲ τοῦ Λογγίνου
Next, what Longinus wrote in a book about Plotinus, Amelius and the philosophers of his time must be set down, so that the judgement on them of a man so eminent and so searching may be complete. The book is titled: Longinus, Against Plotinus and Gentilianus Amelius, On the end. It has this preface:
!note The end (τέλος) is the goal of life, the good for whose sake everything else is done.

@p
@greek πολλῶν καθ’ ἡμᾶς
"There have been many philosophers in our time, Marcellus, and above all in the first years of our life; for the present time, words cannot say how scarce it has become in the matter. When we were still youths, not a few were leaders in philosophical discussion; I happened to see them all, because from childhood I travelled to many places with my parents, and I came to know those who lived on as I mixed with many peoples and cities. Some of them undertook to treat their views in writing, leaving to those who came after a share in their help; others thought it enough to lead those who were with them to grasp what they held.

@p
@greek ὧν τοῦ μὲν προτέρου
"Of the first kind were, among the Platonists, Euclides, Democritus and Proclinus, who lived in the Troad, and those who still teach in public in Rome, Plotinus and Gentilianus Amelius, his pupil; among the Stoics, Themistocles and Phoebion, and Annius and Medius, who flourished until lately; and among the Peripatetics, Heliodorus of Alexandria. Of the second kind were, among the Platonists, Ammonius and Origen, whom I attended for most of my time, men who far surpassed those of their time in understanding, and the successors at Athens, Theodotus and Eubulus. For even if one of them wrote something, as Origen the book On the daemons, and Eubulus the books on the Philebus and the Gorgias and on Aristotle's objections to Plato's Republic, that is not enough to count them among those who worked their teaching out: they took up writing as a by-work, and did not set out with the aim of writing.
!flag the Platonic Successors at Athens, Theodorus and Eubulus | The Greek name is Θεόδοτος, Theodotus.
!flag Origen wrote on Spirit-Beings | The title is περὶ δαιμόνων, On the daemons, the book named in chapter 3.
!flag some writing of a metaphysical order | "Of a metaphysical order" is MacKenna's: the Greek says only "if one of them wrote something".

@p
@greek τῶν δὲ Στωικῶν Ἑρμῖνος
"Of the Stoics, Herminus and Lysimachus, and Athenaeus and Musonius, who lived out their lives in the city; and of the Peripatetics, Ammonius and Ptolemy, the two most learned men of their time, Ammonius above all, for no one has come near him in breadth of learning; yet they wrote nothing systematic, only poems and display speeches, which were kept, I think, against those men's will; for they would not have wanted to become known later through such books, when they had passed over storing up their thought in more serious writings.
!flag the two living at Athens | The Greek καταβιώσαντες is "who lived out their lives" in the city: they were dead when Longinus wrote, not "living".
!flag merely verses and duty-speeches | The Greek λόγους ἐπιδεικτικούς are "display speeches", the orator's showpieces, not speeches of duty.

@p
@greek τῶν δ’ οὖν γραψάντων
"Of those who wrote, some did no more than collect and copy out what their elders had composed, as Euclides, Democritus and Proclinus; others, recording very small matters from the history of the ancients, tried to put together books on the same topics as they, like Annius, Medius and Phoebion, the last wanting to be known for the craft of his style rather than for the ordering of his thought; with these one might class Heliodorus too, since he adds nothing beyond what his elders said in their lectures to the articulation of the argument. But those who showed their seriousness about writing by the number of questions they took up, and used a way of inquiry of their own, are Plotinus and Gentilianus Amelius.

@p
@greek ὁ μὲν τὰς Πυθαγορείους
"Plotinus, it seems, set out the Pythagorean and Platonic principles more clearly than those before him; for the writings of Numenius, Cronius, Moderatus and Thrasyllus on the same subjects come nowhere near Plotinus' in exactness. Amelius chose to walk in his footsteps, and held to most of the same doctrines, but, lavish in working them out and in the wordiness of his style, he was drawn to a zeal opposite to his. These are the only ones whose writings we think worth examining. For why should anyone think it needful to stir up the rest, and leave off examining those from whom they took what they wrote? They added nothing of their own, not the main points, not even the arguments, and took no care either to gather what most hold or to judge what is better.
!flag Content with setting side by side the most generally adopted theories and marking off the better from the worse | The Greek says the opposite: they took no care "either to gather what most hold or to judge what is better" (οὐδ’ οὖν ἢ συναγωγῆς ... ἢ κρίσεως τοῦ βελτίονος ἐπιμεληθέντες).

@p
@greek ἤδη μὲν οὖν καὶ δι’ ἄλλων
"We have done this already in other writings too: as when we replied to Gentilianus on justice according to Plato, and examined Plotinus' book On the forms. For Basileus of Tyre, a companion common to us and to them, who himself wrote not a little in imitation of Plotinus, and who, preferring him to our own teaching, tried to show in a book that Plotinus held a better opinion on the forms than the one we hold, we seem to have refuted fairly in a reply, showing that his recantation was not right. In these we have stirred up not a few of these men's opinions, as also in the letter to Amelius, which has the size of a book and answers some of the things he sent us from Rome; he himself titled it a letter On the character of Plotinus' philosophy, but we were content with the common title of the writing, calling it To Amelius' letter."
!flag My own method has been different | The Greek says the opposite: "we have done this already in other writings too" (ἤδη μὲν οὖν καὶ δι’ ἄλλων τουτὶ πεποιήκαμεν), that is, examined these men's opinions.
!note Basileus of Tyre is Porphyry himself (chapter 17). His recantation is the one told in chapter 18.

@ch Life.21 | What Longinus' judgement shows
Porphyry draws out Longinus' verdict: Plotinus and Amelius surpassed everyone; Plotinus did not take his doctrines from Numenius; and Porphyry himself was praised for avoiding Amelius' wordiness. Had Longinus met him, he would not have written against what he had not yet grasped.

@p
In these words, then, he granted that Plotinus and Amelius surpassed all of their time by the number of questions they took up, and that they above all used a way of inquiry of their own; that, far from passing off Numenius' doctrines and championing his views, they chose to follow the doctrines of the Pythagoreans, and his own; and that the writings of Numenius, Cronius, Moderatus and Thrasyllus come nowhere near Plotinus' on the same subjects in exactness.
!flag followed the Pythagorean and Platonic schools | The Greek has "the doctrines of the Pythagoreans, and his own" (τὰ τῶν Πυθαγορείων αὐτοῦ τε): "Platonic" is MacKenna's, and he leaves out "his own".

@p
@greek εἰπὼν δὲ περὶ Ἀμελίου
And after saying of Amelius that he walked in Plotinus' footsteps, but, lavish in working things out and in the wordiness of his style, was drawn to a zeal opposite to his, he remembered me, Porphyry, who was then still at the start of my time with Plotinus, and said: "Basileus of Tyre, a companion common to us and to them, has himself composed not a little in imitation of Plotinus"; truly seeing that I wholly avoided the unphilosophical wordiness of Amelius and looked to Plotinus' manner as my model in writing. So it is enough that so great a man, first in judgement and held so until now, writes such things about Plotinus; and if I, Porphyry, had been able to meet with him when he called me, he would not even have written the reply that he undertook before he had grasped the doctrine exactly.

@ch Life.22 | Apollo's oracle on Plotinus' soul
@older 22 23
Why rely on men's judgement? When Amelius asked Apollo where Plotinus' soul had gone, the god who called Socrates wisest of all men answered in a long hymn: Plotinus, once a man, is now a daemon; the gods guided him in life, and he has joined Minos, Rhadamanthus, Aeacus, Plato and Pythagoras among the blessed.

@p
But why should I talk of these things "about oak or rock", as Hesiod says? If we are to use the testimony of the wise, who could be wiser than a god? And of the god who said truly: "I know the number of the sand and the measures of the sea; I understand the dumb, and hear him who does not speak." For when Amelius asked where Plotinus' soul had gone, Apollo, who said of Socrates "Of all men Socrates is wisest", declared this about Plotinus; hear how much, and what:
!note "About oak or rock": Hesiod, Theogony 35, a proverb for idle talk. The god's verses are the oracle Apollo gave to Croesus (Herodotus 1.47). The verse on Socrates is the form of the oracle given by Diogenes Laertius (2.37); Plato's Apology (21a) reports it in prose: no one is wiser.

@p
@greek ἄμβροτα φορμίζειν
"I begin to strike up on the lyre an immortal hymn of song for a gentle friend, weaving it with the honey-sweetest sounds of the well-tuned lyre under the golden plectrum. I call the Muses too to raise their voice together, with cries of every sound and rushes of every harmony, as they were called to set up a dance for Aeacus' grandson in the immortal frenzies and songs of Homer. Come, holy chorus of the Muses, let us cry out, breathing together to the last end of all song; I, Phoebus of the thick hair, am in your midst.
!flag I Phoebus, Bathychaites, singing in the midst | βαθυχαίτης is an epithet, "thick-haired" (of deep hair), not a name; and "singing" is MacKenna's: the god says only that he is in their midst.
!note Aeacus' grandson (Αἰακίδης, "of Aeacus' line") is Achilles: the Muses sang his dirge at his funeral (Odyssey 24.60-62).

@p
@greek δαῖμον, ἄνερ τὸ πάροιθεν
"Daemon, a man before, but now nearer the diviner lot of a daemon, since you loosed the bond of human necessity, and, strong in mind, out of the loud-roaring tumult of the body's limbs you swam hard to the shore of a sea-washed headland, far from the crowd of sinners, to set foot on the easy path of the pure soul, where the light of god shines round, where right is, in the pure place, far from lawless sin. Even then, as you struggled to escape the bitter wave of the blood-fed life and its sickening whirls, in the midst of the surge and the unlooked-for tumult, often the goal appeared to you from the blessed ones, dwelling near. Often, when the throws of your mind were rushing to be carried by their own impulses along crooked paths, the immortals lifted them up into straight courses, to the heavenly circles and the immortal path, granting a thick ray of light for your eyes to see out of the gloomy darkness.
!flag Celestial ! Man at first | The Greek δαῖμον is "daemon", the first word of the poem's turn: Plotinus, a man before, is now a daemon, which the next line says outright ("the diviner lot of a daemon").
!note The swim to the shore recalls Odysseus' swim through the storm to the Phaeacians' land (Odyssey 5). Plotinus himself, in I.6.8, uses Odysseus' flight home as the image of the soul's return.

@p
@greek οὐδέ σε παμπήδην
"Sweet sleep never wholly held your eyelids; but, scattering from your eyes the heavy blot of mist, carried in the whirls you saw with your eyes many lovely things, which none of the men who have sought wisdom could easily see. But now that you have loosed the tent, and left the tomb of your daemonic soul, you go already to the company of daemons, which breathes with lovely breezes, where there is friendship, and tender longing, delightful to see, full of pure gladness, ever filled with ambrosial streams from the gods, from which come the persuasions of loves, and sweet breath, and windless air; where the brothers Minos and Rhadamanthus dwell, of the golden race of great Zeus, where just Aeacus is, and Plato, holy strength, and fair Pythagoras, and all who set up the dance of immortal love, who were allotted a common birth with the most blessed daemons, where the heart is warmed forever in festivals and joys. O blessed one, after enduring so many contests, you go among the holy daemons, armed with mighty lives. Let us end the song and the well-turning circle of the dance for Plotinus, Muses of many joys; this much my golden lyre has told of the one whose life is blessed."
!flag not granted to all that labour in wisdom's quest | The Greek οὔτις is "no one": the things Plotinus saw were such as "none" of the seekers of wisdom could easily see, not "not all".
!flag crowned with unfading life | The Greek κορυσσάμενος is "armed" (helmeted, as a warrior), and ζαμενέσσι ... ζωῇσι "mighty lives": nothing is crowned or unfading.
!note "Tent" and "tomb" for the body: the Phaedo (81c) and the play on σῶμα, body, and σῆμα, tomb, in the Gorgias (493a) and the Cratylus (400c). Minos, Rhadamanthus and Aeacus are the judges of the dead in the Gorgias' myth (523e-524a).

@ch Life.23 | What the oracle means
Porphyry reads the oracle: it says Plotinus was gentle, wakeful and pure, and strove to escape this life; the god beyond intellect appeared to him four times while Porphyry was with him, and once to Porphyry himself; the gods guided his writing; and after death he went, not to be judged, but to the company of the blessed.

@p
In these verses it is said that he was good, and gentle, and mild above all, and kindly, which we too knew him truly to be; it is said that he was wakeful, and kept his soul pure, and always pressed on towards the divine, which he loved with all his soul, and that he did everything to be set free, "to escape the bitter wave" of the "blood-fed" life here. So it was above all to this daemonic man, who often lifted himself up in his thoughts to the first god, the god beyond, by the ways Plato lays out in the Symposium, that that god appeared who has neither shape nor any form, but is set above intellect and all the intelligible. I, Porphyry, say that I too once drew near to him and was united with him, in my sixty-eighth year.
!flag that God appeared, the God throned above the Intellectual-Principle | MacKenna leaves out that this god has "neither shape nor any form" (ὁ μήτε μορφὴν μήτε τινὰ ἰδέαν ἔχων).
!note Volkmann prints a "and" in square brackets, as a word to be cut ("to be set free [and] to escape"); it is left out here. The ways of the Symposium are Diotima's ascent from beautiful bodies to beauty itself (210a-212a). The god beyond intellect is Plotinus' One, or Good (VI.9).

@p
@greek ἐφάνη γοῦν τῷ Πλωτίνῳ
"The goal appeared" to Plotinus, "dwelling near": for his end and goal was to be united with the god who is over all, and to come near him. And he reached this goal four times, I think, while I was with him, by an unspeakable act (and not in potency). And it is said that the gods often set him straight when he was carried aslant, "granting a thick ray of light", so that what he wrote was written under their oversight and watching.
!flag by no mere latent fitness but by the ineffable Act | The Greek ἐνεργείᾳ ... καὶ οὐ δυνάμει is Aristotle's pair, "in act and not in potency": the union really happened, and was not only possible.
!note Volkmann sets "and not in potency" (καὶ οὐ δυνάμει) in round brackets.

@p
@greek ἐκ δὲ τῆς ἀγρύπνου
From his wakeful contemplation, within and without, "you saw", it says, "with your eyes many lovely things", which none of the men who give themselves to philosophy "could easily see". For the contemplation of men may be better than human; but compared with divine knowledge it may be lovely, but not so as to be able to grasp the depth that the gods grasp. So the oracle has made plain what he did while he still wore a body, and what he attained. After he was set free from the body, it says, he came into the company of daemons, where friendship, longing, gladness and love hanging on the god live as citizens; and where those called the judges of souls are stationed, sons of the god, Minos, Rhadamanthus and Aeacus, to whom he has gone not to be judged, but to be with them, as are the others who are pleasing to the gods. These are Plato, Pythagoras, and all others who "set up the dance of immortal love"; there the most blessed daemons have their birth, and live a life packed with festivals and joys, and go on in it forever, blessed by the gods.
!flag not vouchsafed to all that take the philosophic path | As in the oracle, the Greek is "none" (οὔτις), not "not all".
!note Volkmann prints "they are" (εἰσί) in square brackets, as a word to be cut; English needs it.

@ch Life.24 | The edition: the first three Enneads
Plotinus left the arrangement and correction of his books to Porphyry. Porphyry did not keep their order by date, but, like the editors of Epicharmus and of Aristotle, gathered them by subject into six sets of nine, the easier first: the first Ennead on ethics, the second and third on the cosmos.

@p
Such, then, is the life of Plotinus as we have told it. But since he himself entrusted us with the arranging and correcting of his books, and I promised him while he was alive, and announced to the others, that I would do it, first I thought it right not to leave the books in confusion by their dates, as they had been given out. Following Apollodorus of Athens and Andronicus the Peripatetic, of whom the one gathered Epicharmus the comic poet into ten volumes, and the other divided the works of Aristotle and Theophrastus into treatises, bringing related subjects together, so I too, having the fifty-four books of Plotinus, divided them into six Enneads, gladly finding the perfection of the number six and the nines; and to each Ennead I brought together the books that belong together, giving first place to the lighter questions.
!note Six is a "perfect" number: it is the sum of its parts (1 + 2 + 3). An Ennead is a set of nine. Porphyry's list by date of writing is in chapters 4 to 6.

@p
@greek ἡ μὲν γὰρ πρώτη ἐννεὰς
The first Ennead holds these, the more ethical: 1. What is the living being, and what is the human being [I.1]. 2. On virtues [I.2]. 3. On dialectic [I.3]. 4. On happiness [I.4]. 5. Whether happiness grows with time [I.5]. 6. On beauty [I.6]. 7. On the first good and the other goods [I.7]. 8. Where evils come from [I.8]. 9. On the reasonable going out of life [I.9].
!flag 8. On Evil. | Porphyry's title is "Where evils come from" (πόθεν τὰ κακά).
!note The numbers and the Ennead places in square brackets here and below are the Workshop's; Porphyry lists the titles only.

@p
@greek ἡ μὲν οὖν πρώτη ἐννεὰς τάδε
The first Ennead, then, contains these, taking in the more ethical subjects. The second, gathering the writings on nature, contains those on the cosmos and on what belongs to the cosmos. They are these: 1. On the cosmos [II.1]. 2. On the circular movement [II.2]. 3. Whether the stars act [II.3]. 4. On the two kinds of matter [II.4]. 5. On what is in potency and in act [II.5]. 6. On quality and form [II.6]. 7. On complete blending [II.7]. 8. How things seen far off look small [II.8]. 9. Against those who say that the maker of the cosmos is evil and that the cosmos is evil [II.9].
!flag 3. On the Stars. | Porphyry's title is "Whether the stars act" (εἰ ποιεῖ τὰ ἄστρα), the question the treatise asks.

@p
@greek ἡ δὲ τρίτη ἐννεὰς
The third Ennead, still holding to the cosmos, has taken in these, on what is studied in the cosmos: 1. On destiny [III.1]. 2. On providence, first [III.2]. 3. On providence, second [III.3]. 4. On the daemon allotted to us [III.4]. 5. On love [III.5]. 6. On the impassibility of the bodiless [III.6]. 7. On eternity and time [III.7]. 8. On nature, contemplation and the One [III.8]. 9. Various considerations [III.9].

@ch Life.25 | The fourth and fifth Enneads
