The life that is best is measured not by time but by eternity, whole and all at once.

@ch I.5.1 | Happiness is always present
Happiness is taken in the present: remembering having been happy adds nothing, nor does saying so. It lies in being disposed in a certain way, and that disposition is present, as the activity of life is.

@p
Does being happy increase with time, when being happy is always taken in the present? The memory of having been happy would not do anything, nor would being happy lie in saying so; it lies in being disposed in a certain way. And the disposition is in being present, as is the activity of life.

@ch I.5.2 | Our desire is for what is present
If it is because we always long to live and be active, then tomorrow's happiness would be greater than today's, happiness would no longer be measured by virtue, and the gods would never be perfectly happy. But longing, once it gets what it seeks, has the present; and the longing to live, which seeks being, seeks what is present now.

@p
If someone says that, since we always long to live and to be active, getting this is being happier, then in the first place tomorrow's happiness will be greater, and each next one always greater than the one before, and being happy will no longer be measured by virtue. Then the gods too will be happier now than before, and not yet perfectly happy, and never perfectly. Then again, longing, when it gets what it seeks, has got what is present, and always seeks what is present, until it has being happy. The longing to live, which seeks being, would be for the present, if being is in the present. And if it wants the future and what comes next, it wants what it has and what it is, not what is past or what is to come, but for what already is to be: not seeking the forever, but that the present should already be, now.
!flag at once time supplants moral excellence as the measure of felicity | The Greek says only that happiness "will no longer be measured by virtue" (τῇ ἀρετῇ); that time takes its place is MacKenna's gloss.

@ch I.5.3 | Seeing the same thing longer
What if he was happy for longer, and saw the same thing for longer? If he saw it more exactly, time did something for him; if he saw it the same throughout, one look gives as much.

@p
What then? Suppose he was happy for a longer time, and looked at the same thing with his eyes for a longer time. If in the longer time he saw it more exactly, time would have done something more for him. But if he saw it in a like manner throughout, the one who looked once has as much.

@ch I.5.4 | Longer pleasure
One man had pleasure for longer; but pleasure is not rightly counted in happiness. If pleasure means unhindered activity, it is the same as what we are asking about; and even more pleasure has only the present, its past is gone.

@p
But the other man had pleasure for longer. This, however, would not rightly be counted towards being happy. And if someone calls pleasure unhindered activity, he is saying the same as what we are looking for. And even the greater pleasure always has only the present; its past is gone.
!note "Unhindered activity" (ἐνέργεια ἀνεμπόδιστος) is Aristotle's account of pleasure, Nicomachean Ethics VII.12-13 (1153a14-15, 1153b9-12).

@ch I.5.5 | Happy from the start, or only at the end
If one man was happy from beginning to end, another only later, and a third only earlier, are they equal? The comparison is not among the happy, but of the unhappy, when they were unhappy, with the happy: the happy man's advantage is in the present.

@p
What then? If one man was happy from the beginning to the end, another in the later time, and another was happy earlier and then changed, do they have equal shares? Here the comparison has been made not among all who are happy, but of those who were not happy, at the time when they were not, with one who is happy. If he has something more, then, he has as much as a happy man has over those who are not happy; and he has this advantage over them by what is present.

@ch I.5.6 | Does misery grow with time?
Is not the miserable man more miserable for longer, as long pains are worse? There time adds something only because the illness becomes a settled state and the body worsens; misery grows by becoming more, not by lasting longer. Happiness has a limit and a bound, and is always the same; if it increases, it is with greater virtue, not with more years.

@p
What, then, of the unhappy man? Is he not more unhappy for a longer time? And do not other hardships, long pains, griefs, and everything of that kind, give greater misfortune over a longer time? But if these things increase the evil in this way with time, why not their opposites too, and being happy, in the same way? With griefs and pains, one could say that time gives an addition, as when an illness persists: it becomes a settled state, and the body gets worse with time. For if it stayed the same and the harm were no greater, here too what is painful would always be what is present; unless one counts in what is past, looking at what has come about and remains; and, in the unhappy state, the evil is stretched out over the longer time, as the vice grows by persisting. At any rate it is by the addition of more, not by the longer time, that being more unhappy comes about.

@p
@greek τὸ δὲ πλεῖον ἴσως
But the longer is perhaps not all at once, and one must not call it longer at all, counting what no longer is together with what is. Happiness has a limit and a bound, and is always the same. If there is an increase here too beyond the longer time, so that one who advances to greater virtue is happier, it is not the happiness of many years that one is counting up and praising, but the happiness that has become greater at the time when it is greater.
!flag true happiness is not vague and fluid | The Greek says positively that happiness "has a limit and a bound" (ὅρον τε καὶ πέρας) and is always the same.

@ch I.5.7 | Time, and the life of eternity
We count past time with the present, and call it more; but it is absurd to call happiness that no longer exists more than present happiness. Time scatters what is one in the present: it is the image of eternity, and dissolves what stays there. The best life is that of real being, measured not by time but by eternity, which is neither more nor less, without extension, whole and all at once.

@p
But why, if we must look only at what is present and not count it in with what has been, do we not do the same with time, but count the past time in with the present and call it more? Why, then, shall we not say that the happiness is as great as the time? And we would divide happiness by the divisions of time too; for, again, measuring it by the present, we shall make it indivisible. Rather, it is not absurd to count time that no longer is, since we could make a count of things that came to be and no longer are, the dead, for example. But it is absurd to say that a happiness that no longer is is present, and more than the present one. For being happy is something we hold to have come about, while the time beyond the present is what no longer is. And in general, the more of time means a scattering of something that is one in the present. That is why it is fittingly called an image of eternity, an image that wants to make what stays in eternity disappear in its own scattering. So if it takes from eternity what stayed in it and makes it its own, it has destroyed it: it was kept safe, in a way, for a while, by eternity, but destroyed if it all came to be in time.
!note Time as "an image of eternity" is from Plato's Timaeus (37d), where the maker makes "a moving image of eternity". Plotinus treats time and eternity at length in III.7, done beside Taylor.
!flag a mimic of eternity | The Greek says εἰκὼν αἰῶνος, "an image of eternity", Plato's words in the Timaeus (37d): an image, not a mimic.

@p
@greek εἴπερ οὖν τὸ εὐδαιμονεῖν κατὰ ζωὴν
If, then, being happy is a matter of good life, clearly we must place it in the life of what really is, for that is the best. So it must not be counted by time, but by eternity. And eternity is neither more nor less, nor of any length, but is this "this" itself, without extension and not in time. So we must not join what is to what is not, nor time to eternity, nor what is everlasting in time to what is eternal, nor stretch out what has no extension. We must take it all as a whole, if ever you take it, taking not the undivided of time, but the life of eternity, not made of many times, but all of it together, from all time.
!note "Not made of many times, but all of it together" (πᾶσαν ὁμοῦ) recalls Parmenides' being, "all together, one, continuous" (fragment 8.5), as Plotinus reads it in III.7.

@ch I.5.8 | The memory of past pleasures
Does the memory of the past make the longer happiness greater? Memory of wisdom would make him wiser, which changes the question; memory of pleasure would mean he needs much delight. What is pleasant in remembering a good dinner yesterday, or ten years ago, or being wise last year?

@p
If someone were to say that the memory of the past, staying in the present, gives the more to one who has been happy for a longer time, what would he mean by memory? Either memory of wisdom that came before, and then he would be speaking of one who is wiser, and would not keep to the supposition; or memory of pleasure, as if the happy man needed much delight and were not content with what is present. And what pleasure could there be in remembering something pleasant? It would be like remembering that yesterday one enjoyed a fine dish; and if it were ten years ago, it would be more laughable still. And so with wisdom: remembering that last year I was wise.
!flag last year's morality | The Greek is τὸ δὲ τῆς φρονήσεως, ὅτι πέρυσιν ἐφρόνουν, "and of wisdom, that last year I was wise": wisdom, not morality.

@ch I.5.9 | The memory of fine things
Would memory of fine things count for something? That belongs to a man who lacks fine things now and seeks, for want of them, the memory of the past.

@p
But if the memory were of fine things, would something not be said for it here? No: that belongs to a man who is lacking in fine things in the present, and who, because he does not have them now, seeks the memory of what has been.

@ch I.5.10 | Many fine actions
A long time gives many fine actions. But that makes happiness out of things past and one thing present. A man can be happy without actions at all, and more so; actions do not give the good of themselves: the disposition makes them fine, and the wise man enjoys the good from what he has. The happy man's pleasure comes from his settled state; to put happiness in actions is to put it outside virtue and the soul.

@p
But a long time makes many fine actions, which the one who is happy only for a short while has no share in, if we should call happy at all one who is not so through many fine actions. Rather, whoever says that being happy comes from many times and actions forms it out of what no longer is but has passed, together with one thing that is present. That is why we placed being happy in the present, and then asked whether to have been happy for longer is to be happier. So we must ask this: whether happiness over a long time has the advantage by its greater number of actions.

@p
@greek πρῶτον μὲν οὖν ἔστι
In the first place, it is possible to be happy without having been engaged in actions at all, and not less, but more so than one who has acted. Then, actions do not give well-being of themselves: it is dispositions that make actions fine as well, and the wise man enjoys the good even when he acts, not because he acts, nor from what happens, but from what he has. For the saving of one's country could come even from a bad man, and the pleasure at the saving of one's country could come to him even if another had done it. So it is not this that makes the happy man's pleasure, but his settled state, which makes his happiness, and any pleasure that comes through it. To put being happy in actions is to put it in things outside virtue and outside the soul. For the activity of the soul lies in being wise and in being active in this way within itself. And this is being happy.
!note The chapter answers Aristotle, who held in the Nicomachean Ethics I.7 (1098a18-20) that happiness needs "a complete life", since one swallow does not make a spring.
!flag the good of the act is for all alike, no matter whose was the saving hand | The Greek says that "the pleasure at the saving of one's country could come to him even if another did it": it is about the good man's own pleasure, not a good shared by all.

@treatise I.6 | On beauty
What makes things beautiful? Not symmetry alone, Plotinus argues, since simple things are beautiful too, and the same face is beautiful at one time and not at another. A body is beautiful by sharing in a form, which comes from the divine and gathers many parts into one; the soul knows it because the soul is akin to it. Higher up are the beauties of ways of life, of knowledge and of the virtues, which the soul sees without the senses; the soul is made ugly by mixing with the body and made beautiful by purification, for every virtue is a purification. Beyond the soul, intellect and the Forms are beauty, and beyond them the Good, the source of beauty. To see it we must flee, not on foot or by ship, but by waking another sight within, and working on ourselves like a sculptor on a statue until we become beautiful ourselves.

@ch I.6.1 | Beauty is not symmetry
Beauty is mostly in what we see, but also in what we hear, and higher up in ways of life, knowledge and the virtues. What makes bodies beautiful? Almost everyone says symmetry, with good colour. But then only composite things would be beautiful, never simple ones: not colours, sunlight, gold, lightning or the stars, nor a single note. The same face, its symmetry unchanged, is beautiful at one time and not at another; and what symmetry is there in ways of life, laws or virtues, or in intellect alone?

@p
Beauty is found mostly in what we see. But it is found in what we hear too, in the putting together of words and in every kind of musical art, for melodies and rhythms are beautiful. And for those who go on upwards from perception, there are beautiful ways of life, actions, states of character and kinds of knowledge, and the beauty of the virtues. Whether there is some beauty before these, the inquiry itself will show. What is it, then, that has made us imagine bodies as beautiful, and made our hearing lean towards sounds as beautiful? And the things that come after, which depend on the soul: how are they all beautiful? Are all things beautiful by one and the same thing, or is beauty one thing in a body and another in something else? And what are these, or this?
!flag another for the bodiless | The Greek asks whether beauty is "one thing in a body, another in something else" (ἐν ἄλλῳ); it does not yet speak of the bodiless.

@p
@greek τὰ μὲν γὰρ οὐ παῤ αὐτῶν
For some things, bodies for instance, are not beautiful because of what underlies them, but by sharing; others are beautiful in themselves, like the nature of virtue. The same bodies appear beautiful at one time and not beautiful at another, so that being a body is one thing and being beautiful another. What, then, is this that is present to bodies? This is what we must look into first. What is it that moves the eyes of those who look, turns them towards itself, draws them on, and makes them glad at the sight? If we find this, we might use it as a step and see the rest as well.
!flag so that there is a good deal between being body and being beautiful | The Greek says "as if being bodies were one thing and being beautiful another" (ἄλλου ... ἄλλου): the two are different, not far apart.

@p
@greek λέγεται μὲν δὴ παρὰ πάντων
Almost everyone says that what makes beauty for the eye is the symmetry of the parts towards one another and towards the whole, with good colour added; and that for these things, and for everything else in general, being beautiful is being symmetrical and measured. On this view nothing simple will be beautiful, and only what is composite will necessarily be beautiful. The whole will be beautiful, but the parts will not each be beautiful of themselves; they will only contribute to the whole so that it is beautiful. And yet, if the whole is beautiful, the parts must be beautiful too: a beautiful thing is surely not made of ugly parts, but beauty must take hold of all of them. And on this view the beautiful colours, and the light of the sun as well, since they are simple and do not have their beauty from symmetry, will be outside beauty. How, then, is gold beautiful? And lightning in the night, or the stars: what makes them beautiful to see? With sounds, likewise, the simple will be gone, though each note, in many cases, is beautiful in itself as well as in the beautiful whole.

@p
@greek ὅταν δὲ δὴ καὶ τῆς αὐτῆς συμμετρίας
And when, with the same symmetry remaining, the same face appears beautiful at one time and not at another, surely we must say that being beautiful is something other than the symmetrical, and that the symmetrical is beautiful because of something else. And if they pass on to beautiful ways of life and beautiful words, and make symmetry the cause there too, what symmetry could be meant in beautiful ways of life, or laws, or studies, or kinds of knowledge? How could theorems be symmetrical with one another? If it is because they agree, there is agreement and harmony among bad things too: that self-control is foolishness agrees and is in tune with justice being a "noble simplicity", and the two accord with each other. Again, every virtue is a beauty of the soul, and a truer beauty than those before; but how is it symmetrical? Not as magnitudes, nor as numbers, though the soul has many parts. In what ratio would the putting together or the blending of the parts, or of the theorems, be? And what would the beauty of intellect be, when it is alone?
!flag morality means weakness of will | The Greek says that self-control (σωφροσύνη) is foolishness (ἠλιθιότης), and justice a "noble simplicity" (γενναία εὐήθεια): these are the bad views of Thrasymachus in the Republic, not "weakness of will" and "honesty".
!note "Noble simplicity" is Thrasymachus' name for justice in the Republic (348c-d). The view Plotinus attacks, that beauty is symmetry with good colour, is the Stoics' (Cicero, Tusculan Disputations IV.31).

@ch I.6.2 | Beauty in bodies is form
The soul knows beauty at first glance and welcomes it as akin to itself, and turns away from the ugly. Bodies are beautiful by sharing in a form: what has no share in form and reason is ugly. The form gathers many parts into one, and beauty settles on what has been made one, giving itself to the parts and the whole; on what is one and of like parts, it gives itself to the whole. So a body becomes beautiful by sharing in a reason that comes from the gods.

@p
Let us take it up again, then, and say what it is that is first beautiful in bodies. There is something that becomes perceptible at the first glance, and the soul speaks of it as if it understood it, recognises it and welcomes it, and as it were fits itself to it. But when it meets the ugly, it shrinks back, refuses it and turns away from it, out of tune with it and estranged. We say that the soul, being by nature what it is, and close to the better reality among beings, rejoices when it sees something akin to it, or a trace of what is akin, is thrilled by it, refers it to itself, and is reminded of itself and of its own; it recollects itself.
!flag something which the soul names as from an ancient knowledge | The Greek says the soul speaks of it "as if it understood it" (ὥσπερ συνεῖσα): no ancient knowledge is named, though the soul is "reminded" of itself later in the chapter.

@p
@greek τίς οὖν ὁμοιότης τοῖς τῇδε
What likeness, then, is there between the beautiful things here and those there? For if there is a likeness, they will be alike; but how are both these and those beautiful? These, we say, by sharing in a form. For everything shapeless that is by nature able to receive shape and form, as long as it has no share in reason and form, is ugly and outside divine reason; and this is the utterly ugly. But ugly too is what has not been mastered by shape and reason, because the matter has not let itself be wholly shaped by the form.

@p
@greek προσιὸν οὖν τὸ εἶδος
So the form comes to it, and what is to be one thing out of many parts it orders into one by putting it together, brings it to one completion, and makes it one by agreement; since the form was one itself, what it shaped had to be one, as far as it could be, being made of many. Beauty, then, settles on it once it has been brought into one, and gives itself both to the parts and to the whole. But when it takes hold of something that is one and of like parts, it gives itself to the whole: as if at one time it gave beauty to a whole house with its parts, and at another a nature gave beauty to a single stone, and art to the house. In this way a body becomes beautiful, by sharing in a reason that comes from the gods.
!flag by communicating in the thought that flows from the Divine | The Greek says "by sharing in a reason (λόγου) that has come from gods" (ἀπὸ θεῶν), in the plural: a forming principle, not a thought, and from gods rather than from "the Divine".

@ch I.6.3 | How the soul judges beauty in bodies
A power of the soul set over beauty knows it, using the form within it as a ruler. The builder calls a house beautiful when it fits the form of house within him: the house outside is that form divided in the mass of matter. Perception gathers the form that has mastered matter into one and gives it to the form within. The beauty of colour is simple, the mastery of the dark in matter by light; so fire is beautiful beyond other bodies. Unheard harmonies make the heard ones. These beauties of sense are images and shadows that run out into matter.

@p
It is known by the power set over it, which has more authority than anything else to judge its own things, when the rest of the soul joins in the judgement. Or perhaps the soul itself speaks, fitting it to the form it has within, and using that form for its judgement as a ruler is used for what is straight. But how does what is in body agree with what is before body? How does the builder fit the house outside to the form of house within and say that it is beautiful? Is it not that the house outside, if you take away the stones, is the form within, divided by the outer mass of matter, partless but appearing in many parts?
!flag never in doubt whenever any lovely thing presents itself for judgement | The Greek says that nothing has more authority to judge its own objects "when the rest of the soul judges along with it" (ὅταν καὶ ἡ ἄλλη συνεπικρίνῃ ψυχή): a judgement shared with the rest of the soul, not a power that is never in doubt.

@p
@greek ὅταν οὖν ἡ αἴσθησις
So when perception sees the form in bodies binding together and mastering the nature opposed to it, which is shapeless, and a shape riding gloriously on other shapes, it gathers into one what was scattered, brings it up and takes it within, partless now, and gives it to what is within as something in tune with it, fitting it, and dear to it: as a trace of virtue appearing in a young man is welcome to a good man, being in tune with the true virtue within him.

@p
@greek τὸ δὲ τῆς χρόας κάλλος
The beauty of colour is simple, by shape and by the mastery of the dark in matter, through the presence of light, which is bodiless and is reason and form. That is why fire itself is beautiful beyond other bodies: it has the rank of form among the other elements. It is above them in place and the finest of bodies, as if near the bodiless; and it alone does not receive the others, while they receive it. They are warmed, but it is not cooled; it is coloured first, and the others take the form of colour from it. So it shines and sparkles, as if it were a form. What is not mastered, but fades in the light, is no longer beautiful, since it does not share in the whole form of colour.
!flag The beauty of colour is also the outcome of a unification | The Greek says the beauty of colour is "simple" (ἁπλοῦν), by shape and mastery of the dark: Plotinus has just said that colours are beautiful without being composite, so they are not a unification of parts.

@p
@greek αἱ δὲ ἁρμονίαι
The harmonies in sounds, the hidden ones making the open ones, have in this way too made the soul understand beauty, showing it the same thing in another medium. It belongs to perceptible harmonies to be measured by numbers, not in every ratio, but in one that serves to make form and to master. So much for the beauties of perception, which are images and shadows that run out, as it were, and come into matter, adorn it, and thrill us when they appear.
!note "The hidden harmony is better than the open one": Heraclitus (fragment 54), whose words Plotinus seems to echo.

@ch I.6.4 | The higher beauties, and the lovers
For the beauties beyond perception we must go up and leave perception below. As those born blind cannot speak of visible beauty, so no one can speak of the beauty of ways of life and virtue who has not seen how beautiful the face of justice and self-control is, more beautiful than the evening or the morning star. Those who see it feel wonder, longing and love; all souls do, but the lovers most.

@p
As for the beauties further on, which perception is no longer allotted to see, but which the soul sees and speaks of without instruments, we must go up and look at them, leaving perception to wait below. As with the beauties of perception no one could speak of them who had not seen them or grasped them as beautiful, those born blind, for instance, in the same way no one can speak of the beauty of ways of life who has not welcomed the beauty of ways of life, of kinds of knowledge and the like; nor of the splendour of virtue, if one has never even imagined how beautiful is the face of justice and of self-control, and that "neither the evening nor the morning star is so beautiful".
!note "Neither the evening star nor the morning star is so wonderful" is said of justice in Aristotle's Nicomachean Ethics V.1 (1129b28-29), after a line of Euripides.

@p
@greek ἀλλὰ δεῖ ἰδόντας μὲν εἶναι
But there must be those who have seen it with that by which the soul sees such things, and who, having seen it, are glad and struck with amazement and thrilled, much more than before, since now they are touching what is true. For these are the feelings that must come about with whatever is beautiful: wonder and a sweet amazement, longing and love, and a thrill with pleasure. Souls can feel these for the things not seen too, and they do: all of them, so to speak, but more so those of them that are more given to love; just as with bodies all love, but they are not stung equally, and there are some who are stung most, and these are the ones who are called lovers.
!note The amazement, the thrill (πτόησις) and the sting of love are the Phaedrus' (250a-251e), where the soul that sees beauty here is reminded of beauty there.

@ch I.6.5 | The beautiful soul and the ugly soul
What do the lovers of beauty beyond perception feel at beautiful souls, their own or another's? Not shape or colour, but a soul without colour, with self-control, justice, grim-faced courage and intellect shining on all. To find what makes them beautiful, look at the ugly soul: full of desires, fears and envy, it has been mixed with the body and taken on a foreign form, like a man who has fallen into mud. Its ugliness is an addition; purified, like gold freed from earth, the soul lays it all aside.

@p
So we must ask the lovers of what is beyond perception too: What do you feel about what are called beautiful ways of life, beautiful manners, self-controlled characters, and in general the works of virtue and its dispositions, and the beauty of souls? And when you see that you yourselves are beautiful within, what do you feel? How is it that you are stirred to frenzy and roused, and long to be with yourselves, gathering yourselves together away from your bodies? For this is what those who are truly lovers feel.

@p
@greek τί δέ ἐστι, περὶ ὃ ταῦτα πάσχουσιν
What is it about which they feel this? Not a shape, not a colour, not some size, but a soul, itself without colour, and having a self-control without colour, and the rest of the splendour of the virtues: when you see in yourselves, or behold in another, greatness of soul, a just character, pure self-control, courage with its grim face, and dignity and modesty blooming on a disposition that is steady, calm and untroubled, and over all of these intellect, in the form of a god, shining upon them. Admiring and loving these, how do you call them beautiful? They are, and they appear, and whoever has seen them will never say anything other than that they are what really is. Being what, really? Beautiful. But the argument still wants to know what they are that has made the soul lovable: what is it that shines out on all the virtues like a light?
!flag courage of the majestic face | The Greek says courage "with a grim face" (βλοσυρὸν ἔχουσαν πρόσωπον), fierce to look at, as Homer's Ajax is "grim of face" (Iliad 7.212); not majestic.

@p
@greek βούλει δὴ καὶ τὰ ἐναντία λαβών
Will you take the opposites too, the ugly things that come about in the soul, and set them beside it? For perhaps what ugliness is, and why it appears, may help us towards what we are looking for. Let there be an ugly soul, then, without restraint and unjust, full of very many desires and very much turmoil, in fear through cowardice, in envy through pettiness, thinking, in all it thinks, of mortal and lowly things, crooked everywhere, the friend of impure pleasures, living a life of whatever it feels through the body, and taking ugliness for a pleasure. Shall we not say that this very ugliness has come to it as an evil brought in from outside, which has mutilated it and made it impure and smeared with much evil? It no longer has a pure life or perception: it lives a dim life, through the mixture of evil, blended with much death. It no longer sees what a soul should see, and it is no longer allowed to stay within itself, because it is always being dragged to the outside, the below and the dark.
!flag thinking, in the little thought it has, only of the perishable and the base | The Greek says "thinking all that it thinks as mortal and lowly things" (πάντα φρονοῦσα ἃ δὴ καὶ φρονεῖ θνητὰ καὶ ταπεινά): "the little thought it has" is MacKenna's.

@p
@greek ἀκάθαρτος δή, οἶμαι
It is impure, I think, carried everywhere by the pull of whatever strikes perception, with much of the body mixed into it, keeping company with much that is material, and taking into itself a different form, it has been changed by a blending with what is worse: as if someone who had sunk into mud or mire no longer showed the beauty he had, but what was seen was what he had taken on from the mud or mire. His ugliness has come to him by the addition of something foreign, and his task, if he is to be beautiful again, is to wash himself and be purified, and so to be what he was. So we should be right to say that a soul becomes ugly by mixing and blending, and by leaning towards the body and matter.
!flag by a fall, a descent into body, into Matter | The Greek speaks of "mixing and blending and inclining" (νεύσει) towards body and matter: a leaning, not a fall or a descent.

@p
@greek καὶ ἔστι τοῦτο αἶσχος ψυχῇ
And this is ugliness for a soul: not to be pure and unmixed, like gold filled with earthy matter; if someone takes this away, the gold is left, and it is beautiful, alone, apart from the others, and with itself only. In the same way the soul too, when it has been set apart from the desires it has because of the body, with which it kept too close company, and freed from the other feelings, and purified of what it has from being embodied, staying alone, lays aside all the ugliness that came from the other nature.

@ch I.6.6 | Every virtue is a purification
As the old saying goes, every virtue is a purification: self-control shuns the body's pleasures, courage does not fear death, greatness of soul looks down on things here, wisdom turns from what is below. The purified soul becomes form and reason, wholly bodiless, of the divine, where beauty springs from. To become good and beautiful is to become like God. The Good and beauty are first; intellect is beautiful from it, soul from intellect, and the rest from soul.

@p
For, as the old saying has it, self-control and courage and every virtue is a purification, and wisdom itself too. That is why the mystery rites are right to hint that whoever has not been purified will lie in the mire in Hades, since what is not pure is fond of mire because of its badness, as pigs, whose bodies are not pure, delight in such things. For what would true self-control be but not keeping company with the pleasures of the body, but fleeing them as impure and not belonging to what is pure? And courage is not fearing death. And death is the soul being apart from the body. One who is glad to be alone does not fear this. Greatness of soul is looking down on things here. And wisdom is thinking, turned away from things below, and leading the soul to what is above.
!flag And Wisdom is but the Act of the Intellectual-Principle withdrawn | The Greek says wisdom is "thinking" (νόησις), "in a turning away from the things below": the soul's own thinking, not an act of intellect.
!note The "old saying" is the Phaedo's (69b-c): self-control, justice, courage and wisdom itself are a purification, and those who come to Hades uninitiated and unpurified "will lie in the mire". That death is the soul being apart from the body is also the Phaedo's (64c, 67d).

@p
@greek γίνεται οὖν ἡ ψυχὴ καθαρθεῖσα
So the soul, once purified, becomes form and reason, wholly bodiless and intellectual, and wholly of the divine, from which comes the spring of beauty and all that is akin to it. So the soul, led up to intellect, becomes more beautiful. Intellect, and what comes from intellect, is its own beauty and not a foreign one, since then it is truly only soul. That is why it is rightly said that the soul's becoming good and beautiful is its becoming like God, since from there comes the beautiful and the other portion of beings. Or rather, beings are beauty, and the other nature is the ugly. And the ugly is the same as the first evil; so that the opposite is the same as good and beautiful, or the Good and beauty.
!flag Hence the Soul heightened to the Intellectual-Principle is beautiful to all its power | The Greek says the soul led up to intellect "is beautiful the more" (ἐπὶ τὸ μᾶλλον): more beautiful, not beautiful to all its power.
!flag for from the Divine comes all the Beauty and all the Good in beings | The Greek says "from there comes the beautiful and the other portion of beings" (ἡ μοῖρα ἡ ἑτέρα τῶν ὄντων); the Good is not named. The phrase is hard, and translators read it differently.
!note Becoming like God is the Theaetetus' (176a-b), where it is "becoming just and holy, with wisdom".

@p
@greek ὁμοίως οὖν ζητητέον
So we must look for the beautiful and the good, and for the ugly and the evil, in the same way. And first we must set down beauty, which is also the Good. From it comes intellect, which is at once the beautiful. Soul is beautiful by intellect; and the other things are now beautiful from soul as it shapes them, those in actions and those in ways of life. And bodies too, as many as are called beautiful, the soul makes so. For since it is divine, and like a portion of the beautiful, it makes beautiful whatever it touches and masters, as far as they can share in it.

@ch I.6.7 | The ascent to the Good, and the greatest contest
We must go up to the Good, which every soul desires. It is reached by those who go up, turn round and strip off what they put on coming down, as those who go into the holy places are purified and go in naked, until they see it alone, pure and simple, on which all things hang. Whoever sees it is filled with love and wonder and laughs at other loves. Seeing beauty itself, pure, not filled with flesh, he would need no other beauty. This is the greatest and last contest for souls: not to miss the best of sights, for which one should give up kingdoms over earth, sea and sky.

@p
We must go up again, then, to the Good, which every soul desires. Whoever has seen it knows what I mean, and how beautiful it is. For it is desired as good, and the desire is towards it; but it is reached by those who go up to what is above, turn round, and strip off what we put on as we came down: as for those who go up to the holy places of the temples there are purifications, and the laying aside of the clothes they wore before, and going up naked; until, passing in the ascent all that is foreign to the god, one sees with oneself alone the god alone, unmixed, simple and pure, on which all things hang, and to which they look, and are, and live, and think: for it is the cause of life, of intellect and of being.
!flag Even the desire of it is to be desired as a Good | The Greek says "it is desired as good, and the desire is towards it" (ἐφετὸν μὲν γὰρ ὡς ἀγαθὸν καὶ ἡ ἔφεσις πρὸς τοῦτο): the Good is what is desired; the desire itself is not called desirable.
!flag who will set all their forces towards it | The Greek says those who "have turned round" (ἐπιστραφεῖσι): the soul's turning back to what is above, not an effort of all its forces.

@p
@greek τοῦτο οὖν εἴ τις ἴδοι
If anyone sees this, what loves will he feel, what longings, wishing to be blended with it, and how he will be amazed, with pleasure! One who has not yet seen it can desire it as good; but for one who has seen it, it is a matter of admiring it as beautiful, being filled with wonder and pleasure, being amazed without harm, loving with a true love and sharp longing, laughing at other loves, and looking down on what he used to think beautiful. This is what those feel who have met with the forms of gods or daemons, and could no longer welcome the beauty of other bodies in a like way. What, then, do we think, if someone were to see beauty itself, by itself, pure, not filled with flesh or body, not on earth or in heaven, so that it is pure? For all these are brought in from outside and mixed, and are not first, but come from it.
!flag Gods or Supernals | The Greek has "of gods or daemons" (θεῶν ... ἢ δαιμόνων), the spirits between gods and men; "Supernals" is MacKenna's word.
!note Beauty "itself by itself", "pure, unmixed, not filled with human flesh", and to be seen at the top of the ascent, is the Symposium's (211a-e).

@p
@greek εἰ οὖν ἐκεῖνο, ὃ χορηγεῖ
If, then, one were to see that which supplies all things, and gives while staying by itself, and receives nothing into itself, staying in the sight of it and enjoying it, being made like it, what other beauty would he still need? For this, being itself beauty above all and the first beauty, makes its lovers beautiful and lovable. Here, too, the greatest and last contest is set before souls, for which all the toil is: not to be left without a share in the best of sights. Whoever reaches it is blessed, having seen a blessed sight, and whoever does not is unfortunate. For it is not the man who has not won beautiful colours or bodies, or power, or offices, or kingship, who is unfortunate, but the one who has not won this, and this alone; for the winning of which one ought to give up kingships and rule over the whole earth and sea and sky, if by leaving them behind and looking down on them, turned towards it, he might see it.
!flag spurning the world of sense from beneath his feet | The Greek says only "leaving these behind and looking down on them" (καταλιπών τις ταῦτα καὶ ὑπεριδών), the kingdoms and rule just named; the world of sense under his feet is MacKenna's picture.
!note The "greatest and last contest" for souls recalls the Phaedrus (247b) and the Republic (608b, "great is the contest"); "a blessed sight" is the Phaedrus' (250b).

@ch I.6.8 | The flight to the fatherland
How can one see a beauty that stays within the holy places? Leave the sight of the eyes outside and do not run after beautiful bodies, which are images: like the man in the myth who reached for a beautiful reflection on the water and sank, the one who clings to them sinks in soul into darkness. "Let us flee to our dear fatherland": as Odysseus left Circe and Calypso. Our fatherland is where we came from, and our father is there. The journey is not on foot, by chariot or by ship: shut your eyes and wake another sight, which all have and few use.

@p
What is the way, then? What is the means? How will one see a beauty beyond all means, which stays as it were within the holy temples and does not come out where the uninitiated too might see it? Let whoever can go and follow it within, leaving outside the sight of the eyes, and not turning back to the former splendours of bodies. For when one sees the beauties in bodies one must not run to them, but, knowing that they are images, traces and shadows, flee to that of which they are images. For if someone runs to it, wishing to take hold of it as if it were real, like a beautiful image riding on the water, which someone, as some myth hints, I think, wished to take hold of and sank down into the stream and vanished, in the same way the one who holds on to beautiful bodies and does not let go will sink, not in his body but in his soul, into depths that are dark and joyless to intellect, where he will stay blind in Hades, and keep company with shadows there as here.
!note The myth is surely Narcissus', who fell in love with his own reflection in a spring (Ovid, Metamorphoses III.339-510), though Plotinus does not name him; in some versions he drowned reaching for it.

@p
@greek φεύγωμεν δὴ φίλην ἐς
"Let us flee to our dear fatherland," one might urge with more truth. What, then, is the flight? And how shall we put out to sea? As Odysseus did, who says, hinting at it, I think, that he left the sorceress Circe, or Calypso, not content to stay, though he had pleasures for the eyes and kept company with much beauty of sense. Our fatherland is the place from which we came, and our father is there. What, then, is the voyage, and the flight? It is not to be made on foot: feet only carry us from one land to another. Nor should you get ready a carriage drawn by horses, or anything that goes by sea. You must let all these go and not look, but, as if shutting your eyes, change to and wake another sight, which everyone has but few use.
!note "Let us flee with our ships to our dear fatherland" is Agamemnon's line in the Iliad (2.140, 9.27). Odysseus is kept by Circe and Calypso in the Odyssey (9.29-32, 10.135-574, 5.1-268); the flight from here is also the Theaetetus' (176a-b).

@ch I.6.9 | Make yourself beautiful, and see
The inner sight, newly woken, cannot yet bear bright things. Train it on beautiful ways of life, then on good men's works, then on their souls. To see a beautiful soul, look at yourself, and, if you are not yet beautiful, work like a sculptor on a statue until virtue shines out. When you have become pure light, unmeasured, you are sight itself: look, for only this eye sees the great beauty. The eye must become like the sun to see it; the soul must become beautiful. First comes intellect, where the Forms are beauty; beyond it is the Good, the spring and source of beauty.

@p
What, then, does that inner sight see? When it is just waking it cannot quite look at bright things. So the soul itself must be trained to look first at beautiful ways of life, then at beautiful works, not those that the arts produce, but those that men who are called good produce; then look at the souls of those who produce the beautiful works. How, then, would you see a good soul, and what kind of beauty it has? Go back into yourself and look. And if you do not yet see yourself as beautiful, do as the maker of a statue that is to be beautiful does: he takes away here, scrapes there, makes one part smooth and another clean, until he shows a beautiful face on the statue. So you too, take away what is excessive, straighten what is crooked, purify what is dark and work to make it bright, and do not stop fashioning your own statue until the godlike splendour of virtue shines out on you, until you see self-control "standing on a holy pedestal".
!flag the perfect goodness surely established in the stainless shrine | The Greek says "self-control standing on a holy pedestal" (σωφροσύνην ἐν ἁγνῷ βεβῶσαν βάθρῳ), the Phaedrus' words (254b): self-control, not "perfect goodness".
!note The lover who fashions the beloved "like a statue" (ἄγαλμα) is the Phaedrus' (252d), and the charioteer sees beauty "standing with self-control on a holy pedestal" at 254b.

@p
@greek εἰ γέγονας τοῦτο
If you have become this, and have seen it, and have come to be with yourself purely, with nothing to stop you from becoming one in this way, nor having anything else mixed in within you, but being wholly yourself true light alone, not measured by size, nor bounded by shape so as to be made less, nor again made larger by being without limit, but everywhere unmeasured, as greater than every measure and better than every quantity: if you see that you have become this, then, having become sight, be confident about yourself, and having already gone up here, needing no one to show you any more, look intently and see. For this alone is the eye that sees the great beauty.

@p
@greek ἐὰν δὲ ἴῃ ἐπὶ τὴν θέαν
But if it comes to the sight bleared with vices and not purified, or weak, unable through lack of courage to look at very bright things, it sees nothing, even if another shows it what is there and can be seen. For the seer must make itself akin and like to what is seen before it turns to look. No eye would ever have seen the sun if it had not become sunlike, nor would a soul see beauty if it had not become beautiful. Let each one, then, first become wholly godlike and wholly beautiful, if he is to see the Good and the beautiful.
!flag who cares to see God and Beauty | The Greek says "the Good and the beautiful" (τἀγαθόν τε καὶ καλόν): the Good, not God.
!note The eye that is "sunlike" (ἡλιοειδής) is the Republic's (508b), in Socrates' likeness of the sun and the Good; and seeing "very bright things" after being in the dark is the cave's (515e-516a).

@p
@greek ἥξει γὰρ πρῶτον
For first, going up, he will come to intellect, and there he will see all the Forms as beautiful and will say that this is beauty, the Forms: for all things are beautiful by them, the offspring and being of intellect. What is beyond this we call the nature of the Good, which holds beauty in front of itself. So, speaking roughly, the first is the beautiful; but distinguishing the intelligibles, he will call the intelligible beautiful the place of the Forms, and the Good what is beyond, the spring and source of the beautiful. Or he will put the Good and the first beautiful in the same; but in any case, beauty is there.
!flag the Primal Good and the Primal Beauty have the one dwelling-place | The Greek puts this as another way of speaking: "or he will place the Good and the first beautiful in the same" (ἢ ἐν τῷ αὐτῷ ... θήσεται), "but in any case the beautiful is there": a choice left open, not a conclusion.
!note "The place of the Forms" (ὁ τῶν εἰδῶν τόπος) recalls the Republic's "intelligible place" (508c, 517b); and the Good "beyond" being is the Republic's (509b).

@treatise I.7 | On the first good and the other goods
A short treatise, among the last Plotinus wrote. The good of each thing is its natural activity, and of a composite thing the activity of its best part; but the Good itself acts towards nothing, and all things act towards it: it is good by staying still, beyond being, activity and intellect, as the centre of a circle or the sun with its light. Other things have the good by being one, being and form, and the soul through intellect. Life is a good only to the good; and death is no evil, but rather a good, for a soul that is not living by the compound.

@ch I.7.1 | The Good stays still, and all things turn to it
The good of each thing is its natural activity, and of a composite, the activity of its better part. If something is best of all and beyond beings, and acts towards nothing while all else acts towards it, it is the Good, and others have the good by becoming like it and acting towards it. So the Good is good not by activity or thought, but by staying still: beyond being, it is beyond activity and intellect. All things hang on it, as the circle on its centre, or light on the sun.

@p
Could anyone say that the good for each thing is anything other than the activity of its life according to nature; and, if it is made of many, that its good is the activity of the better part in it, its own, according to nature, and lacking nothing? The soul's activity according to nature, then, is its good. And if, being best, it is also active towards the best, the good will not only be good for it, but will be the Good without qualification. So if something is active towards nothing else, being the best of beings and beyond beings, while the other things are active towards it, clearly this would be the Good, through which the others too can share in good. And all the others that have the good in this way would have it in two ways: by having become like it, and by directing their activity towards it.
!note Aristotle's account of the human good, "activity of soul in accordance with virtue", and of the best virtue if there are more (Nicomachean Ethics I.7, 1098a16-18), is the starting point; "beyond beings" is the Republic's "beyond being" (509b).

@p
@greek εἰ οὖν ἔφεσις καὶ ἐνέργεια
If, then, desire and activity towards the best is good, the Good must not look towards anything else or desire anything else, but be at rest, the spring and source of activities according to nature, making the others like the Good, not by its activity towards them, since they are active towards it. So it is not by activity, nor by thinking, that it is the Good, but by its very staying. For since it is beyond being, it is beyond activity too, and beyond intellect and thinking. And again, we must set down as the Good that on which all things hang, while it hangs on nothing; for only so is it true that it is "what all things desire".
!flag it can but remain unmoved | The Greek says the Good is "at rest" (ἐν ἡσύχῳ), "in quiet": not "unmoved" in Aristotle's sense, though the meaning is close.
!note "What all things desire" is the definition of the good with which Aristotle opens the Nicomachean Ethics (I.1, 1094a2-3).

@p
@greek δεῖ οὖν μένειν αὐτό
So it must stay, and all things must turn towards it, as a circle does towards the centre from which all the radii come. And the sun is a pattern of this, being as it were a centre for the light that comes from it and hangs on it: the light is everywhere with it and is not cut off, and even if you wish to cut it off on one side, the light is towards the sun.
!flag try all you will to separate the light from the sun, or the sun from its light, for ever the light is in the sun | The Greek says "even if you wish to cut it off on one side, the light is towards the sun" (πρὸς τὸν ἥλιον): the light still stretches back to its source. "Or the sun from its light" is not in the Greek, and the light is "towards" the sun, not "in" it.

@ch I.7.2 | How other things have the good
The lifeless are turned to soul, and soul to the Good through intellect. Each thing has something of the Good by being one, being and sharing in form, but only an image, since what it shares are images of being and the one. The first soul, after intellect, is good through intellect and has the good when it looks to it. Life is good for what lives, intellect for what shares in it: what has both is turned towards the Good in two ways.

@p
And how are all the other things towards it? The lifeless are towards soul, and soul is towards it through intellect. Each thing has something of it by being in a way one and in a way being, and it shares in form too. As it shares in these, so it shares in the Good. In an image, then: for what it shares in are images of being and of the one, and the form likewise. For the soul there is life: for the first soul, the one after intellect, a life nearer to truth, and through intellect it has the form of good; and it would have the Good if it looked towards intellect; and intellect comes after the Good. Life, then, is the good for whatever has life, and intellect for whatever has a share in intellect; so that whatever has life with intellect is towards it in two ways.
!flag But the Universe outside ; how is it aligned towards the Good ? | The Greek asks "and all the other things, how are they towards it?" (τὰ δ’ ἄλλα πάντα): it does not speak of a universe outside.
!flag are no more than images of the Ideal-Form | The Greek says that what each thing shares in are "images of being and of the one, and the form likewise" (εἴδωλα ὄντος καὶ ἑνός, καὶ τὸ εἶδος ὡσαύτως): images of being and the one, not of the Ideal-Form.

@ch I.7.3 | Is life a good, and death an evil?
Life is not good for every living thing: in the bad it limps, like an eye that cannot see clearly. If life, mixed as it is, is good, is death an evil? Not to what no longer is, or has no life; and if the soul lives after death, it is better off without the body. To a soul that keeps itself pure there is no evil, as to the gods; to one that does not, life is the evil, not death. Life in the body is an evil in itself, but by virtue the soul is in the good, living not by the compound but already separating itself.

@p
If life is a good, does this belong to everything that lives? No: life limps in the bad man, like an eye in one who does not see clearly, for it does not do its work. If life for us, mixed as it is, is a good with evil in it, how is death not an evil? Or evil for whom? For evil must happen to something; and what no longer is, or if it is, has been deprived of life, cannot receive evil, any more than a stone can. If there is life and soul after death, it would be a good, all the more as it does its own work without the body. And if it becomes part of the whole soul, what evil could there be for it, being there? And in general, as for the gods there is good, but no evil, so it is for the soul that keeps itself pure; and if it does not, it is not death that would be evil for it, but life. And if there are punishments in Hades, again life is evil for it there too, since it is not life alone.
!flag the misfortune is still life, a life of a definite character | The Greek says only that life is evil there too, "since it is not life alone" (ὅτι μὴ ζωὴ μόνον): it is life with something added, the punishment; "a life of a definite character" is MacKenna's.

@p
@greek ἀλλ’ εἰ σύνοδος μὲν ψυχῆς
But if life is the coming together of soul and body, and death their parting, the soul will be able to receive both. But if life is good, how is death not an evil? Life is good for those for whom it is good, not in so far as it is a coming together, but because it wards off evil through virtue; and death is the greater good. Or we must say that life in the body is in itself an evil, but that by virtue the soul comes to be in the good, not living by the compound, but already separating itself.
!flag feel itself at home | The Greek says the soul "will be able to receive both" (ἀμφοτέρων δεκτική), life and death: it can be in either state, not that it feels at home in both.
!note Death as the soul's being apart from the body, and the philosopher's practice of dying, are the Phaedo's (64c-67e).

@treatise I.8 | What evils are, and where they come from
To know what evil is, we must first know the Good: what all things hang on and desire, needing nothing, the measure and limit of all, which gives intellect, being, soul and life. Evil cannot be among the beings or beyond them; if it exists, it is among the things that are not, as an image of being. It is what is unmeasured, unlimited, formless and always in need, not as an attribute but as its very nature: matter, the first evil, evil in itself. Bodies are evil in so far as they share in matter, and the soul's vice is a second evil, in so far as the soul looks to matter, is mixed with it, or falls into it. Evil exists of necessity, as Plato says, because the Good is not alone: going out from it, things must come to a last, after which nothing more can come to be, and that is matter. Vice is not evil itself, as virtue is not the Good itself; the weakness of the soul is matter's presence in it. To deny evil is to deny the Good; and evil, of necessity, is bound in beautiful chains, like a prisoner in golden fetters, so that the gods need not see it, and men may see images of beauty even in it.

@ch I.8.1 | To know evil, first know the Good
Those who ask where evils come from should first ask what evil is. But with what power could we know it, if we know by likeness, and evil is not a form but the absence of every good? If the same knowledge covers contraries, we must first see clearly what the Good is; though how good and evil are contrary is a question for later.

@p
Those who ask where evils come from, whether they have come into the beings or into some kind of beings, would make a fitting beginning for their inquiry if they first laid down what evil is, and what the nature of evil is. For so it would be known where it has come from, where it has its seat and to what it belongs as an attribute; and, in general, it would be agreed whether it exists among the beings at all. But with what power in us could we know the nature of evil? That would be hard to say, since knowledge of each thing comes about by likeness. Intellect and soul, being forms, would know forms and would have their desire for them; but how could anyone imagine evil as a form, when it shows itself in the absence of every good?
!note Knowledge by likeness, like known by like, is an old principle: Aristotle reports it of Empedocles and of Plato's Timaeus (On the Soul I.2, 404b13-18).

@p
@greek ἀλλ’ εἰ, ὅτι τῶν ἐναντίων
But if, since the same knowledge is of contraries, and evil is contrary to the good, the knowledge of the good will also be the knowledge of evil, then those who are going to know evils must look closely at the Good, since the better things come before the worse and are forms, while the worse are not, but are rather a privation. It is still a question, though, how the good is contrary to the evil: unless it is as beginning to end, or as form to privation. But that will come later.
!flag are nearer to Non-Being | The Greek says the worse are "rather a privation" (στέρησις μᾶλλον), the lack of a form, Aristotle's word; MacKenna's "nearer to Non-Being" says something else. Where the question comes back, he has "utter Lack" for the same word.
!note "The same knowledge is of contraries" is Aristotle's principle (for instance Metaphysics IV.2, 1004a9-10; Prior Analytics I.1, 24a21). The question is taken up in chapter 6.

@ch I.8.2 | The Good, intellect, and the soul that dances round it
What the Good is: what all things hang on and desire, needing nothing, the measure and limit of all, giving intellect, being, soul and life. Intellect there is not like ours, which learns from premises: it has all things and is all things, each whole and distinct. The soul dances round it and through it sees God. This is the life of the gods, without evil: the first, second and third goods round the king of all.

@p
For now, let us say what the nature of the Good is, as far as the present argument requires. It is that on which all things hang, and which all beings desire, having it as their principle and needing it. It lacks nothing, is sufficient to itself, needs nothing; it is the measure and limit of all things, and gives from itself intellect and being, soul and life, and the activity about intellect. And up to this point all things are beautiful. For it is itself beyond beauty, and reigns beyond the best things in the intelligible world; and the intellect there is not an intellect like those one might suppose, by comparison with what we call intellects here: those filled out from premises, able to understand what is said, reasoning, studying what follows from what, and seeing the beings by following the steps, as if they did not have them before, but were empty until they learned, intellects though they are.
!note "What all things desire" is Aristotle's definition of the good (Nicomachean Ethics I.1, 1094a2-3); the Good as "beyond beauty" and as king of the intelligible world recalls the Republic's Good, beyond being in rank and power (509b), "a beauty beyond telling" (509a).

@p
@greek οὐ δὴ ἐκεῖνος ὁ νοῦς τοιοῦτος
That intellect is not like this. It has all things and is all things, and is with them by being with itself, and has all things without having them. For they are not other than it, and it something else; nor is each of the things in it apart. Each is the whole, and is everything everywhere, and they are not confused, but are again apart: at any rate, what shares in it does not share in all of them at once, but in whatever it can. And intellect is the first activity of the Good and its first being, while the Good stays in itself; intellect is active about it, as if living about it.
!flag And the First Act is the Act of The Good stationary within Itself, and the First Existence is the self-contained Existence of The Good | In the Greek it is intellect that "is the first activity of that one and its first being" (πρώτη ἐνέργεια ἐκείνου καὶ πρώτη οὐσία), while the Good "stays in itself": the Good does not itself act or exist as intellect does. MacKenna gives the first activity and the first existence to the Good.

@p
@greek ἡ δὲ ἔξωθεν περὶ τοῦτον χορεύουσα
And the soul, dancing round intellect from outside, looking at it and contemplating what is within it, sees God through it. And this is the life of the gods, "without sorrow and blessed"; and evil is nowhere here, and if things had stopped here, there would be no evil, but first, second and third goods. "All things are around the king of all, and he is the cause of all beautiful things, and all things are his; and the second around the second, and the third around the third."
!flag circles around the Intellectual-Principle | The Greek says the soul "dances" round it (χορεύουσα), as a chorus round an altar: Plotinus' image for the soul's ordered life about what it loves.
!note The closing words are a quotation of the Second Letter ascribed to Plato (312e), which the Platonists read as a teaching of three principles: the Good, intellect and soul. "Without sorrow and blessed" (ἀπήμων καὶ μακάριος) has a ring of Homer's untroubled gods.

@ch I.8.3 | Evil is among the things that are not: matter
Evil cannot be among the beings or beyond them, for they are good. It must be among the things that are not: not what does not exist at all, but an image of being, or something still further from it. It is measurelessness against measure, unlimited against limit, formless, always in need, never at rest, utter poverty; and these are not attributes of it but its very being. What lies under shapes, forms and limits, decked with an order not its own, an image of the beings: that is the first evil, evil in itself.

@p
If the beings, and what is beyond the beings, are such as this, then evil cannot be among the beings, nor in what is beyond the beings, for these are good. It remains, then, if evil exists, that it is among the things that are not, as a kind of form of non-being, and that it has to do with something mixed with non-being, or sharing in non-being in some way. Non-being, here, is not what wholly is not, but only something other than being: not non-being in the way that motion and rest are, which are about being, but as an image of being, or something still more non-being than that. And this is the whole world of sense, and all that sensible things undergo; or something later than these, an attribute of them; or their principle; or one of the things that make this up, being such as this.
!flag of an utterly different order from Authentic-Being | The Greek says non-being here is "only other than being" (ἕτερον μόνον τοῦ ὄντος), Plato's sense of non-being in the Sophist; MacKenna makes it "utterly different".
!flag there is no question here of movement or position with regard to Being | The Greek says this non-being is not like "motion and rest, which are about being" (ὡς κίνησις καὶ στάσις ἡ περὶ τὸ ὄν): two of the Sophist's greatest kinds, which are "not being" only because they are other than it. MacKenna's "position" is rest (στάσις).
!note Non-being as "other than being", and motion and rest as kinds that are other than being, are from the Sophist (254d-259b; 258b for non-being as "the other").

@p
@greek ἤδη γὰρ ἄν τις εἰς ἔννοιαν
For one might now come to some notion of it as measurelessness against measure, unlimited against limit, formless against what gives form, always needy against the self-sufficient: always undefined, never standing still, open to every affection, insatiable, utter poverty. And these are not attributes of it, but are, as it were, its very being; and whatever part of it you see, that too is all of these. The other things, those that share in it and become like it, become evil, but are not what evil is.
!flag that part is all lawless void | The Greek says only "that too is all of these" (καὶ αὐτὸ πάντα ταῦτα): the whole list of what evil is. "Lawless void" is MacKenna's.
!note "Utter poverty" (πενία παντελής) recalls Poverty in Diotima's story of the birth of Eros (Symposium 203b-c), which Plotinus reads as matter in III.5; measure, limit and the unlimited are the Philebus' (23c-26d).

@p
@greek τίνι οὖν ὑποστάσει
To what reality, then, do these belong, not as things other than it, but as itself? For if evil happens to something else, it must first be something itself, even if it is not a substance. As there is the good itself and the good that belongs to something, so there is evil itself, and the evil that, depending on it, belongs to something else. What, then, is measurelessness, if not in what is unmeasured? What is measure, if not in what is measured? But just as there is measure not in what is measured, so there is measurelessness not in what is unmeasured. For if it were in something else, it would be either in what is unmeasured, but that has no need of measurelessness, being unmeasured itself; or in what is measured, but what is measured cannot have measurelessness in the respect in which it has been measured. There must, then, be something unlimited in itself, and formless in itself, and the other things listed before, which marked out the nature of evil; and if there is anything after it of this kind, either it has this mixed in it, or it is such by looking towards it, or it makes things such.
!flag become a cause of evil by consecration to this Absolute | The Greek says such a thing is evil "by looking towards it" (βλέπον πρὸς αὐτό), or "makes things such" (ποιητικόν): there is no "consecration".

@p
@greek τὴν δὴ ὑποκειμένην σχήμασι
So what underlies shapes and forms, and figures and measures and limits, and is decked with an order not its own, having nothing good of itself, an image compared with the beings, the being of evil, if there can be a being of evil: this the argument finds to be the first evil, evil in itself.
!note What "underlies" (ὑποκειμένη) is matter, Aristotle's substrate; Plotinus does not name it until chapter 4, but the description is his account of matter in II.4.

@ch I.8.4 | Bodies, and the evil soul
Bodies are evil in so far as they share in matter: their form is not true, they are without life, they destroy one another, they hinder the soul and are always flowing away. The soul in itself is not evil; the evil soul is the one enslaved to what is evil in it, mixed with measurelessness through the body, its reason darkened by matter. The perfect soul, turned to intellect, stays pure; the soul that goes out from itself is filled with indefiniteness and sees darkness.

@p
The nature of bodies, then, in so far as it shares in matter, would be an evil. For bodies have a form that is not true, are deprived of life, destroy one another by their disorderly motion, are hindrances to the soul in its own activity, and flee from being, always flowing. But the soul in itself is not evil, nor again is every soul evil. Which soul, then, is evil? The one, as Plato says, "enslaved to that in which the vice of the soul naturally arises", since it is the irrational form of the soul that receives evil: measurelessness and excess and deficiency, from which come licentiousness and cowardice and the rest of the soul's vice, involuntary affections, which produce false opinions, making it think evil and good the things it flees and pursues.
!flag Soul, on the contrary, since not every Soul is evil, is not an evil Kind | The Greek has two claims: the soul "in itself is not evil" (καθ’ ἑαυτὴν μὲν οὐ κακή), and "nor is every soul evil"; MacKenna leaves out "in itself" and makes the second the reason for a conclusion of his own.
!flag all the states, foreign to the true nature | The Greek calls them "involuntary affections" (ἀκούσια παθήματα): the soul does not choose them.
!flag not by their true value but by the mere test of like and dislike | The Greek says the false opinions make the soul "think evil and good the things it flees and pursues": it mistakes what it avoids and seeks for evil and good. There is no "true value" or "like and dislike".
!note "Enslaved to that in which the vice of the soul arises" turns round the Phaedrus (256b), where the lovers who live in philosophy have "enslaved that in which the vice of the soul arose, and freed that in which virtue arose".

@p
@greek ἀλλὰ τί τὸ πεποιηκὸς
But what made this vice, and how will you trace it back to that principle and cause? First, such a soul is not outside matter, nor by itself. So it is mixed with measurelessness and has no share in the form that orders it and brings it to measure, since it is blended with a body that has matter. Then, too, the reasoning part, if it is harmed, is kept from seeing, by the affections, by being darkened by matter, by inclining towards matter, and, in general, by looking not towards being but towards becoming, whose principle is the nature of matter; and matter is so evil that it fills with its own evil even what is not yet in it, but has only looked at it. For being wholly without a share in good, a privation of it and unmixed lack, it makes like itself whatever touches it in any way.

@p
@greek ἡ μὲν οὖν τελεία
The soul that is perfect, then, and inclines towards intellect, is always pure: it has turned away from matter, and neither sees nor approaches anything undefined, unmeasured or evil. So it stays pure, wholly bounded by intellect. But the soul that does not stay so, but goes out from itself, by being not perfect, not first, a kind of phantom of that soul by its deficiency, in so far as it fell short, is filled with indefiniteness and sees darkness, and already has matter, looking at what it does not see, in the way that we are said to see darkness too.
!note Seeing darkness as a kind of not-seeing is Aristotle's (On the Soul II.10, 422a20-21; III.2, 425b20-22).

@ch I.8.5 | Evil is total lack; evils outside the soul
Is evil, then, the soul's lack of good, and darkness only second? No: not any lack, but total lack is evil, and that is matter, which does not even have being except in name. Evil must be thought of not as this or that evil, but as what all the particular evils are forms of. Disease, ugliness and poverty also go back to matter. So we are not the source of evils: they are before us, and hold men against their will; those who can, escape them.

@p
But if the lack of good is the cause of seeing darkness and being with it, evil would be in the soul's lack, and first there, with the darkness second; and the nature of evil would no longer be in matter, but before matter. Or rather, evil is not in any lack whatever, but in total lack. What falls a little short of the good is not evil, since it can even be perfect for its own nature. But when it lacks wholly, as matter does, this is real evil, with no share in good. For matter does not even have being, so as to share in good in that way: being belongs to it only in name, so that it is true to say that it is not. Lack, then, has not-being-good; total lack has being evil; and greater lack has being able to fall into evil, and is evil already.
!note "Only in name" (ὁμώνυμον) is Aristotle's term for things that share a name but not its meaning (Categories 1, 1a1-6).

@p
@greek τῷ χρὴ τὸ κακὸν νοεῖσθαι
So we must think of evil not as this or that evil, such as injustice or some other vice, but as that which is none of these yet, while these are, as it were, forms of it, made specific by additions: as in the soul there is wickedness, and its forms in turn, distinguished either by the matter it concerns, or by the parts of the soul, or by one being like seeing, another like impulse or being affected.

@p
@greek εἰ δέ τις θεῖτο καὶ τὰ ἔξω
But if someone holds that the things outside the soul are evils too, such as disease, ugliness and poverty, how will he trace them back to that nature? Disease is the lack and excess of material bodies that do not bear order and measure; ugliness is matter not mastered by form; poverty is want and privation of the things we need because of the matter to which we are yoked, whose nature is to be need itself. If this is rightly said, we must not suppose that we are a principle of evils, being evil of ourselves: evils are before us, and those that take hold of men hold them against their will; there is an escape from the evils in the soul for those who are able, though not all are able. The position, then, is this: since matter is present in sensible things, evil is not the vice that men have, because it is not even in all men. Some master it (and better still are those in whom it is not present at all), and they master it by what in them is not in matter.
!flag the Evil which holds men down binds them against their will | The Greek speaks of the evils (ἃ δ’ ἂν ἀνθρώπους κατάσχῃ) "that take hold of men", in the plural; it means the evils, not "the Evil".
!note "Need itself" (χρησμοσύνη) is the word for Poverty's want in the Symposium's story (203b-c).

@ch I.8.6 | Why evils must exist: Plato's Theaetetus, and contraries
Plato says that evils can never perish, but exist of necessity, haunting mortal nature and this place. This does not mean the earth, for the flight he speaks of is becoming just and holy with wisdom while on earth. But how is vice contrary to the Good, and is there anything contrary to substance? There is: to the nature of the Good the nature of evil, the two principles farthest apart, limit against unlimitedness, true being against false. Contraries are what are most separate.

@p
We must also consider how it is said that evils could not perish, but exist of necessity; that they are not among the gods, but haunt mortal nature and this place for ever. Is it said in this sense, that heaven is pure of evils, always going in order and carried round in an ordered way, with no injustice there, nor any other vice, nor things wronging one another, but carried round in order, while injustice and disorder are on earth? For this is "mortal nature" and "this place". But the saying that we must flee from here is no longer said of what is on earth. For flight, he says, is not going away from earth, but being just and holy with wisdom, while on earth; so what is meant is that we must flee from vice, and for him evils are vice and what comes from vice. And when the man talking with Socrates says that evils would be done away with, if he could persuade men of what he says, Socrates says that this cannot happen: "For evils must exist of necessity, since there must always be something contrary to the good."
!flag with justice and piety in the light of philosophy | The Greek, quoting Plato, says "just and holy with wisdom" (μετὰ φρονήσεως): practical wisdom, not philosophy.
!note The passage is the Theaetetus (176a-b): "evils can neither perish, Theodorus, for there must always be something contrary to the good, nor have their seat among the gods, but of necessity they haunt mortal nature and this place. So we must try to flee from here to there as quickly as we can; and flight is becoming like God, as far as possible; and to become like him is to become just and holy with wisdom."

@p
@greek τὴν μὲν οὖν κακίαν τὴν περὶ ἄνθρωπον
How, then, can the vice in man be contrary to that Good? It is contrary to virtue, and virtue is not the Good, but a good, which makes us master matter. And how could anything be contrary to that Good? It is not a quality. Then, what necessity is there everywhere that, if one of two contraries exists, so does the other? Let it be possible, and even granted, that the contrary exists when its contrary does, as when there is health there can also be disease; but not of necessity. Or perhaps he does not need to be saying that this is true of every contrary, but has said it of the Good. But if the Good is substance, or beyond substance, how can there be anything contrary to it? That nothing is contrary to substance is credible in the case of particular substances, shown by induction; but it has not been shown in general.
!flag The Good in the Supernal | The Greek says only "that Good" (ἐκείνῳ τῷ ἀγαθῷ).
!flag the good thing by which Matter is brought to order | The Greek says virtue is "a good, which makes us master matter" (ὃ κρατεῖν τῆς ὕλης ποιεῖ): mastering, not ordering.
!flag established by practical proof | The Greek says "shown by induction" (τῇ ἐπαγωγῇ δεδειγμένον), by going through the cases one by one: Aristotle's argument in the Categories.
!note "Nothing is contrary to substance" is Aristotle's (Categories 5, 3b24-27); he shows it from cases ("for what would be contrary to a particular man?").

@p
@greek ἀλλὰ τί τῇ καθόλου οὐσίᾳ
But what will be contrary to substance in general, and in general to the first things? To substance, non-substance; to the nature of the Good, whatever is the nature and principle of evil. For both are principles, one of evils, the other of goods; and all the things in each nature are contrary to those in the other, so that the wholes are contrary too, and more contrary than the other contraries. For the other contraries are either in the same species or the same genus, and share something common in what they are in. But things that are wholly apart, where what completes the being of one has its contraries in the other, how would they not be most contrary of all, if contraries are what stand furthest from one another? To limit, then, and measure and all the other things in the divine nature, unlimitedness and measurelessness and all the other things the evil nature has are contrary; so that the whole is contrary to the whole. And this one has a false being, and is first and really a lie; the other has true being: so that, as falsehood to truth, what it has instead of substance is contrary to their substance.
