!note Contraries as "what stand furthest from one another" is Aristotle's definition: "things furthest apart in the same genus" (Categories 6, 6a17-18; Metaphysics X.4, 1055a3-10). Plotinus drops "in the same genus".

@p
@greek ὥστε ἡμῖν ἀναπέφανται
So it has come to light that it is not true everywhere that nothing is contrary to substance. Even with fire and water, we would have granted that they are contraries, if matter were not common to them, in which the hot and dry, the wet and cold, came about as attributes. If these alone were in them, making up their substance without a common substrate, then here too substance would be contrary to substance. Things, then, that are wholly separate, have nothing in common, and stand at the greatest distance in their nature, are contraries; since contrariety does not belong to them as qualities, nor as any kind of being at all, but as things most separate from each other, made of opposites, and doing contrary things.
!note Fire as hot and dry, water as wet and cold, with matter underlying them, is Aristotle's account of the elements (On Generation and Corruption II.3, 330a30-b7).

@ch I.8.7 | Matter is necessary, and so is evil
Why must there be evil, if there is the Good? Because the universe must have matter, and is a mixture of intellect and necessity, as the Timaeus says: the goods from God, the evils from the "ancient nature". "Mortal nature" is the world that came to be. The escape is not by place, but by virtue and separation from the body. And evil is necessary in another way: going down from the Good, there must be a last thing, after which nothing more can come to be; that is matter, with nothing of the Good left in it.

@p
But how, then, is there of necessity evil, if there is the Good? Is it in this way, that there must be matter in the universe? For this universe is of necessity made of contraries; or rather, it would not exist at all if there were no matter. "The nature of this cosmos is mixed from intellect and necessity", and whatever comes into it from God is good, while the evils come "from the ancient nature": he means the underlying matter, not yet ordered by forms.
!note Plato's Timaeus: the cosmos was born "mixed, from the union of necessity and intellect" (47e-48a); and the Statesman, where the cosmos has all its good from its maker, and its harsh and unjust things from "its former state" and "its ancient nature" (273b-c). Plotinus reads both as matter.

@p
@greek ἀλλὰ πῶς θνητὴν φύσιν
But how does "mortal nature" fit? Let "this place" mean the universe. As for "mortal", the answer is the saying: "since you have come into being, you are not immortal, yet you shall not be undone, by my will." If so, it would rightly be said that evils could not perish. How, then, will one escape? Not by place, he says, but by acquiring virtue and separating oneself from the body; for that is separating oneself from matter too, since whoever is with the body is with matter. He himself somewhere makes clear what it is to separate oneself or not; and "to be among the gods" is to be among the intelligibles, for these are immortal.
!flag Since you possess only a derivative being | The Greek of the Timaeus says "since you have come into being" (ἐπείπερ ἐγένεσθε); MacKenna's words of introduction, "in which the Father of Gods addresses the Divinities of the lower sphere", are his own explanation.
!note The saying is from the Timaeus (41a-b), the maker's words to the gods he has made; the separation of soul from body is the Phaedo's (67c-d), and "among the gods" the Theaetetus' (176a).

@p
@greek ἔστι δὲ τοῦ κακοῦ λαβεῖν
One can also grasp the necessity of evil in this way. Since the Good is not alone, then, by the going out from it, or, if one prefers to say so, by the continual going down and going away, there must be a last, after which nothing more could come to be: and this is evil. And of necessity there is what comes after the first, and so also the last; and this is matter, which has nothing of the first any more. And this is the necessity of evil.

@ch I.8.8 | It is matter, not form, that makes us evil
Some say we are made bad not by matter but by the forms in it: heat, cold, bitterness, fullness and emptiness. But forms in matter act only with matter, as the shape of an axe cuts only in iron; and in matter they are corrupted, as food becomes dog. Bodies, through their blending, make desires violent and judgement dull; and our states change with them. The unmeasured is evil first; vice, as ignorance and measurelessness in the soul, is evil second, as virtue is good by likeness to the Good.

@p
But someone might say that it is not because of matter that we become evil, since neither ignorance nor wicked desires are due to matter. Even if the bad constitution of the body brings about our vice, he would say, it is not the matter that does this, but the form: heat and cold, bitter and salt, and all the forms of flavour; and again fillings and emptyings, and not fillings simply, but fillings with this or that; and, in general, it is being of such and such a kind that makes the differences of desires, and, if you like, of mistaken opinions; so that the form, rather than the matter, is the evil. Even so, he will be forced to grant no less that matter is the evil.
!flag not in the concrete signification but in the presence or absence of just such forms | The Greek says "fillings, emptyings, and not fillings simply, but fillings with such and such things" (πληρώσεις τοιῶνδε): being full of this food or that. There is nothing about concrete significations or the absence of forms.

@p
@greek ἅ τε γὰρ ποιεῖ ἡ ἐν ὕλῃ ποιότης
For what the quality in matter does, it does not do apart from matter, just as the shape of the axe does not cut without the iron. Then too, the forms in matter are not the same as they would be if they existed by themselves: they are forming principles in matter, corrupted in matter, and filled with its nature. Fire itself does not burn, nor does any of the things that exist by themselves do what they are said to do when they come to be in matter. For matter, becoming master of what is reflected in it, corrupts it and destroys it, setting its own nature, which is contrary, beside it: not bringing cold to the hot, but bringing its own formlessness to the form of the hot, its shapelessness to the shape, its excess and deficiency to what is measured, until it has made it matter's own and no longer its own; as in the feeding of animals, what is taken in is no longer what it was when it came, but becomes dog's blood and everything dog, and all the juices become like whatever received them. If, then, body is the cause of evils, matter would be the cause of evils in this way too.
!note The shape of the axe, which cuts only in iron, is Aristotle's example of soul and body (On the Soul II.1, 412b11-15), used already in I.1.4.

@p
@greek ἀλλὰ κρατεῖν ἔδει
"But one should master it", someone might say. But what is able to master it is not pure, unless it flees. And desires are more violent with a certain blending of bodies, some with one and some with another, so that what is in each person does not master them; and people are duller in judging, chilled and hampered by the badness of their bodies, while the opposite blendings make them light and without ballast. Our passing states bear witness to this too: when full, we are different in our desires and in our thoughts from when empty, and different again when filled with one thing or with another.
!flag the incoming Idea should have been able to conquer the Matter | The Greek says only "but one should master it, someone might say" (ἀλλὰ κρατεῖν ἔδει): it is we, or the reason in us, who should master the body's desires; "the incoming Idea" is MacKenna's.
!flag in times of stress, we are not the same | The Greek speaks of being "full" and "empty" (πλήρεις, κενοί), filled with this or with that: our desires and thoughts change with what we have eaten and drunk, not with stress and peace.

@p
@greek ἔστω δὴ πρώτως μὲν
Let it be, then, that the unmeasured is evil first, and what has come to be in measurelessness, by being made like it or by sharing in it, is evil second, because measurelessness belongs to it as an attribute; first the darkness, and in the same way, second, what has been darkened. Vice, then, being ignorance and measurelessness in the soul, is evil second, and not evil itself; for virtue is not the first good either, but is good because it has been made like the Good or has a share in it.

@ch I.8.9 | How we know vice, and how we know matter
We know virtue by intellect itself and wisdom; vice we know as a ruler shows what is not straight, by what it lacks. Total vice we know by taking away; ugliness in matter we see where form has not mastered it. Matter, which has no form at all, we know by taking away every form and receiving formlessness into ourselves: an intellect that is not intellect, like an eye that leaves the light to see darkness.

@p
With what, then, did we come to know these things? And first, with what did we know vice? Virtue we know by intellect itself and by wisdom, for wisdom knows itself; but vice, how? As with a ruler we know what is straight and what is not, so too do we know what does not fit virtue? Do we know it by seeing it, or by not seeing it, vice I mean? Total vice we know by not seeing it, for it is unlimited: by taking away, then, we know what is nowhere; and vice that is not total, by its falling short of this. Seeing part, then, and grasping by the part that is present the part that is absent, which is in the whole form but is missing there, we call it vice, leaving what has been deprived in the undefined.
!flag by means of the philosophic habit | The Greek says "by intellect itself and by wisdom" (νῷ αὐτῷ καὶ φρονήσει), and gives the reason MacKenna leaves out: "for it knows itself".
!note The ruler that shows both straight and crooked was a saying of the schools (Aristotle, On the Soul I.5, 411a5-7: "by the straight we know both itself and the crooked").

@p
@greek καὶ δὴ ἐπὶ τῆς ὕλης
So too with matter: seeing, for instance, an ugly face, where the forming principle has not mastered it so as to hide the ugliness of matter, we imagine ugliness by the lack of form. But what has had no form at all, how do we know that? By taking away every form altogether, we call what has none of it present matter, and we too take formlessness into ourselves in taking away every form, if we are going to look at matter. So this is another intellect, not intellect, that has dared to see what is not its own.
!flag an ugly appearance in Matter | The Greek has "an ugly face" (αἰσχρόν τι πρόσωπον), an example taken from life.
!note Plato's Timaeus says the receptacle, matter for Plotinus, is grasped "by a kind of bastard reasoning, without perception, and hardly to be believed" (52b): Plotinus' "another intellect, not intellect".

@p
@greek ὥσπερ ὄμμα ἀποστῆσαν
As an eye withdraws itself from light, so as to see darkness, and so does not see, because it has left the light without which it could not see darkness; nor could it see without the light, but only not see, so that it may see darkness as far as it could be seen: so intellect too, leaving its own light within itself and going out, as it were, from itself into what is not its own, not bringing its own light with it, is affected in the way contrary to what it is, so as to see what is contrary to it.

@ch I.8.10 | How can what has no quality be evil?
Matter is called "without quality" because it has of itself none of the qualities it receives, not because it has no nature. Quality is an attribute in something else; matter is the substrate. So matter is rightly called both without quality and evil: evil not by having quality, but by not having it, a nature contrary to form.

@p
So much for this. But if matter is without quality, how is it evil? It is called without quality because it has of itself none of the qualities it will receive and that will be in it as in a substrate, but not because it has no nature. If it has some nature, what prevents this nature from being evil, though not evil as a quality? For a quality is that by which something else is said to be qualified. Quality, then, is an attribute, and in something else; but matter is not in something else: it is the substrate, and the attribute is about it. So, since quality has the nature of an attribute, and matter does not have this, it is called without quality. If, then, quality itself is without quality, how could matter, which has not received quality, be called qualified? So it is rightly said to be both without quality and evil: it is not called evil by having quality, but rather by not having it, so that perhaps it might not be evil by being a form, but as a nature contrary to form.
!flag give it Quality and in its very Evil it would almost be a Form | The Greek says matter is evil not by having a quality, "so that perhaps it is not evil as being a form" (ἵνα μὴ ᾖ ἴσως κακὴ εἶδος οὖσα), but as a nature contrary to form; MacKenna's "give it Quality" and "almost" are his reading.

@ch I.8.11 | Is evil only a privation in the soul?
@older 10
An objection: what is contrary to form is privation, and privation is always in something else; so evil would be in the soul, as its vice, and nowhere outside, and matter would be needless. The reply: if the soul were deprived of good, it would be deprived of life and not be soul. But the soul has life of its own nature, a trace of intellect, so it is not evil of itself, nor is the first evil in it.

@p
But the nature contrary to every form is privation; and privation is always in something else, and is not a reality by itself; so that if evil is in privation, evil will be in what is deprived of form, and so it will not exist by itself. If, then, there is evil in the soul, the privation in it will be the evil and the vice, and nothing outside. For other arguments too think fit to do away with matter altogether, and others say that matter, though it exists, is not evil. So we need not look for evil elsewhere, but put it in the soul, and put it there as the absence of good.
!flag This objection may be answered by applying the principle to the case of Evil in the Soul | In the Greek the objection goes on: if evil is privation, it is in the soul, as its vice, and "nothing outside"; there is no answer until the next paragraph ("But if privation..."). MacKenna makes the objector's conclusion into an answer.
!note "Privation" (στέρησις), the lack of a form in what should have it, is Aristotle's (Physics I.7-9; Metaphysics V.22). Plotinus argues in II.4 that matter itself is privation, against Aristotle, who kept them apart (Physics I.9, 192a3-6).

@p
@greek ἀλλ’ εἰ ἡ στέρησις ἐπιβάλλοντός
But if privation is the absence of a form that ought to be present, if there is privation of good in the soul, and the soul makes vice in itself by its own nature, then the soul has nothing good: so it has not even life, though it is soul. The soul will then be soulless, if it has not even life; so that, being soul, it will not be soul. It has life, then, by its own nature, and so it does not have the privation of good of itself. So it is like the good, having something good, a trace of intellect, and is not evil of itself. Nor, then, is it the first evil, nor is the first evil an attribute of it, since not all the good is absent from it.
!flag it has much good in it | The Greek says the soul "has something good" (ἔχουσά τι ἀγαθόν), "a trace of intellect": the argument needs only some good, not much.

@ch I.8.12 | A partial privation?
@older 10
If vice were only a partial privation of good, the soul would be mixed, and its evil not unmixed: we would not yet have found the first, unmixed evil, and the soul would have good as its substance and evil as an attribute.

@p
What, then, if someone said that vice, the evil in the soul, is not a total privation of good, but some privation of good? But if so, having some good and deprived of some, it will have a mixed disposition, and the evil will not be unmixed; and the first, unmixed evil has not yet been found. And the soul will have the good in its substance, and evil as an attribute.
!note "Disposition": Volkmann prints αἴσθησιν, "perception", which makes no sense here; editors since have read διάθεσιν, "disposition", and MacKenna's "a mingled nature" reads it so too.

@ch I.8.13 | Vice is a hindrance; going down to evil itself
@older 10
Perhaps vice is evil as a hindrance, as for the eye; but then it is a cause of evil, not evil itself. As virtue is not the beautiful or the good itself, but shares in what is before and beyond it, so going down from vice one comes to evil itself, and becomes evil by sharing in it, in the place of unlikeness, fallen into dark mud. A soul wholly given to vice has changed into a worse nature, and dies, as a soul can die: sunk in matter, and after the body lying in the mud, asleep in Hades.

@p
Unless, perhaps, vice is evil to the soul in this way, as a hindrance, as something is to the eye for seeing. But then evil would be what makes evil for it, and makes it as something other than evil itself. If, then, vice is a hindrance to the soul, it makes evil, but vice will not be evil itself; and virtue is not the Good either, except as a fellow-worker; so that if virtue is not the Good, neither is vice the evil. Then, too, virtue is not the beautiful itself, nor the good itself; and so neither is vice the ugly itself, nor evil itself.

@p
@greek ἔφαμεν δὲ τὴν ἀρετὴν
We said that virtue is not beauty itself nor the good itself, because before it and beyond it are beauty itself and the good itself, and it is good and beautiful by sharing in them in some way. So, as one going up from virtue comes to the beautiful and the good, so one going down from vice comes to evil itself, starting from vice: seeing it, in so far as this is seeing evil itself, and becoming it, in so far as this is sharing in it. For one comes to be wholly in "the place of unlikeness", where, sinking into it, one will have fallen into dark mud. For if the soul went wholly into total vice, it no longer has vice, but has changed into another nature, the worse; for vice is still something human, mixed with something contrary to it. So it dies, as a soul could die; and its death, while it is still in the body, is to be sunk in matter and filled with it, and, when it has gone out, to lie there, until it runs back up and somehow lifts its sight out of the mud. And this is "going to Hades and falling asleep there".
!flag where, fallen from all our resemblance to the Divine, we lie in gloom and mud | The Greek says "sinking into it, one will have fallen into dark mud" (εἰς βόρβορον σκοτεινόν): "fallen from all our resemblance to the Divine" is MacKenna's gloss on "the place of unlikeness".
!flag And the death of Soul is twofold | The Greek says only "its death": "while still in the body" to be sunk in matter, "and when it has gone out" to lie there. The two times are there, but "twofold" is MacKenna's.
!note "The place of unlikeness" is from the Statesman (273d), where the world, left to itself, is in danger of sinking into "the boundless place (or sea) of unlikeness"; the mud is the Phaedo's (69c), where the uninitiated "lie in mud" in Hades; and "going to Hades and falling asleep there" is the Republic's (534c-d), said of the man who cannot give an account of the Good.

@ch I.8.14 | The weakness of the soul is matter's presence
@older 11
If vice is the soul's weakness, easily moved by desire, anger, rash assent and dim imaginings, where does the weakness come from? Not as in bodies, but by analogy, unless matter is the cause in both. Not in souls apart from matter, which are pure and winged, but in fallen souls, by the presence of something foreign. Matter and soul are in one place; matter begs at the soul, presses in, is lit up but cannot see its source, darkens the light, and keeps the soul's powers from acting. So matter is the cause of the soul's weakness and of its vice: matter is evil first, the first evil.

@p
But if someone says that vice is a weakness of the soul (the evil soul is, at any rate, easily affected and easily moved, carried from every evil into every evil, easily moved to desires, easily provoked to anger, rash in its assents, readily yielding to dim imaginings, like the weakest things made by art or nature, which are easily carried about by winds and by the heat of the sun), it would be worth asking what this weakness of the soul is, and where it comes from. For weakness is not in the soul as it is in bodies: but as there it is the inability to work and being easily affected, so here too the soul received the name of weakness by analogy; unless in this way matter is the same cause of weakness in both. But we must come closer, by argument, to the cause of what is called the weakness of the soul. For it is not densities or rarities, nor again thinness or thickness, nor a disease, such as a fever, that have made the soul weak.
!flag as hasty to compromises | The Greek says the evil soul is "rash in its assents" (προπετῆ εἰς συγκαταθέσεις): quick to agree to impressions, the Stoics' word for judgement, not "compromises".
!flag burned away by every heat | The Greek says such weak things are "easily carried about" (ῥᾳδίαν τὴν φοράν) by winds and the heat of the sun: carried about, not burned away.

@p
@greek ἀνάγκη δὴ τὴν τοιαύτην
This weakness of soul must be either in the souls wholly separate, or in those in matter, or in both. If it is not in those without matter (for these are all pure, and, as the saying is, winged and perfect, and their work is unhindered), it remains that the weakness is in the fallen souls, not pure and not purified; and their weakness would not be the taking away of something, but the presence of something foreign, like phlegm or bile in a body. If we grasp more clearly, and as we ought, the cause of the soul's fall, what we are looking for will become plain.
!note The "winged and perfect" soul is the Phaedrus' (246b-c): "when it is perfect and winged, it travels on high and governs the whole cosmos; but the soul that has shed its wings is carried along until it lays hold of something solid".

@p
@greek ἔστιν οὖν ἐν τοῖς οὖσιν ὕλη
There is, then, among the things that are, matter, and there is soul too, and, so to speak, one place. For there is not one place for matter and another for soul, as if matter's were on earth and soul's in the air: the soul's place is apart by not being in matter; and this is by not being united with matter; and this by not becoming one thing made from soul and matter; and this by not coming to be in matter as in a substrate: and this is being apart. But the powers of the soul are many, and the soul has a beginning, middles and ends; and matter, being present, begs, and as it were pesters, and wants to come inside. "But the whole place is holy", and there is nothing without a share in soul. So matter, laying itself under, is lit up, but cannot grasp that by which it is lit up; for that does not bear with it, though it is present, because matter cannot see it, owing to its badness. But matter darkened the illumination, the light from there, by the mixture, and made it weak, itself providing the coming to be, and the reason for coming into it; for the soul would not have come to what was not present. And this is the fall of the soul, to come in this way into matter; and it is weakened, because not all its powers are present for activity, since matter keeps them from being present, by taking up the place that matter holds, and making the soul, as it were, contract; and what it has taken, as if by theft, it makes evil, until the soul is able to run back up.
!flag for the Soul cannot lift up this foreign thing close by, since the evil of it makes it invisible | The Greek says the source of light "does not bear with" matter (οὐ γὰρ ἀνέχεται αὐτήν), though matter is present, "because matter does not see" it, by its badness: it is matter that cannot see, not matter that is invisible, and nothing is "lifted up".
!note "The whole place is holy" is a line of Sophocles (Oedipus at Colonus 54), said of the grove of the Eumenides at Colonus. Matter "begging" (προσαιτεῖ) recalls Poverty begging at the gods' feast in the Symposium (203b).

@p
@greek ὕλη τοίνυν καὶ ἀσθενείας
Matter, then, is the cause of the soul's weakness and the cause of its vice. So matter is evil before it, and is the first evil. For even if the soul itself, being affected, generated for matter, and if it had to do with matter and became evil, matter is the cause by being present; for the soul would not have come into it, if it had not taken its coming to be from matter's presence.

@ch I.8.15 | To deny evil is to deny the Good; the golden chains
@older 12
If anyone denies that matter exists, the treatise on matter shows that it must. To deny evil is to deny the Good, and with it desire, aversion and wisdom. There is the unmixed good, the mixed, and the wholly evil. The soul has evil only by touching the worse nature: desires, griefs, fears and false opinions belong to the compound. But evil is never evil alone: by the power of the Good it is bound in beautiful chains, like prisoners in gold, hidden so that the gods need not see it, and men may have images of beauty for recollection.

@p
If anyone says that matter does not exist, we must show him the necessity of its existence from the discussion of matter, where this has been dealt with at more length. But if someone says that evil does not exist at all among the things that are, he must also do away with the good, and say that there is nothing to be desired: so no desire either, nor aversion, nor thought. For desire is of the good, aversion of evil, and thought and wisdom are of good and evil, and wisdom is itself one of the goods. There must be, then, both the good, unmixed good, and what is already mixed of evil and good; and what has a greater share of evil has already contributed itself to that which is wholly evil, while what has less, in so far as the evil is less, contributes to the good.
!note The discussion of matter is II.4 (On the two kinds of matter), an earlier treatise in Porphyry's order of writing (number 12) than this one (number 51).

@p
@greek ἐπεὶ καὶ ψυχῇ τί ἂν εἴη
For what would evil be to the soul? Or to what soul would it belong, if it had not touched the worse nature? For there would be no desires, nor again griefs, nor angers, nor fears: fears belong to the compound, lest it be broken up, and griefs and pains to it as it is breaking up; and desires belong to it when something troubles its make-up, or when it looks ahead to a remedy so that nothing troubles it. Imagination is the blow of something irrational from outside; and the soul receives the blow through what is not without parts. And false opinions come to it when it has gone outside its truth; and it goes outside by not being pure. But the desire for intellect is something else: for the soul must be with intellect alone, settled in it, not inclining towards the worse.
!flag within that place of its choice | The Greek says the soul must be "with intellect alone, settled in it" (ἐν αὐτῷ ἱδρυμένην): "that place of its choice" is MacKenna's.

@p
@greek τὸ δὲ κακὸν οὐ μόνον
But evil is not evil alone, because of the power and nature of the Good; for it has appeared, of necessity, wrapped in beautiful bonds, as some prisoners are bound in gold, and is hidden by them, so that, being evil, it may not be seen by the gods, and men may not always have to look at evil, but, even when they do look at it, may be with images of beauty for recollection.
!flag not destitute of Images of the Good and Beautiful | The Greek has "images of the beautiful" (εἰδώλοις τοῦ καλοῦ): "the Good" is added.
!note Recollection (ἀνάμνησις), set off by the images of beauty here, is the Phaedrus' (249c-250d): beauty alone, of the Forms, shines clearly through its images in this world.

@treatise I.9 | On going out of life
A short treatise, perhaps notes, on suicide. The soul should not be forced out of the body, for it would go out with something of the body, and that is to move to another place. It should wait for the body to let it go. To free oneself by force is not to be unaffected, but to act in disgust, grief or anger. If madness comes, it may be among the necessary things, but it is unlikely in the good man; and if each soul's rank hereafter depends on how it leaves, one should not leave while progress is still possible.

@ch I.9.1 | Do not lead the soul out
"You shall not lead it out, lest it go out having something": a soul forced out goes with something, and going out means moving to another place. Let it wait until the body leaves it wholly. Freeing oneself by force is done in disgust, grief or anger. Madness may make it necessary, but not chosen for its own sake; drugs do not suit the soul; and if our time is fated, or our rank hereafter depends on how we leave, one should not go before the time, while progress is possible.

@p
"You shall not lead it out, lest it go out having something." For it will go out having something, if it goes out at all; and going out is passing to another place. Rather, it will wait for the body to stand apart from it wholly, when it does not need to move elsewhere, but is wholly outside. How, then, does the body stand apart? When nothing of the soul is bound to it any longer, since the body can no longer bind it, its harmony, by having which it had the soul, being no longer there.
!flag For wheresoever it go, it will be in some definite condition | The Greek says "it will go out having something" (ἔχουσά τι), something of the body, "if it goes out at all": the reason for the opening command, not a condition it will be in.
!note The opening words are quoted in later antiquity as an oracle (Psellus gives them among the Chaldaean Oracles, fragment 166 in des Places' edition). The prohibition of suicide is the Phaedo's (61c-62c): we are in a kind of guard post, and must not release ourselves. The harmony of the body is the Phaedo's too (85e-86d), though there it is the soul that Simmias calls a harmony.

@p
@greek τί οὖν, εἰ μηχανήσαιτό
What, then, if someone contrived that the body be undone? He used force, and stood apart himself; the body did not let the soul go. And when he undoes it, he is not unaffected: there is disgust, or grief, or anger; but one must do nothing. What, then, if he perceives the beginning of madness? Perhaps that does not happen to the good man; but if it did, this would be ranked among the necessary things, chosen because of circumstances, not chosen simply. For taking drugs to send the soul out is perhaps not suitable to the soul either.
!flag movements which it is unlawful to indulge | The Greek says only "but one must do nothing" (δεῖ δὲ μηδὲν πράττειν): nothing is done that would come from these feelings. There is no law in the Greek.
!flag That is not likely in the Sage | The Greek's σπουδαῖος is "the good man", serious about virtue, as elsewhere in the Workshop (see I.4); MacKenna's "Sage" makes him a Stoic figure.
!note "Chosen because of circumstances" (ἐκ περιστάσεως αἱρετοῖς) is Stoic language: the Stoics allowed a "reasonable exit" from life in some circumstances (Diogenes Laertius VII.130), and ranked it among things chosen not for themselves.

@p
@greek καὶ εἰ εἱμαρμένος χρόνος
And if the time given to each is fated, it is not well to go before it, unless, as we say, it is necessary. And if each holds a rank there according to the state in which he goes out, one must not lead the soul out while there is still room to make progress.
!note "There": in the other world. That the soul's lot after death follows the state in which it leaves is the Phaedo's teaching (107c-108c, 113d-114c), and the Republic's myth of Er (614b-621b).

# Ennead II (milestone E7 onward). MacKenna's vol. II (1921) prints the Third Ennead first and then the Second; his
# chapters of II.1 and II.2 are Volkmann's.

@treatise II.1 | On the heaven
Why does the cosmos, which has a body, last for ever, and do the heaven and the stars last as the very things they are, while the things on earth last only in their kind? To say "God wills it" may be true but explains nothing. Plotinus answers with the soul of the All, which holds the body of the cosmos without toil, and with that body itself: nothing flows out of the All, and the heaven is made of a pure fire, a light, that has no reason to flow away and needs no food. Along the way he weighs the Timaeus on what the stars are made of, and Aristotle's fifth body.

@ch II.1.1 | Lasting for ever: as this thing, or only in kind?
We say the cosmos always was and always will be, though it has a body. To refer this to God's will may be true, but explains nothing. Perhaps the All lasts only as living things on earth do, the same in form while its matter changes; then the heaven would differ from the earth only in lasting longer. If instead the heaven and the stars last as particular things, we must show that God's will can do this, why some things last so and others not, and how the parts of the heaven can.

@p
We say that the cosmos, though it has a body, always was before and always will be. If we trace the cause back to the will of God, we would perhaps be saying something true, but we would make nothing clear. For the elements change, and the living things on earth perish while their form is kept; perhaps, then, someone will claim that it happens in the same way with the All: that the will is able, while the body is always slipping away and flowing, to put the same form on one body after another, so that what is kept for ever is not one in number but one in form. Otherwise, why should the things here have their "always" only by their form, while the things in the heaven, and the heaven itself, have their "always" as this particular thing?
!flag however true an explanation, is utterly inadequate | The Greek says only that this would "perhaps" (ἴσως) be true, but would give no clarity (σαφήνεια): the complaint is that it explains nothing, not that it is "utterly inadequate".
!flag The Will of God is able to cope with the ceaseless flux | In the Greek this is part of what "someone will perhaps claim" (μήποτε ... ἀξιώσει), a view Plotinus puts forward to test; MacKenna states it as fact.
!note "One in number", "one in form": Aristotle's distinction (Metaphysics V.6, 1016b31-1017a3). The living things on earth last as a kind, man and horse, while each man and each horse perishes; the question is whether the heaven lasts in the other way, as this very sun and these very stars.

@p
@greek εἰ δὲ τῷ πάντα συνειληφέναι
Suppose we give as the cause of its not perishing that it has taken everything into itself, so that there is nothing into which it could change, and nothing from outside could fall on it and destroy it. Then by this argument we will grant to the whole, the All, that it cannot perish. But the sun, and the being of the other stars, since each is a part and not a whole and all, will not get from this argument any assurance that it will last for all time; they would seem to have only the lasting in form, as fire and such things have. And so too with the whole cosmos itself. For nothing prevents it, though it is not destroyed by anything else from outside, from having its parts destroy one another, and so, always perishing, from lasting only in its form: the nature of what underlies it would always be flowing, and something else would give it the form, so that the same would happen with the whole living being as happens with a man, a horse and the rest. There is always a man and a horse, but not the same one.

@p
@greek οὐ τοίνυν ἔσται τὸ μὲν μένον
Then there will not be one part of it that stays for ever, the heaven, and another that perishes, the things about the earth; all will be like one another, differing only in time, since the things in the heaven will last longer. If, then, we agreed that "always" holds in this sense of the All and of its parts, the view would carry less difficulty; or rather we would be wholly free of difficulty, if it were shown that the will of God is enough to hold the All together even in this way and in this manner. But if we say that some particular thing in it, of whatever size, has the "always" as itself, then we must show whether the will is able to do this; and the difficulty remains why some things are so and others are not so but last only in their form; and how the parts in the heaven are so as well. For in that case all the parts too would be so.
!flag by such communication | The Greek says "even in this way and in this manner" (κἂν οὕτω καὶ τοῦτον τὸν τρόπον); there is no word for "communication".

@ch II.1.2 | The heaven's body: Plato, Heraclitus and Aristotle's fifth body
If the heaven lasts as this very thing, we must show how a body can, when body always flows, as Plato and the others who wrote on nature held, and Heraclitus said even of the sun. Aristotle, with his fifth body, has no difficulty; but for those who make the heaven of the same elements as the living things here, the cause must be in the soul, or the body, or both; and one who puts it in the soul must show that the body does not work against it.

@p
Suppose, then, we accept this view, and say that the heaven and everything in it have the "always" as particular things, while the things below the sphere of the moon have it by their form. Then we must show how the heaven, having a body, can keep its "this" in the strict sense, so that each thing stays always the same, when the nature of body is always flowing. For this is what the others who have spoken about nature think, and Plato himself, not only about the other bodies but about the heavenly ones as well. "For how," he says, "can things that have bodies and are seen stay always the same without any change?" He plainly agrees on these too with Heraclitus, who said that even the sun is always coming to be.
!note Plato's words are from the Republic VII (530a-b): the true astronomer will think it absurd that the heavenly bodies, "having bodies and being seen", should always be the same and never change. Compare the Statesman (269d-e): to be always the same belongs only to the most divine things, and the nature of body is not of that order.
!note Heraclitus' saying is reported by Aristotle (Meteorology II.2, 355a13-14): "the sun is new each day" (Heraclitus fragment 6, Diels-Kranz).

@p
@greek Ἀριστοτέλει μὲν γὰρ
For Aristotle there would be no difficulty, if one accepted his hypotheses about the fifth body. But for those who do not posit it, and who make the body of the heaven out of the same things as the living things here, how could it keep its "this" for ever? And still more, how could the sun and the things in the heaven, which are parts? Every living being is put together out of soul and the nature of body; so the heaven, if it is to last for ever as one in number, must do so either through both, or through one of the two that are in it, soul or body. Whoever gives incorruptibility to the body would need nothing from the soul for this, except that the two should always be together to make up a living being. But whoever says that body is perishable of itself and gives the cause to the soul must try to show that the state of the body too does not oppose the frame and its lasting: that in things put together by nature nothing is out of tune, and that the matter too must suit the will of the finished work.
!flag no need, then, to call on a soul or on any perdurable conjunction | The Greek says he would need nothing from the soul "except" (ἢ) that the two be always together to make up a living being: the lasting union with soul is the one thing still needed, where MacKenna has it not needed either.
!note Aristotle's fifth body (On the Heavens I.2-3, 268b-270b): the heaven is made of a body other than the four elements, whose natural motion is in a circle and which neither comes to be nor perishes. Plotinus, like the Platonists generally, does not accept it, and makes the heaven out of fire, as the Timaeus does (chapters 6 and 7 below).

@ch II.1.3 | Nothing flows out of the All
Matter helps the cosmos to last because, though it flows, it flows within and not out, so nothing grows old. Earth, water and air keep their bulk; we last a long time though our parts flow away; and the All has nothing outside it. Fire above stays where it is, docile to the soul that carries it round; and if something there flowed away and were replaced, the All would no longer be the same living being.

@p
How, then, can matter and the body of the All, which is always flowing, help the cosmos to be immortal? Our answer would be: it flows, yes, but it does not flow out. If, then, it moves within itself and not away from itself, staying the same, it would neither grow nor waste; and so it does not grow old either. We should look at the earth too, which stays in the same shape and bulk from all time; air never runs short, nor does the nature of water; and so whatever part of them changes has not altered the nature of the whole living being. For in us too, though our parts are always changing and going away outside, each of us lasts a long time; and for that which has nothing outside it, the nature of body would not be out of tune with soul so as to keep it from being the same living being and lasting for ever.
!note "Does not grow old": the Timaeus (33a) makes the cosmos "without age and without disease", since there is nothing outside it to attack it.

@p
@greek πῦρ δὲ ὀξὺ μὲν
Fire is sharp and quick because it does not stay here below, just as earth is what it is because it does not stay above. But when fire has come to where it must stand, we must surely not think that, settled in its own place, it is in such a state as not to seek, like the other elements, to stretch out both ways. Higher it could not be carried, for there is nothing beyond; downward is not its nature. What is left for it is to be easily led and, drawn by a natural pull from the soul towards living, to move in a very fair place, in the soul. And if anyone fears that it may fall, he should take heart: the soul's carrying it round comes before any leaning down, so that the soul, being stronger, holds it up. And if it has no downward pull of its own, it stays without resisting.
!flag co-operates by its upward tendency as earth by its lingering below | The Greek says only that fire is "sharp and quick" because it does not stay down here, as earth is because it does not stay above; there is no word for "co-operates".
!note "In the soul": the body of the cosmos is in its soul rather than the soul in it; the Timaeus (36d-e) has the soul woven through the body of the All and wrapped around it from outside.

@p
@greek τὰ μὲν οὖν ἡμέτερα μέρη
Our parts, once they have taken shape, cannot keep their own frame together, and need particles from other things in order to last. But if nothing flows away from there, there is no need of food. If something did flow away from there, being quenched, another fire would have to be kindled; and if the heaven also had some other element and that flowed away from there, that too would need to be replaced by another. But then, even if it lasted in this way, the All would not stay the same living being.
!flag occur in some of the superiors from which the celestial fire depends | The Greek says "if it had something else" (εἰ ἄλλου τινὸς ἔχοι), some other element besides fire in the heaven, which might flow away too; there are no "superiors" on which the fire depends.

@ch II.1.4 | The soul that holds it, without toil
Whether anything flows from the heaven, and whether it is only fire, needs a separate look. But add the soul, the most sovereign cause, to such pure bodies, and the immortality of the heaven is secure. The soul is set next to the best and, coming from God, is stronger than any bond; what can hold for a time can hold for ever. The cosmos never began, so it will not end; its governing costs the soul no toil, so it will not change its mind.

@p
But this must be looked at by itself, and not as part of the present question: whether something flows away from there, so that the things there too need what is called, not in the strict sense, food; or whether the things there, once set in order, stay as nature made them and suffer no outflow. And whether there is fire only there, or mostly fire, or whether the other elements too can be held up and lifted aloft by what masters them. For if one added the most sovereign cause, the soul, to bodies so pure and in every way better (in the other living beings too, nature picks out the better things for their sovereign parts), one would have a firm opinion about the immortality of the heaven. For Aristotle too is right to call flame a kind of boiling, a fire that runs riot, as it were, from surfeit; but the fire there is even and calm, and suits the nature of the stars.
!flag docile to the purposes of the stars | The Greek says the fire there "suits the nature of the stars" (τῇ τῶν ἄστρων πρόσφορον φύσει); there is nothing about their "purposes".
!note Aristotle calls flame a boiling (ζέσις) of dry exhalation (Meteorology I.3, 340b). "Runs riot from surfeit" recalls the old saying that surfeit breeds insolence (κόρος ὕβριν τίκτει, Theognis 153; Solon).

@p
@greek τὸ δὲ δὴ μέγιστον
But the greatest thing is this: the soul, set next to the best things, with a wonderful power, how could anything once placed in it escape from it into not being? Not to think that the soul, having set out from God, is stronger than any bond belongs to people who have no experience of the cause that holds all things together. For it is absurd that what was able to hold things together for any time at all should not do so for ever, as if holding together were done by force, and what is natural were something other than what is in the nature of the All and in things well placed; or as if there were something that would use force and dissolve the frame, and overthrow the nature of soul like a kingdom or a rule.
!flag moving in its marvellous might | The Greek says the soul is "set" or "lies" (κειμένην) next to the best things, with a wonderful power; it is not said to move.
!note "Stronger than any bond": in the Timaeus (41a-b) the maker tells the gods he has made that they will not be undone, since they have his will, "a greater and more sovereign bond" than those they were bound with when they came to be.

@p
@greek τό τε μήποτε ἄρξασθαι
And that it never began (the opposite is absurd, and has been said already) gives assurance about the future too. For why should it perish at some time to come, and not already? The elements are not worn away like pieces of wood and such things; and since they stay always, the All stays too. And even if they always change, the All stays, for the cause of the change stays too. That a change of mind in the soul is an empty idea has been shown, since its governing is without toil and without harm; and even if it were possible for every body to perish, nothing would be any different for the soul.
!note Aristotle (On the Heavens II.1, 284a) argued that a heaven held in its motion by a soul against its nature would make the soul's life toilsome, like Ixion bound to his wheel. Plotinus answers that the soul's governing is "without toil" (ἄπονος), and comes back to it in II.2.1.

@ch II.1.5 | Why the heaven lasts and we do not
Plato says the heaven came from God himself, the living things here from the gods he made, and what comes from God may not perish. That is, the heavenly soul is next after the maker, while the living things on earth are made by an image of it, working in worse bodies and a worse place. The heaven must last with its parts, the stars; it ends at the moon. We are shaped by the soul given by the heavenly gods; the other soul in us is the cause of our being well, not of our being.

@p
How then do the parts there last, while the elements and living things here do not? Because, Plato says, the things there came into being from God, while the living things here came from the gods who came into being from him; and what has come from him may not perish. This is the same as saying that the heavenly soul comes next after the maker, and our souls too; and that an image of it, going out from the heavenly soul and as it were flowing down from above, makes the living things on earth.
!note The Timaeus (41a-d): the maker tells the gods he has made that what he has made will not be undone unless he wills it, and gives them the making of the mortal living things, so that these should not be equal to gods. The gods are the stars and the heaven; "may not" (οὐ θεμιτόν) is "it is not right", "it is not lawful".
!note "The maker" (ὁ δημιουργός, the craftsman) is the Timaeus' maker of the cosmos.

@p
@greek ψυχῆς οὖν μιμουμένης
Since a soul of this kind imitates the soul there, but lacks the power, because it must use worse bodies for its making, and in a worse place, while the things taken up into the frame are not willing to stay, the living things here cannot stay for ever, and the bodies would not be mastered in the same way, as they would be if the other soul ruled them directly. But if the whole heaven was to stay, its parts, the stars in it, had to stay too; or how could it have stayed if they did not stay like it? For the things under the heaven are no longer parts of the heaven; otherwise the heaven would not end at the moon.
!flag whose region does not in fact extend so low as to the moon | The Greek says the opposite: the things under the heaven are not parts of it, "or else the heaven would not reach (only) as far as the moon" (ἢ οὐ μέχρι σελήνης ὁ οὐρανός). The heaven ends at the moon; what is below it is not the heaven.

@p
@greek ἡμεῖς δὲ πλασθέντες
We are shaped by the soul given from the gods in the heaven, and from the heaven itself, and it is in accordance with that soul that we are joined to our bodies. For the other soul, the one by which we are we, is the cause of our being well, not of our being. In fact it comes when the body has already come to be, helping a little, from reasoning, towards its being.
!flag bringing with it some effluence from the Divine Reason | The Greek says the higher soul comes when the body is already formed, "helping a little, from reasoning" (μικρὰ ἐκ λογισμοῦ), towards its being; there is no "effluence" and no "Divine Reason", only our own reasoning.
!note In the Timaeus (42d-43a, 69c-d) the gods made by the maker take the immortal principle of the soul from him and build around it the body and a mortal kind of soul. "Being" and "being well": the lower soul gives us life, the higher a good life.

@ch II.1.6 | What are the stars made of?
Is there only fire in the heaven? The Timaeus made the body of the All of fire, to be seen, and earth, to be solid, and so the stars mostly of fire with some earth. But why water and air there? Plotinus questions whether each element needs the others: then nothing would be itself. Light, not fire, makes things visible; fire can have resistance, and a frame given by the soul, without earth; there are living beings of fire. Earth cannot be lifted into the heaven.

@p
But we must now look at whether there is only fire there, and whether something flows away from there and needs food. Timaeus made the body of the All first of earth and fire, so that it would be visible because of the fire and solid because of the earth; and it seemed to follow that he made the stars not wholly but mostly of fire, since the stars plainly have solidity. And perhaps this would be right, since Plato too judges this view to be the likely one. To perception, both to sight and to the grasp of touch, they appear to have mostly or wholly fire; but for those who look into it by reason, if solidity could not come about without earth, they would have earth too.
!flag Plato accepts it as indicated by all the appearances | The Greek says Plato judged the view by "the likely" (τῷ εἰκότι), the "likely account" of the Timaeus, not by "all the appearances". The appearances come next, and lead the other way.
!flag derive from them the impression of illumination | The Greek names sight and "the grasp of touch" (τὴν τῆς ἁφῆς ἀντίληψιν): to both senses the stars seem made of fire. MacKenna leaves out touch.
!note The Timaeus (31b): "what comes to be must be bodily, visible and tangible; nothing could be visible without fire, nor tangible without something solid, nor solid without earth." The stars are made "mostly of fire" (40a). Timaeus is the speaker in Plato's dialogue.

@p
@greek ὕδατος δὲ καὶ ἀέρος
But what need would they have of water and air? It would seem absurd for water to be in so much fire, and air, if it were there, would change into the nature of fire. But if two solids standing as extremes need two means, one might ask whether this holds of natural things too; for one could mix earth with water without needing any mean. If we said "the others are already present in the earth and the water," we would perhaps be saying something; but someone might reply, "but not so as to bind the two when they come together." Still, we will say that they are already bound, by each of them having all.
!note The Timaeus (32a-b): two plane figures are bound by one mean, but solids always need two; so the maker set water and air between fire and earth, in proportion.

@p
@greek ἀλλ’ ἐπισκεπτέον
But we must look at whether earth is not visible without fire, and fire not solid without earth. If that were so, perhaps nothing would have its own being by itself, but all would be mixed, and each would be named by what prevails in it. For they say that not even earth can hold together without moisture, since the wetness of water is earth's glue. But even if we grant this, it is absurd to say that each is something by itself and yet not give it a frame of its own, but only together with the others, when none of them is anything on its own. How could there be a nature of earth, and the "what it was to be" of earth, if there were no portion of earth that is earth, unless water were present to glue it? And what would the water glue, if there were no magnitude at all for it to join to another portion so as to make them continuous? For if there is any magnitude of earth itself, however small, earth will be able to exist by nature even without water; or, if not, there will be nothing to be glued by the water. And what need would a mass of earth have of air in order to be, while the air is still air, before it changes?
!flag It is still not proven that fire is necessary to the visibility of earth | The Greek is a question to be looked into (ἐπισκεπτέον, "we must look at whether"), and its point is the next sentence: if each element needed the others, nothing would have a being of its own.
!note "The what it was to be" (τὸ τί ἦν εἶναι) is Aristotle's phrase for what a thing is in itself, its essence.

@p
@greek περὶ δὲ πυρὸς εἰς μὲν
About fire, it was not said that it is needed for earth to be earth, but for earth and the others to be visible; for it is reasonable to grant that being seen comes from light. We should not say that darkness is seen, but that it is not seen, as silence is not heard. But fire need not be present in earth: light is enough. Snow, at any rate, and many very cold things are bright without fire. "But fire was in them," someone will say, "and coloured them before it left."

@p
@greek ἀλλ’ ἐνεγένετο
And about water too we must ask whether there is no water unless it takes some earth. And how could air, which crumbles so easily, be said to share in earth? And about fire: does it need earth, since of itself it has no continuity, nor the three dimensions? But solidity, taken not as the three dimensions but plainly as resistance, why should fire not have it, as a natural body? Hardness belongs to earth alone. For density comes to gold, which is water, not because earth is added, but because of density or freezing. And why should fire by itself not take a frame, when soul is present, by the soul's power? There are living beings of fire among the daemons.
!flag there are living beings of fire among the Celestials | The Greek says "among the daemons" (δαιμόνων): MacKenna's "Celestials" makes them gods of the heaven.
!note Gold is "a kind of water": the Timaeus (59b) counts gold among the fusible waters, the densest of them, frozen solid.
!note In the Timaeus (40a) the kind of the gods, the stars, is made mostly of fire; the daemons, in the Platonists' reading, have bodies of air or of fire.

@p
@greek ἀλλὰ κινήσομεν
But shall we then overturn the view that the whole living being has its frame from all the elements? One will say that this holds of the things on earth; but to lift earth into the heaven is against nature and contrary to what nature has ordered; and that the swiftest motion should carry earthy bodies round with it is not believable; and earth would hinder the shining whiteness of the fire there.

@ch II.1.7 | Plato's light: the stars are of a fire that is light
Better to read Plato this way: the cosmos needs resistance, so that the earth may be a firm footing for the living beings on it; the elements share in one another's qualities, not by being mixed, but by their fellowship in the cosmos. The sun is a light kindled by God, bright and white, a gentle fire that is light, not flame; our flame cannot reach it and dies below the moon. The light of the stars gives them their sizes and colours; the rest of the heaven is of such light too, but is too fine to be seen.

@p
Perhaps, then, we should listen better to Plato, who says that in the whole cosmos there must be something solid of this kind, that is, resistant, so that the earth, set in the middle, may be a footing and a firm base for those that walk on it, and so that the living beings on it may by necessity have such solidity from the earth. The earth would have its continuity of itself, but would be lit up by fire; it would share in water so as not to be parched, and so that its parts are not hindered from coming together; and air would lighten the masses of earth. The earth would be mixed with the fire above not in the making of the stars; but since each element is in the cosmos, the fire too gets some benefit from the earth, as the earth does from the fire, and each from each. It is not that what benefits comes to be made of both, of itself and of what it shares in; but by their fellowship in the cosmos, staying what it is, it takes not the other itself but something of it: not air, but the softness of air, and of fire its brightness. Mixing would give everything, and would then make something composed of both, not earth alone, with this solidity and density, together with the nature of fire.
!note The earth "in the middle" as our nurse, and the guardian of night and day: the Timaeus (40b-c).

@p
@greek μαρτυρεῖ δὲ καὶ αὐτὸς
Plato himself bears witness to this, saying "God lit a light in the second circuit from the earth," meaning the sun; and elsewhere he calls the sun most bright, and the same one most white, leading us away from thinking it anything other than fire; and a fire of neither of its other kinds, but light, which he says is other than flame, and only gently warm. This light is a body; and from it shines out the light that has the same name, which we say is bodiless: this comes from that first light, shining out from it like its flower and gleam, and that first light is what is truly the "white body". We take "earthy" in the worse sense, while Plato took earth with regard to solidity: we call one thing earth, while he sets out differences of earth.
!note "God lit a light": the Timaeus (39b), of the sun. On the kinds of fire, the Timaeus (58c-d) names flame, light that does not burn but gives light to the eyes, and the glow left in embers; the fire of the eyes is gentle and does not burn (45b). In the myth of Er (Republic X, 616e) the sun's circle is the brightest.
!note "That first light is what is truly the white body": the Greek's "which" (ὃ) may go back to the bodily light or to the bodiless gleam that shines from it; MacKenna's "the veritable incandescent body" leaves it open too. The modern version takes the bodily light, since it is the body.
!note The kinds of earth: the Timaeus (60b-61c) sets out several, stone, clay, glass and others.

@p
@greek τοῦ δὴ τοιούτου πυρὸς
Since a fire of this kind, which gives light, the purest, lies in the upper place and is settled there by nature, we must not suppose that this flame of ours mixes with the things there. Rather, reaching up to a certain point, it is quenched when it meets more air; going up with earth, it is thrown down, unable to pass beyond to what is above, and stops below the moon, so that it makes the air there finer; and the flame, if it lasts, dies down and becomes gentler, and has no brightness fit for boiling, but only as much as it is lit by the light above. And the light there, varied by ratios in the stars, makes the difference among them, as in their sizes, so also in their colours; and the rest of the heaven too is of such light, but is not seen, because of the fineness of its body and its transparency, which offers no resistance, as pure air is not seen; and besides, because it is so far away.
!flag extinguished by the currents of air opposed to it | The Greek says the flame is quenched when it "meets more air" (ἐντυχοῦσαν πλείονι ἀέρι); nothing is said of currents opposing it.
!flag constitutes also the still higher heavenly bodies | The Greek says "the rest of the heaven" (τὸν δ’ ἄλλον οὐρανόν), the heaven between the stars, is of this light too, and is not seen; there are no "still higher heavenly bodies".

@ch II.1.8 | No outflow, no food: the heaven needs nothing else
Such light, pure in the purest place, has no outflow: it does not flow down by nature, and nothing there forces it; a body with soul is not as a body alone. What borders on it, air or fire, cannot harm it. So the heaven needs no other body to last or to move in a circle, which is its nature; it needs no food. The bodies here change because the nature that governs them is weak and imitates in becoming what is before it.

@p
If such light stays above, where it has been set, pure in what is purest, what kind of outflow from it could there be? Such a nature does not flow downward, nor again is there anything there that forces it and pushes it down. And every body with soul is something other, not the same as it would be if it were alone; and the body there is of this kind, not like a body alone. And as for what borders on it, whether it is air or fire: what could air do to it? And fire would not be fitted to act on it, nor could it touch it so as to do anything: in its rush it would pass by before that body was affected; and it is lesser, and not as strong as the fire down here. Then too, to act is to heat; but what is to be heated must not be hot of itself.
!flag no match for what it would be opposing in those higher regions | The Greek compares that fire with the fire down here: it is "lesser, and not as strong as the things here" (ἰσχύον τε οὐκ ἴσα τοῖς ἐνθάδε), since flame dies down as it rises (chapter 7).

@p
@greek εἰ δέ τι φθαρήσεται
And if anything is to be destroyed by fire, it must first be heated, and be put against its nature by being heated. So the heaven needs no other body in order to last, nor in order that its circling be according to nature; for it has not yet been shown that its natural motion is in a straight line. For them, either to stay or to go round is according to nature; the other motions belong to things under force. So we must not say that the things there need food, nor make claims about them from the things here, which neither have the same soul holding them together, nor the same place; nor is there there the cause through which the compounds here, always flowing, are fed. The change of the bodies here is a changing away from themselves, since another nature presides over them, which from weakness does not know how to keep them in being, but imitates the nature before it in coming to be and in begetting. That the things there are not in every way the same, as the intelligibles are, has been said.
!flag a Principle not dwelling in the higher regions | The Greek says only "another nature presiding over them" (ἄλλης ἐπιστατούσης φύσεως); where it dwells is MacKenna's gloss.
!note Straight and circular motion: Aristotle (On the Heavens I.2, 268b-269a) makes circular motion natural to the heaven and straight motion to the four elements; Plotinus takes up the heaven's circling in the next treatise.

@treatise II.2 | On the movement of the heaven
Why does the heaven move in a circle? Because it imitates intellect. Its motion belongs first to its soul, whose motion is not in place but a turning towards itself, an awareness and a life; the body, which would go straight, is led round by the soul, which is everywhere whole, so that the heaven, seeking it, is always going round and always reaching it. The soul circles around God, as the stars do, each in its place; our bodies cannot, being earthy. Last, a lower power of soul woven through the All turns towards the higher one in the spheres, and so the All moves and stands still at once, like intellect.

@ch II.2.1 | Why in a circle?
The heaven moves in a circle because it imitates intellect. Its soul's own motion is not in place but towards itself, an awareness, a thinking and a life, embracing all; the body would go straight, the soul holds it, and so it goes round. Fire, once it reaches the edge of the All, has nowhere further to go and runs in a circle in its own place. The soul does not tire in carrying it; being everywhere whole, it makes the heaven go everywhere, seeking it and always having it.

@p
Why does it move in a circle? Because it imitates intellect. And whose is the motion, the soul's or the body's? The soul's. Why, then? Because the soul is in itself and always hastens to go towards itself? Or because, though it is in itself, it is not continuous? Or because, as it is carried, it carries the body with it? But then, carrying it along, it should no longer be carrying it, but have carried it, that is, it should rather have made it stand still, and not go always in a circle. Or the soul itself too will stand still, or, if it moves, not in place. How then does it move the body in place, when it moves in another way itself? Or perhaps the circular motion is not motion in place either, except incidentally.
!flag being self-centred it is not of unlimited extension (and consequently must move ceaselessly to be omnipresent) | A hard place. The Greek says only "or is it in itself, not being continuous?" (ἢ ἔστιν ἐν αὑτῇ οὐ συνεχὴς οὖσα;); MacKenna's "unlimited extension" and his words in brackets are his reading of it.
!note "Because it imitates intellect": the Timaeus (34a) gives the cosmos, of the seven motions, the one "most about intellect and wisdom", turning in the same place; the Laws (X, 897c-898b) likens the motion of intellect to a sphere turning on a lathe.

@p
@greek ποία οὖν τις;
What kind of motion is it, then? A motion towards itself, of awareness, of thinking with itself, and of life; nowhere outside and nowhere else, since it must take in everything. For the ruling part of a living being is what takes it in and makes it one. But the soul will not take the body in as a living thing does, if it stays still, nor will it keep safe what is inside, since that has a body; for the life of a body is motion. If, then, the motion is also in place, the heaven will move as it can, and not as soul alone, but as an ensouled body and as a living being; so that the motion is a mixture of bodily and soul motion: the body carried straight by its nature, the soul holding it back, and from both comes the motion of something carried and staying at once.

@p
@greek εἰ δὲ σώματος ἡ κύκλῳ
But if the circular motion is said to be the body's, how can it be, when every body goes straight, fire too? Or does it go straight until it reaches the place where it has been set? For as a thing is set, so, it seems, it both stands by nature and is carried to where it was set. Why, then, does fire not stay when it has come there? Is it because the nature of fire is in motion? If, then, it did not go in a circle, it would be scattered in a straight line; so it must go in a circle. But this is the work of providence; or rather, it is in fire from providence, so that, if it comes there, it moves in a circle of itself. Or again: aiming at the straight line and no longer having any place, it slides round, as it were, and bends back in the places where it can; for it has no place beyond itself, since this is the last. So it runs in the place it has, and is itself its own place, not so as to stay there when it has come, but so as to move.
!flag its straight line would finally fling it outside the universe | The Greek says only that fire "will be scattered" (σκεδασθήσεται) in a straight line; "outside the universe" is MacKenna's (Plotinus says next that there is no place beyond).
!flag itself its own sphere | The Greek says "itself its own place" (αὐτὸ αὑτοῦ τόπος).

@p
@greek καὶ κύκλου δὲ τὸ μὲν κέντρον
And of a circle, the centre stays by nature; but if the circumference outside stayed, it would be one big centre. So motion around the centre suits all the more a body that is alive and in its natural state. For so it will incline together towards the centre, not by sinking into it (that would destroy the circle), but, since it cannot do that, by whirling round it; only in this way will it fulfil its desire. And if the soul carries it round, it will not grow tired: for it does not drag it, nor move it against nature, since nature is what has been ordered by the whole soul. Further, since the soul of the All is everywhere whole and not divided into parts, it gives the heaven too, as far as the heaven can, to be everywhere; and it can by passing over all things and going through them. If the soul were standing still somewhere, the heaven would have stopped when it came there; but as it is, since the soul is whole everywhere, the heaven longs for all of it. What then? Will it never reach it? Rather, it always reaches it in this way; or rather, the soul, always leading it towards itself, in always leading it always moves it; and moving it not to somewhere else but towards itself, in the same place, leading it not in a straight line but in a circle, it gives it to have the soul wherever it comes. If the soul stayed, as being only there where each thing stays, the heaven would stand still. Since, then, the soul is not only in one place, wherever that may be, the heaven will be carried everywhere and not outside: in a circle, then.
!flag a living thing whose nature binds it within a body | The Greek says "a body that is alive and in its natural state" (ζῶντι καὶ κατὰ φύσιν δὲ ἔχοντι σώματι); nothing binds a living thing within a body.
!flag the custom the All-Soul has established | The Greek says nature is "what has been ordered by the whole soul" (τὸ ὑπὸ ψυχῆς τῆς πάσης ταχθέν): an ordering, not a custom.
!flag dwelt wholly and solely in the realm in which every member is at rest | The Greek "as being only there where each thing stays" (ὡς ἐκεῖ οὔσης μόνον, οὗ ἕκαστον μένει) means a place in the heaven, not the intelligible realm; MacKenna reads "there" (ἐκεῖ) as Plotinus' "There", the world above.
!note "It will not grow tired": the answer to Aristotle (On the Heavens II.1, 284a), who said that a soul forcing the heaven round would have a toilsome life. See II.1.4.

@ch II.2.2 | The soul circles around God
The parts of the All are parts, held in partial places; the heaven is the whole, and nothing hinders it. It goes round because it has more than the soul wherever it is. The centre of soul is not a place but what it comes from; so the soul goes round God, embracing him in love, since it cannot be at him. Every soul does so in its place; our bodies cannot, being earthy and bent on other things, though perhaps the breath about our soul does. In Plato the stars too turn each about its own centre, rejoicing in God.

@p
How, then, about the other things? Each of them is not a whole but a part, held in a partial place. That one is a whole and, as it were, a place, and nothing hinders it, for it is itself the All. How then about human beings? As far as they are from the All, a part; as far as they are themselves, a whole of their own. If, then, wherever it is, the heaven has the soul, why must it go round? Because it does not have only the soul.
!flag (which it desires to possess alone) | A hard place: the Greek says only "because it does not have it alone" (ὅτι μὴ μόνον ἔχει), which may mean that it has other things besides the soul, or that it does not have the soul in one place only. MacKenna's words in brackets are his.

@p
@greek εἰ δὲ ἡ δύναμις αὐτῆς
And if the soul's power is about the centre, it would go in a circle in this way too. But the centre must not be taken in the same way for a body and for the nature of soul: there, the centre is that from which the other nature comes; for a body, it is the centre in place. So we must take the centre by analogy: as there, so here too there must be a centre, which alone is properly the centre of a spherical body; for as that one is around itself, so is this one. If, then, the soul runs around God, it embraces him lovingly, and holds itself around him as far as it can; for all things hang from him. Since, then, it cannot be at him, it is around him.
!note "Embraces him lovingly" (ἀμφαγαπάζεται) is a word of Homer's (Iliad 16.192). Its first part, ἀμφί, means "around": the word itself says that the soul loves by going around.
!note "All things hang from him": Aristotle says of the first mover that "the heaven and nature hang from such a principle" (Metaphysics XII.7, 1072b13-14).

@p
@greek πῶς οὖν οὐ πᾶσαι οὕτως;
Why then are not all souls like this? Each is like this where it is. Why then are not our bodies like this too? Because straight motion is attached to them, and their impulses are towards other things, and what is spherical in us does not run easily, since it is earthy; while there, what follows along is fine and easily moved. For why should it stop, whatever motion the soul moves with? Perhaps with us too the breath about the soul does the same. For if God is in all things, the soul that wishes to be with him must come to be around him, for he is not anywhere in particular. And Plato gives the stars not only the spherical motion with the whole, but also to each its motion around its own centre: for each, where it is, has embraced God and rejoices, not by reasoning but by natural necessities.
!flag what in us is of this circling nature (the soul) | The Greek says "what is spherical in us" (τὸ σφαιροειδὲς ἡμῶν), which is earthy: the head, which the Timaeus (44d) makes spherical in imitation of the All. It is a part of the body, not the soul.
!flag the Spirit which dwells with the Soul does thus circle about the divinity | The Greek πνεῦμα here is the "breath", the fine body around the soul that the later Platonists call its vehicle; MacKenna's capital "Spirit" can be read as something higher.
!flag to each a revolution around their common centre | The Greek gives each star a motion "around their own centre" (τὴν περὶ τὸ κέντρον αὐτῶν), each turning in its own place, as in the Timaeus (40a-b); not around a common centre.
!note The Timaeus (40a-b) gives each star two motions: one turning in the same place, "always thinking the same thoughts about the same things", and one forward, carried by the revolution of the Same.

@ch II.2.3 | The soul's lower and higher powers; moving and standing still
Another way to put it: the soul's last power begins from the earth and is woven through the whole; a higher power, which perceives and holds opinion, is above in the spheres, riding on the lower and making it more alive. The lower turns towards the higher that surrounds it, and its turning carries round the body, as our bodies move when the soul is moved by joy. Intellect moves by standing still, around itself; so the All, moving in a circle, also stands still.

@p
Let it be put this way too. One power of the soul, the last, begins from the earth and is woven through the whole; another, whose nature is to perceive, and which receives the reason that forms opinions, holds itself above, in the spheres, riding on the first and giving it from itself power to make it more alive. So the first is moved by it, as it surrounds it in a circle and is settled upon all of it that has run up to the spheres. As that one surrounds it in a circle, it inclines together with it and turns towards it, and its turning carries round the body in which it is woven. For if any part in a sphere is moved in any way, if it moves while staying where it is, it shakes what it is in, and the sphere gets motion.
!flag another phase possesses sensation, while yet another includes the Reason | A hard place: MacKenna counts three powers. The Greek "the one whose nature is to perceive and the one that receives the reason that forms opinions" (ἡ δὲ αἰσθάνεσθαι πεφυκυῖα καὶ ἡ λόγον δοξαστικὸν δεχομένη) goes with one singular verb and "itself" (ἑαυτήν), and the chapter goes on with two: the lower power woven through the All, and the perceiving power above.
!flag this upward tension | The Greek says "its turning" (ἡ δὲ ἐπιστροφὴ αὐτῆς), Plotinus' word for the soul's turning back to what is above it.
!note "Woven through the whole": in the Timaeus (36e) the soul is "woven through everywhere from the centre to the outermost heaven".

@p
@greek καὶ γὰρ ἐπὶ τῶν σωμάτων
For with our bodies too, when the soul moves in another way, as in joys and at the sight of something good, the body's motion becomes motion in place. So there, the soul, coming to be in the good and becoming more perceptive, moves towards the good, and shakes the body, as is natural there, in place. And the perceiving power, receiving the good in its turn from above, and delighting in what is its own, pursues it, and since the good is everywhere, it is carried towards the everywhere. Intellect does not move in this way; for it both stands still and moves, since it moves around itself. So too the All, by moving in a circle, at the same time also stands still.
!note "Stands still and moves": Plato's Sophist (248e-249d, 254d-255b) gives motion and rest together to what fully is. The text has οὔπω ("not yet", "in no way"), which the modern version takes as "not in this way".

@treatise II.3 | Whether the stars are causes
The astrologers say that the planets make everything, wealth and poverty, beauty and ugliness, even vices and virtues, and that they are glad or angry by their places and figures and as they see one another. Plotinus takes these claims apart: soulless stars could give only heat, and ensouled ones, divine and happy, have no reason to harm us, nor do places and aspects change their minds. The stars do signify, as letters written in the sky, because the All is one living being whose parts all answer to each other; they act only in what they plainly do. Soul is double in us and in the All: the soul that has been separated is free, and only the composite lives under destiny. Then what comes from the stars changes on its way down; the outside world adds a quality, it does not make the thing; and each event has its own cause, in us, in others or in the All.
