Last, soul governs the All by forming principles, its lowest power makes things as a figure is made in water, and evils too have their use: the cosmos is an image always being imaged.

@ch II.3.1 | What the astrologers claim
That the stars signify what will happen but do not do everything has been said before; it must now be said more exactly. The astrologers say that the planets make not only wealth and health but beauty, vices, virtues and the acts that come from them, as if they were angry with us; that they are glad or troubled by their places, good or bad by turns, changed by seeing one another and by the figure in which they see; and that all together make a blend unlike any one of them.

@p
That the movement of the stars signifies what will happen to each thing, but does not itself do everything, as most people believe, has been said before elsewhere, and the argument gave some proofs of it. But it must be said now more exactly and at greater length; for it is no small thing to hold one opinion about it or the other.
!note "Said before elsewhere": in On destiny (III.1.5-6), one of Plotinus' first treatises. This one is among his last: Porphyry counts it fifty-second of the fifty-four, written in the months before his death (Life, chapter 6).

@p
@greek τοὺς δὴ πλανήτας
They say that the planets, as they move, make not only the other things, poverty and wealth, health and sickness, but also ugliness and beauty, and, what is most serious, vices and virtues too, and even the actions that come from them, one by one, each on its occasion: as if the planets were angry with human beings over things in which human beings do them no wrong, since it is by the planets that they have been made as they are. And they say that the planets give what are called goods, not because they are pleased with those who receive them, but because they themselves are badly off in some places of their course and well off in others; and that they change their minds, being one way when they are on the centres and another when they are declining.
!flag so that they must be supposed to change their plans as they stand at their zeniths or are declining | The Greek says "on the centres" (ἐπὶ κέντρων), the astrologers' four cardinal points of the sky: where a star rises, culminates, sets, and is lowest under the earth. The zenith is only one of them.
!note "Centres" (κέντρα) and "declining" (ἀποκλίνειν) are words of the astrologers' craft: a planet is strongest on one of the four cardinal points and weaker as it moves away from it. Ptolemy sets them out in his Tetrabiblos (III.10).

@p
@greek τὸ δὲ μέγιστον, τοὺς μὲν κακοὺς
And the greatest thing: they say that some of them are bad and others good, and yet that the ones they call bad give goods, and the good ones become harmful. Further, that when they see one another they make some things, and when they do not see one another, others, as if they were not themselves, but one kind when they see and another when they do not; that this one is good when it sees that one, but changes if it sees another; and that it sees in one way if its sight is along this figure, in another if along that. And that all of them together make a blend that is different again, as a mixture made from different liquids is different from the things mixed. Since they hold these opinions and others like them, we must examine each and speak about it. A fitting beginning would be this.
!flag so that, after all, they possess no definite nature but vary according to their angles of aspect | The Greek says only "as if they were not themselves" (ὥσπερ οὐχ αὑτῶν ὄντας), but one kind when they see and another when they do not; "no definite nature" and "angles of aspect" are MacKenna's.
!note "Seeing" one another is the astrologers' word for the aspects, the angles between two planets on the zodiac (chapter 4).

@ch II.3.2 | Soulless, or ensouled?
Are the stars soulless or ensouled? If soulless, they can give only heat and cold, bodily things, which mix on earth into one and differ only by nearness; and how could heat make some men scholars and others orators, rich or poor, or give a man such a brother or father, or make him a king? If ensouled and acting by choice, what wrong have we done them, that they should harm us? They are divine, and nothing that makes men bad is in them, and our fortunes do them no good or ill.

@p
Are we to think that these moving bodies are ensouled, or soulless? If they are soulless, they give nothing but heat and cold (if we are to say that some of the stars are cold at all); and so they will confine what they give to the nature of our bodies, since what comes from them to us is plainly a bodily stream. So the change in our bodies would not be great either, since the outflow from each of them is the same, and besides they are mixed together into one on earth; so the only differences would be by place, from being near the stream or far from it. And the cold gives in the same way as the heat.
!flag emanations merely corporeal cannot differ greatly from star to star | The Greek says the outflow from each is "the same" (τῆς ἀπορροῆς ἑκάστων τῆς αὐτῆς οὔσης), not that it cannot differ greatly.

@p
@greek σοφοὺς δὲ καὶ ἀμαθεῖς
But how do they make some men wise and others ignorant, some teachers of letters, others orators, others lyre-players, and those of the other arts, and some rich and others poor? And the other things whose coming to be has its cause in something other than a blend of bodies? For instance, to have such a brother, and father, and son, and wife, to have good fortune at this moment, to become a general or a king?

@p
@greek εἰ δ’ ἔμψυχα ὄντα
If, being ensouled, they act by choice, what wrong have they suffered from us that they should do us harm of their own will, and that when they are set in a divine place and are divine themselves? For what makes human beings bad does not belong to them; nor does anything good or bad come to them at all from our faring well or suffering ill.
!flag suppose the stars to have life and mind and to be effective by deliberate purpose | The Greek says only "being ensouled" (ἔμψυχα ὄντα), acting "by choice"; "mind" is MacKenna's.

@ch II.3.3 | Places and risings cannot change a star's mood
If the stars act under compulsion of their places, the same places should make them all do the same. A star is not in the signs but far below them, and always in the heaven; it is absurd that it should change as it passes each sign, or be glad at a centre and sad as it declines, since each star is at a centre for some places and declining for others at once. The stars are always serene, glad in the good they have and in what they see; their life is their own, not turned towards us. Like birds, they signify incidentally.

@p
But, they say, the stars do these things not of their own will but compelled by their places and figures. But if they are compelled, then surely all of them ought to do the same things when they come into the same places and figures. As it is, what different thing has this planet undergone in passing this segment of the circle of the zodiac, and then that one? For it does not even come into the sign of the zodiac itself, but passes far below it; and whatever sign it comes under, it is in the heaven. For it is absurd that, as it passes each of them, it should become different and give different things. And how is it different when it rises, when it has come to a centre, and when it declines?
!note "Far below it": the zodiac is a band of the sphere of the fixed stars, the outermost; each planet turns in its own sphere below it, and only seems to pass through the signs.

@p
@greek οὐ γὰρ δὴ τοτὲ μὲν ἥδεται
For surely it is not glad at one time, when it is on the centre, and grieved at another, when it declines, or idle; nor is another angry when it has risen and gentle when it declines, while one of them is even better when it declines. For each one is always at a centre for some and declining for others, and declining for some while at a centre for others; and surely it is not glad and grieved, angry and gentle, at the same time. And to say that some of them are glad when setting and others when rising, how is that not unreasonable? For so too it follows that they are grieved and glad at the same time. Then why will their grief do us harm?

@p
@greek ὅλως δὲ οὐδὲ λυπεῖσθαι
In general we must not grant that they are grieved, or glad on occasion, but that they are always gracious, glad in the goods they have and in what they see. For each has its own life by itself, and its well-being in its own activity; and this is not directed towards us. And above all, with living beings that have no fellowship with us, their work towards us is incidental, not their main work, not at all, if, as with birds, signifying is incidental to them.
!flag Like the birds of augury, the living beings of the heavens, having no lot or part with us, may serve incidentally to foreshow the future | The Greek puts it as a condition: their work towards us is incidental "if, as with birds, signifying is incidental" to them (εἰ ὥσπερ ὄρνισι κατὰ συμβεβηκὸς τὸ σημαίνειν). MacKenna makes it a statement.
!note The birds are those of augury, read by their flight and cries; Plotinus comes back to them in chapter 7.

@ch II.3.4 | Aspects, enmities, and how they could act at all
Why should one star be glad to see another and another be vexed? What enmity can there be among them? Why should the trine, the square and the opposition differ, and why should a star not see one that is nearer, in the next sign? How could they act each alone, or all together? They do not bargain with one another, nor force one another, nor give way; and to say that one is glad in the other's houses and the other the reverse is like supposing two friends of whom one loves and the other hates.

@p
And this too is unreasonable, that one should be glad seeing this one, and another, seeing another, the opposite. For what enmity have they, or about what? And why does a star see in one way when it is in trine, and in another when it is in opposition or in square? And why does it see when it stands in this figure, and does not see in the next sign, though it is nearer? And in general, what will be the way in which they do what they are said to do? How does each do it apart, and further, how do all of them together make something else, out of all of them?
!note The aspects: two planets a third of the circle apart (120°) are "in trine", a quarter apart (90°) "in square", half (180°) "in opposition"; planets in neighbouring signs (30°) were held not to see each other at all, though they are nearer.

@p
@greek οὐ γὰρ δὴ συνθέμενοι
For surely they do not come to an agreement with one another and so do to us what they have decided, each giving up something of its own; nor did one prevent the other's gift by force, nor did the other give way to it, persuaded to let it act. And to say that this one is glad when it is in the other's places, and the other, the other way round, when it is in the first one's: how is this not as if someone supposed two who love each other, and then said that the one loves the other, but the other, the other way round, hates the first?
!note "In the other's places" (ἐν τοῖς τοῦδε): the astrologers' "houses", the signs where each planet is at home and strongest.

@ch II.3.5 | Cold and hot stars, the moon, and the stars as parts of one living being
The astrologers' cold star should be good to us in the opposite signs; cold and hot in opposition should blend, not both grow dangerous; it is always day for the stars, and night cannot reach them. Their moon is good when full and bad when waning, but towards the planet above her it would be the other way round. In fact what comes from the stars is warm, more or less, never cold. Yet all of them serve the whole and stand to one another as the whole needs, like the parts of one living being, the gall among them; and so by analogy they are signs.

@p
They say that one of them is cold, and that it is better for us the farther it is from us, putting its harm to us in its coldness; and yet it ought to be good for us in the opposite signs. And that when the cold one is in opposition to the hot, both become dangerous; and yet there ought to be a blend. And that this one is glad in the daytime and becomes good when it is warmed, and that one, being fiery, is glad at night: as if it were not always day for them, I mean light, or as if the second could be overtaken by night, when it is far above the shadow of the earth.
!flag When the cold planet, we are told, is in opposition to the cold, both become menacing | The Greek says the cold one in opposition "to the hot" (τὸν ψυχρὸν τῷ θερμῷ), which is why Plotinus says they ought rather to blend.
!flag as if the first one could be darkened by night | The Greek says "the other" (τοῦ ἑτέρου), the fiery star that is glad at night; MacKenna makes it the first.
!note The astrologers divided the planets into those of the day and those of the night. The cold star is Saturn (Kronos), a planet of the day, tempered by the day's warmth; the fiery one, glad at night, is Mars (Ptolemy, Tetrabiblos I.4-7). The earth's shadow, a cone, reaches only a little beyond the moon, so the planets above it are always in sunlight.

@p
@greek τὸ δὲ τὴν σελήνην
And they say that the moon is good when it is full and meets this planet, and bad when it is waning; but it would be the other way round, if we are to grant this at all. For when it is full towards us, it would be unlit, on its other half, towards that planet, which is above it; and when it is waning for us, it would be full for that one; so that, when waning, it ought to do the opposite, since it then looks at that planet with its light. To the moon itself, however it stands, it would make no difference, since half of it is always lit; to the other planet perhaps it would make a difference, being warmed, as they say. But that one would be warmed if the moon were unlit towards us; and towards the other planet the moon, being good to it, is full towards it in its unlit phase. The moon's being unlit towards us concerns the things on earth; it does not grieve what is above. And because that planet gives no help, being far away, the moon seems to be worse; but when it is full, it is enough for what is below, even if the other is far away. And towards the fiery one, the moon when unlit towards us was thought to be good: for that one's fire is enough, since it is more fiery than it needs to be.
!flag the reason alleged is that the force of Mars is all-sufficient since it contains more fire than it needs | A hard place: the Greek says only "for that one's is enough, being more fiery than towards that one" (αὐταρκεῖ γὰρ τὸ ἐκείνου πυρωδεστέρου ἢ πρὸς ἐκεῖνον ὄντος), which is probably damaged; "than it needs" is MacKenna's reading, and the modern version's. He names Mars where the Greek says "the fiery one".
!note The moon is always half lit by the sun: when its lit half faces the earth, its dark half faces the planets above it, and the other way round. So, Plotinus argues, if the moon's light helped a planet, it would help it most at the new moon.

@p
@greek τὰ δὲ ἰόντα ἐκεῖθεν
The bodies of the ensouled beings that come from there are warmer, some more and some less than others, but none is cold: their place bears witness to it. The one they call Zeus is well tempered with fire, and so is the Morning Star; and so they seem to be in harmony, through likeness. With the one called the Fiery, Zeus is helpful through the blend; with Kronos he is alien, through distance. Hermes is indifferent towards them all, it seems, since he becomes like each.
!note The planets by their Greek names: Zeus is Jupiter, the Morning Star (Heōsphoros) is Venus, the Fiery (Pyroeis) is Mars, Kronos is Saturn, and Hermes is Mercury. "Their place bears witness": the stars are in the upper region of fire (II.1.7).

@p
@greek πάντες δὲ πρὸς
But all of them are helpful to the whole; so they stand towards one another as is helpful to the whole, as each of the parts is seen to in one living being. For it is above all for the sake of this that they are, as the gall is there both for the whole and for its neighbourhood: for it had to rouse spirit, and yet keep both the whole and its neighbourhood from running wild. So in the All too there had to be something of this kind, something unpleasant joined to the pleasant. And some parts had to be eyes; and all are in sympathy through what is without reason in them: for so there is one thing and one harmony. How then would they not be signs, by analogy?
!flag it is to be the inciter of the animal spirits | The Greek says the gall had to "rouse spirit" (θυμὸν ἐγείρειν), the part of the soul that is angry; "animal spirits" is MacKenna's.
!flag but all the members will be in sympathy with the entire animal frame to which they belong | The Greek says all are in sympathy "through what is without reason in them" (τῷ ἀλόγῳ αὐτῶν): the sympathy of the All works in its irrational life, not in reason.
!note In Greek medicine bile, or gall (χολή), is bound up with anger: the word for a quick temper in chapter 11, ἀκροχολία, is made from it. In the Timaeus (70a-71b) the liver and its bile serve the spirited part.

@ch II.3.6 | Mars, Aphrodite, and too much work for the stars
That Mars and Venus should cause adulteries to satisfy their own desires is absurd, and so is their endless pleasure in seeing each other. How could the stars see to every one of countless living beings, wait for the risings of the signs, count degrees as years on their fingers? To give the government of all to them, and not to one who presides, from whom the All hangs, is to dissolve the cosmos and not to know its nature.

@p
And to suppose that Ares here, or Aphrodite, make adulteries if they stand in this way, as if they filled themselves, out of the licentiousness of human beings, with what they want from each other: how is this not quite unreasonable? And that they find the sight of each other pleasant, if they see each other in this way, and that there is no limit for them: how could anyone accept it?
!flag And who could accept the fancy that their happiness comes from their seeing each other in this or that relative position and not from their own settled nature ? | The Greek says "and that there is no limit (πέρας) for them": no end to their desire. Nothing is said of their settled nature.
!note Ares and Aphrodite are the planets Mars and Venus, named for the lovers of Homer's song of Demodocus (Odyssey 8.266-366), caught in adultery by Hephaestus' net.

@p
@greek μυριάδων δὲ ζῴων
When countless myriads of living beings are born and exist, to accomplish for each of them always: to give them fame, to make them rich, poor, licentious, and to accomplish the activities of each themselves: what sort of life is that for them? Or how is it possible to do so much? And to wait for the risings of the signs, and act then; and that as many degrees as each sign rises in, so many are the years of its rising; and to reckon, as it were on their fingers, when they will act, and not to be allowed to do so before those times: and in general to give no one the sovereignty of the government, but to give all things to them, as if there were not one who presides, from whom the All hangs, who gives to each to accomplish its own according to its nature and to do its own work, ordered together with him: this is to dissolve the cosmos and not to know its nature, a cosmos that has a principle and a first cause that goes to all things.
!flag such a one, they see, has risen by so many degrees, representing so many of the periods of its upward path | The Greek says that as many degrees as each sign rises in, "so many years" (ἐνιαυτοὺς τοσούτους) of the rising: the astrologers' way of reckoning the length of a life. "Periods" is MacKenna's.
!note "Risings" (ἀναφοραί): the time each sign of the zodiac takes to rise above the horizon, counted in degrees of the equator; the astrologers turned degrees into years to foretell how long a man would live (Ptolemy, Tetrabiblos III.10). "From whom the All hangs" echoes Aristotle (Metaphysics XII.7, 1072b14), as in II.2.2.

@ch II.3.7 | Signs, like letters written in the sky
If the stars signify, what is the cause, and what the order? Let them be like letters written in the sky, doing their own work, with signifying following on it, as one part of a living being tells of another: the eyes tell character, dangers and safety. All things are full of signs, and the wise man reads one from another. There must be one ordering: all things are bound together, one breathing together, more in the All than in any one living being, and each part does its own work, contributing to the others, not at random but by nature.

@p
But if they signify what will be, as we say many other things too are signs of what will be, what would be the cause? And how is there an order? For there would be no signifying in an ordered way if things did not each come about in order. Let them be, then, like letters always being written in the heaven, or written once and moving, doing indeed another work as well; and let their signifying follow on this, as in one living being, from one principle, one could learn one thing from another part. For one could know a man's character, and his dangers and ways of safety too, by looking at his eyes or some other part of his body. So they are parts, and we are parts too: different things, then, for different things. All things are full of signs, and wise is he who has learned one thing from another. Many things are known to all by now from long habit.
!flag Obviously, unless the particular is included under some general principle of order, there can be no signification. | The Greek says "for there would be no signifying in an ordered way if each thing did not come about" in order (οὐ γὰρ ἂν ἐσημαίνετο τεταγμένως μὴ ἑκάστων γινομένων): the order is in the events, not a general principle above them.
!note Reading character from the eyes and the body is physiognomy, as in the Physiognomonica handed down under Aristotle's name.

@p
@greek τίς οὖν ἡ σύνταξις
What, then, is the one ordering of them all together? For so the signs taken from birds are reasonable too, and from the other animals, by which we are given signs of each thing. All things must be bound together with one another; and not only in one of the individual things, if it is well knit, is there one breathing together, but much more, and first, in the All. And one principle must make one living being of many, and one out of all; and as in each one thing each of the parts has taken one work, so the things in the All must each have their works, and more than those parts, inasmuch as they are not only parts but also wholes, and greater.
!flag but more markedly so since in this case the parts are not merely members but themselves Alls, members of the loftier Kind | The Greek says only that they are "not only parts but also wholes, and greater" (μὴ μόνον μέρη, ἀλλὰ καὶ ὅλα καὶ μείζω); "members of the loftier Kind" is MacKenna's.
!note "One breathing together" (σύμπνοια μία) is a phrase of the Hippocratic book On nourishment (23): "one flowing together, one breathing together, all things in sympathy". The Stoics took it up for the sympathy of the cosmos.

@p
@greek πρόεισι μὲν δὴ ἕκαστον
So each goes forth from one principle, doing its own work, and one contributes to another, for none has been cut off from the whole; and each acts, and is acted on by others, and another in turn came near and grieved or pleased it. But it goes forth not at random, nor by chance: for something else comes from these too, and next, by nature, another.
!flag all is one scheme of differentiation, starting from the Firsts and working itself out in a continuous progression of Kinds | The Greek says only "for something else comes from these too, and next, by nature, another" (ἄλλο τι καὶ ἐκ τούτων καὶ ἐφεξῆς κατὰ φύσιν ἄλλο); the Firsts and the Kinds are MacKenna's.

@ch II.3.8 | Soul's work, justice, and what the stars do
Soul makes all things, and whether it goes straight or goes astray, justice follows what is done in the All, which lasts because the whole is kept upright. The stars, no small parts of the heaven, work with it, splendid for the whole and good for signifying: they signify everything, but do only what they plainly do. We do the works of soul by nature, until we slip in the multitude of the All, and then the slip is our penalty. Wealth and poverty come from meeting outside things; virtue from what is ancient in the soul, vice from its meeting with what is outside.

@p
And soul too, set on doing its own work (for soul makes all things, having the standing of a principle), whether it goes straight or is again thrown into disorder: justice follows on what is done in the All, if the All is not to be dissolved. And it lasts for ever, the whole being kept upright by the order and the power of what rules it. And the stars too, as parts of the heaven that are by no means small, work together with it, being splendid in the whole and good for signifying. So they signify everything that is in the perceptible world, but they make other things, those that they plainly make.
!flag alike in its direct course (its tendence towards the divine ?) and in its divagation (its activity towards the lower ?) | The Greek says "whether it goes straight or is thrown into disorder" (κἂν εὐθυποροῖ καὶ ταράττοιτο αὖ): the soul's acting rightly or wrongly, which justice then follows. MacKenna's guesses in brackets are his.
!flag it is the cause of all by its possession of the Thought of the First Principle | The Greek says soul makes all things "having the standing of a principle" (ἀρχῆς ἔχουσα λόγον): it is itself a principle. Nothing is said of the First Principle or its thought.
!note "Splendid" (ἀριπρεπής) is a word of Homer's for the stars (Iliad 8.556).

@p
@greek ἡμεῖς δὲ ψυχῆς ἔργα
We do the works of soul according to nature, as long as we have not slipped in the multitude of the All; and when we have slipped, we have our penalty: both the slip itself, and a worse lot afterwards. Riches and poverty, then, come by a chance meeting with things outside; and virtues and vices, how? Virtues through what is ancient in the soul, vices through the soul's chance meeting with what is outside. But of these things we have spoken elsewhere.
!flag For our part, nature keeps us upon the work of the Soul | The Greek says "we do the works of soul according to nature" (ψυχῆς ἔργα κατὰ φύσιν ποιοῦμεν): nature is how we do them, not what keeps us on them.
!note "What is ancient in the soul": its ancient nature (ἀρχαία φύσις), which Plato's sea-god Glaucus, crusted with shells and weed, hides from sight (Republic X, 611c-612a). "Elsewhere": in On destiny (III.1.8-10).

@ch II.3.9 | The spindle, the Timaeus, and the two selves
In Plato the Fates turn the spindle at each birth; in the Timaeus the god gives the principle of the soul, and the gods that move give the passions and the lower soul. So what is left that is we? What we truly are, who can master the passions: God gave us virtue that has no master. We must flee from here and not be the ensouled body; the other soul, outside, goes upward to the divine, and whoever is bereft of it lives under destiny, a part carried along by the whole. Each of us is double, and the All too, and the sun: the pure soul gets nothing bad from the stars, and what comes from them is given by body to body, while the star's soul looks to the best. Without its separable soul the All is a great daemon.

@p
Now let us recall the spindle, which for the ancients the Fates spin, while for Plato the spindle is what wanders and what does not wander in the revolution of the heaven; and the Fates and Necessity, their mother, turn it, and spin at the birth of each one, and through Necessity what is born comes to birth. And in the Timaeus the god who made the cosmos gives the principle of the soul, while the gods that are carried round give the terrible and necessary passions, angers and desires, pleasures and pains, and another kind of soul, from which these experiences come. For these words bind us to the stars, from which we bring a soul with us, and subject us to necessity as we come here; and so our characters come from them, and according to our characters our actions and passions, coming from a disposition that is open to passion. So what is left that is we?
!flag To Plato the Spindle represents the co-operation of the moving and the stable elements of the kosmic circuit | The Greek says the spindle is "what wanders and what does not wander" (τό τε πλανώμενον καὶ τὸ ἀπλανὲς) in the revolution: the planets and the fixed stars. "Co-operation" is MacKenna's.
!note The spindle of Necessity is in the myth of Er (Republic X, 616c-617d): its whorls are the spheres of the fixed stars and of the planets, and the three Fates, Lachesis, Clotho and Atropos, daughters of Necessity, turn it. The gods who give the lower soul are in the Timaeus (41c-d, 42a), and "the terrible and necessary passions" are its words (69c-d).

@p
@greek ἢ ὅπερ ἐσμὲν
Or rather, what we truly are: we, to whom nature has given the power to master the passions. For all the same, among these evils, fenced in as we are by the nature of the body, God gave us virtue, which has no master. For it is not when we are at peace that we need virtue, but when there is a danger of being among evils if virtue is not present. So we must flee from here, and separate ourselves from what has grown onto us, and not be the composite, an ensouled body, in which the nature of body has the mastery, having taken a trace of soul, so that the common life is more the body's: for everything that belongs to this life is bodily.
!flag The " We " is the actual resultant of a Being whose nature includes, with certain sensibilities, the power of governing them. | The Greek says "what we truly are" (ὅπερ ἐσμὲν κατ’ ἀλήθειαν ἡμεῖς), to whom nature gave the power to master the passions. For MacKenna the "We" is a "resultant"; for Plotinus it is the true self, not the composite.
!flag virtue the unconquerable | The Greek ἀδέσποτον is "with no master over it", Plato's word: the herald of the myth of Er says that "virtue has no master", and each will have more or less of it as he honours it (Republic X, 617e).
!note "We must flee from here": Plato's Theaetetus (176a-b), where to flee from here is to become like God, as far as one can.

@p
@greek τῆς δὲ ἑτέρας τῆς ἔξω
To the other soul, the one outside, belong the carrying upward, and the beautiful, and the divine, over which no one is master: one either makes use of them, so as to be that, and lives by it, withdrawn; or, bereft of this soul, one lives in destiny, and then the stars do not only signify for him, but he becomes himself a kind of part, and is carried along with the whole of which he is a part.
!flag no longer profiting, merely, by the significance of the sidereal system | The Greek says that for one who lives in destiny the stars "do not only signify" (οὐ μόνον σημαίνει) for him: they act on him as well. Nothing is said of profiting.

@p
@greek διττὸς γὰρ ἕκαστος
For each of us is double: one is a kind of composite of the two, the other is himself. And the whole cosmos too: one is what is made of body and a soul bound to body, the other is the soul of the All, which is not in body, but shines traces of itself into the soul that is in body. And the sun and the other stars are double in this way too. To the other soul, the pure one, they give nothing bad; and what comes to the All from them, inasmuch as they are parts of the All and an ensouled body, the body gives, a part to a part, while the star's choice, and its soul that truly is, look to the best.
!flag body is the agent while, at the same time, it becomes the vehicle through which is transmitted something of the star's will | The Greek says the body gives, a part to a part, "while the star's choice and its soul that truly is look to the best" (προαιρέσεως τοῦ ἄστρου καὶ ψυχῆς τῆς ὄντως αὐτοῦ πρὸς τὸ ἄριστον βλεπούσης). Nothing of the star's will is passed on through the body: the star's will is turned the other way.

@p
@greek παρακολουθεῖ δ’
And the other things follow on it, or rather not on it, but on what is around it: as heat goes from fire into the whole, or as something goes from one soul into another soul that is akin to it. The troublesome things come from the mixture. For the nature of this All is mixed, and if one were to separate from it its separable soul, what is left is not much. It is a god, then, when that soul is counted in; but the rest is, he says, a great daemon, and the passions in it are daemonic.
!flag But (with every allowance to the lower forces) all follows either upon that Highest or rather upon the Beings about It — we may think of the Divine as a fire | A hard place: the Greek says "the other things follow on it, or rather not on it, but on those around it" (παρακολουθεῖ δ’ αὐτῷ τὰ ἄλλα, μᾶλλον δ’ οὐκ αὐτῷ, ἀλλὰ τοῖς περὶ αὐτόν). "It" is masculine, most likely the star, not "that Highest"; and the fire is only an image of how heat spreads, not "the Divine".
!flag " is a mighty spirit and its ways are subdivine." | The Greek is δαίμων μέγας, "a great daemon", and the passions in it are "daemonic" (δαιμόνια): Plato's words for Love in the Symposium (202d-e), a great daemon between gods and mortals. "Spirit" and "subdivine" hide the quotation.

@ch II.3.10 | What to grant the stars, the soul and the revolution
So the stars signify, but they act only in what are affections of the All and in their lower part. The soul brings something of its own before it is born: it would not come into a body unless it were open to being much affected. It meets chance as it enters at a moment of the revolution; and the revolution itself acts, working with the All and filling up what the All must do, each thing in it having the rank of a part.

@p
If it is so, we must grant the signs even now; but the doings, not in every way, nor to the stars as wholes, but only those that are affections of the All, and as much as belongs to what remains of them. And to the soul we must grant that, even before it comes to birth, it comes bringing something of its own: for it would not come into a body if it did not have a great capacity to be affected. We must grant too that chances meet it as it enters, since it enters at the very moment of the revolution. And we must grant that the revolution itself acts, working together and filling up from itself what the All must accomplish, each of the things in it having taken the rank of a part.
!flag for it could never touch body except under stress of a powerful inner impulse | The Greek says it would not come into a body "unless it had a great capacity to be affected" (μὴ μέγα τι παθητικὸν ἔχουσα): something passive in it, not an impulse.
!note "What remains of them" (τὸ λοιπὸν αὐτῶν): the stars' bodies and lower soul, "the rest" of chapter 9, as against their pure soul.

@ch II.3.11 | What comes from above changes on the way down
What comes from the stars does not arrive as it left them: fire grows dim; friendliness grows weak and makes an unlovely love; spirit, not taken in measure, makes a quick temper or faint-heartedness instead of courage; the love of honour makes a desire for what only seems beautiful; and the outflow of intellect becomes knavery, which wants to be intellect and cannot. These things are not bad there, and what comes is changed again by mixing with bodies, matter and each other.

@p
We must also bear in mind that what goes out from them does not come into those who receive it such as it is when it leaves them: if it is fire, it is dim here; and if it is a disposition to friendship, it becomes weak in the receiver and makes a love that is not very beautiful; and spirit, in one who does not receive it in measure, so as to become brave, makes a quick temper or faint-heartedness; and what lies in the love of honour and is concerned with beauty makes a desire for things that seem beautiful; and the outflow of intellect makes knavery: for knavery wants to be intellect, but cannot attain what it aims at. So each of these things becomes bad in us, though they are not so there. For what has come, though it is not those things, does not stay as it came either, being mixed with bodies, with matter and with one another.
!flag and ambition ... in love . . . | MacKenna's dots mark a gap; Volkmann's text has none here: "what lies in the love of honour and is concerned with beauty" (τὸ τιμῆς ἐν ἔρωτι ὂν καὶ περὶ τὸ καλὸν ἔχον) makes a desire for what only seems beautiful.
!flag intellectual power at its lowest produces the extreme of wickedness, for wickedness is a miscalculating effort towards Intelligence | The Greek πανουργία is "knavery", cleverness ready for anything, not "the extreme of wickedness"; and it comes from "the outflow of intellect" (νοῦ ἀπόρροια), not from intellect "at its lowest".
!note "Knavery" (πανουργία): in Plato's Menexenus (246e-247a) "all knowledge cut off from justice and the rest of virtue shows itself as knavery, not wisdom".

@ch II.3.12 | The outflow adds a quality; it does not make the thing
All that comes from above falls together into one, and each thing that comes to be takes something from the mixture, to be what it is and of some quality. It does not make the horse: horse comes from horse and man from man, with the sun working with them; man comes from the forming principle of man. Outside things help or harm, as the father may, but they do not drive a thing out of its nature, though sometimes matter wins and the form is overcome.

@p
And what comes falls together into one, and each of the things that come to be takes something from this mixture, so as to become what it is and also of some quality. For it does not make the horse, but gives the horse something: for horse comes from horse, and man from man; the sun works with them in the shaping, but the man comes from the forming principle of man. But outside things have sometimes harmed or helped: for they are like the father: things often fell out for the better, but sometimes for the worse. But they do not drive a thing out of what underlies it; though sometimes matter prevails, and not nature, so that the thing does not come to be complete, since the form is overcome.
!flag though sometimes Matter is the dominant, and the human principle takes the second place | The Greek says "matter prevails, and not nature" (ἡ ὕλη κρατεῖ, οὐχ ἡ φύσις); "the human principle" is MacKenna's.
!note "Man begets man, and the sun too": Aristotle's Physics (II.2, 194b13).

@ch II.3.13 | Where each thing comes from: the All as one living being
To sort out what comes from the revolution and what does not, begin from this: soul governs the All by a forming principle, as the principle in a living being shapes its parts and orders them to the whole. The soulless things are tools; animals move at random, like horses before the charioteer sets their course; the rational living being has its charioteer within. Some parts act more, some are acted on, some do both; the best follow, like soldiers following Zeus to the intelligible. No part can change the forming principles, though one can make another better or worse: by weakening the body, by making the soul that sympathizes with it worse, or by being a badly tuned lyre.

@p
From here on, then, since some things come about from the revolution and others do not, we must distinguish and sort them, and say where each one comes from in general. The beginning is this. Soul governs this All according to a forming principle, just as in each living being there is the principle in it from which each of the living being's parts is shaped and ordered to the whole of which they are parts; and in the whole there is all of it, but in the parts only as much as each one is. What comes to each from outside is sometimes contrary to the will of its nature, and sometimes helpful; but to the whole all things are ordered together in every way, being parts of it, having received the nature they have, and yet filling up, by their own impulse, the whole life of the All.
!flag the entire force of the Soul is represented in the All but, in the parts, Soul is present only in proportion to the degree of essential reality held by each of such partial objects | The Greek says only "in the whole there is all, but in the parts only as much as each is" (ἐν μὲν τῷ ὅλῳ ἐστὶ τὰ πάντα, ἐν δὲ τοῖς μέρεσι τοσοῦτον μόνον, ὅσον ἐστὶν ἕκαστον); "the degree of essential reality" is MacKenna's.

@p
@greek τὰ μὲν οὖν ἄψυχα
The soulless things in it are wholly instruments, and are, as it were, pushed from outside to act. Of the ensouled, some have motion without a fixed course, like horses under the chariot before the charioteer marks out their course for them, since they are driven by the whip; but the nature of a rational living being has its charioteer from itself, and when it has one who knows, it goes straight, and not, as it often does, at random. Both are inside the All and contribute to the whole.
!note The charioteer and the horses are Plato's image of the soul (Phaedrus 246a-b). "Driven by the whip" (πληγῇ νεμόμενα) recalls the Critias (109b-c), where the gods guide men not by blows, as shepherds drive their flocks, but by persuasion.

@p
@greek καὶ τὰ μὲν μείζω
And the greater of them, and of more worth, do many great things and contribute to the life of the whole, having an active rather than a passive rank; others go on being acted on, having little power to act; and those between are acted on by others, but do many things, and in many matters have from themselves a principle of action and making. And the All becomes a complete life, the best things doing the best works, as far as the best is in each; and this must be ordered under the leader, as soldiers under a general, who are said to follow "Zeus as he goes towards" the intelligible nature. Those that have a lesser nature are the second things of the All, as are the second things of soul in us; and the rest are in proportion to the parts in us: for in us too not all are equal.
!flag just as in us the members rank lower than the Soul | The Greek says "as are the second things of soul in us" (οἷα καὶ τὰ ἐν ἡμῖν ψυχῆς δεύτερα): the soul's own lower powers, not the members of the body.
!note "Follow Zeus": in the Phaedrus (246e-247a) Zeus, the great leader in heaven, drives his winged chariot first, and the army of gods and daemons follows him up to the place above the heaven.

@p
@greek ζῷα μὲν οὖν
All living beings, then, are according to the whole forming principle of the All, both all those in the heaven and the others that have been parted out to make the whole; and none of the parts, however great, has the power to work a change in the forming principles, or in what comes to be according to the forming principles. It can work an alteration either way, for the worse or for the better, but it cannot make a thing stand outside its own nature. It makes things worse either by giving the body weakness; or by becoming, incidentally, a cause of badness to the soul that is in sympathy with the body and has been given by the All to what is below; or, when a body is badly put together, by hindering the activity directed at it from working through it: as when a lyre has not been tuned so as to take the exactness of the harmony that makes its notes musical.
!flag which by the medium of the material frame, become a power to debasement, has been delivered over, though never in its essence, to the inferior order of being | The Greek says the part becomes "incidentally a cause of badness" (κατὰ συμβεβηκὸς φαυλότητος αἴτιον) to the soul that is in sympathy with the body; "though never in its essence" is MacKenna's turn of "incidentally", and the debasing body is his.

@ch II.3.14 | Wealth, fame, office, marriage, birth: their real causes
Inherited wealth the stars only signify, as they signify noble birth. Wealth won by one's own deeds goes back to the body's strength, and so to the parents and the place, or to virtue alone; wealth given goes back to the givers, good or bad; wealth from farming to the farmer, with the weather; treasure found to the All, and so it is signified. Losses go to the robber or the sea; fame and office to the deeds or to the injustice of those who give them; marriage to choice or chance; and a child's form to its forming principle, or to some hindrance within or around the mother.

@p
And how about poverty and wealth, fame and office? If wealth comes from one's fathers, the stars signified the rich man, just as they only made known the man of good birth, born of such parents and having fame through his family. If it comes from manly excellence, then, if the body has worked with it, those who made the body's strength would contribute: first the parents, then, if any of the places supplied something, the heavens and the earth. If virtue won it without the body, to virtue alone the most must be given, and whatever divine gifts came with it from those who rewarded it. If others gave the wealth, and they are good, the cause must be traced back to virtue in this way too; if they are bad, but gave justly, it came about through what is best in them acting. If the man who grew rich is wicked, his wickedness comes first, and whatever is the cause of his wickedness; and the givers must be taken in too, as having become joint causes in the same way.
!flag Wealth may be due to personal activity | The Greek ἀνδραγαθία is "manly excellence", brave and good deeds, not activity in general.
!flag must be attributed to virtue exclusively | The Greek gives virtue alone "the most" (τὸ πλεῖστον), not all of it, and adds the divine gifts that came with it.

@p
@greek εἰ δ’ ἐκ πόνων
If it comes from labour, as from farming, the cause goes back to the farmer, with his surroundings working with him. If he found a treasure, something from the All fell in with him; and if so, it is signified: for all things follow on one another in every way, and so it is signified in every way. If a man lost his wealth, if it was taken from him, the cause goes back to the one who took it, and he to his own principle; if he lost it at sea, to the things that fell out together. Fame is either just or not. If it is just, the cause goes back to the deeds, and to the better judgement of those who give it; if it is not just, to the injustice of those who honour. And the same account holds of office: it is held either fittingly or not; and the first goes back to the better judgement of those who chose, or to the man himself, who brought it about by the backing of his friends, or in some other way. With marriages, it is either choice, or chance and the falling together of things from the wholes. The births of children follow on these, and the child is either shaped according to its forming principle, with nothing hindering it, or is worse, some hindrance having arisen within, either because of the mother herself, or because the surroundings were so disposed as to be ill-suited to this pregnancy.
!flag As for good fame, it is either deserved and then is due to the services done and to the merit of those appraising them | The Greek says the cause goes back to the deeds and "to the better" judgement in those who hold the opinion (τὸ παρὰ τοῖς δοξάζουσι βέλτιον): their good sense, not their merit.
!note "The surroundings" (τὸ περιέχον) is the astrologers' word for the sky and air around the earth, which works with the farmer and on the pregnant mother.

@ch II.3.15 | Plato's lots, the Fates, and those who rise above them
In Plato the lots and choices come before the spindle turns; then the stars in the spindle help to fulfil what was chosen, and the daemon too. The lots are the state of the All when we enter the body: this body, these parents, this place. Clotho spins it all together, Lachesis gives the lots, Atropos brings on what has fallen out. Some people are bewitched by the outside and are little themselves; others rise above it, head first, and keep the soul's ancient being. For the soul has a nature of its own, with its own powers and desires; once separated, it does its own works and sees that the body's passions are not its own.

@p
Plato gives the lots and the choices before the turning of the spindle, and afterwards gives the things in the spindle as fellow workers, as bringing to completion in every way what was chosen; for the daemon too is a fellow worker in fulfilling it. But what are the lots? To be born when the All is as it was then, when the souls entered the body; and to enter this body, and to be born of these parents and in such places; and, in general, as we said, the things outside. That all these come about together and are, as it were, spun together, is shown through the first of the so-called Fates, both for each and for all; Lachesis gives the lots; and it is necessary that Atropos should bring on in every way what has fallen out together.
!flag that once done, only the Spindle-destiny is valid ; it fixes the chosen conditions irretrievably | The Greek says that Plato afterwards gives "those in the spindle" (τοὺς ἐν τῷ ἀτράκτῳ), the stars that turn in it, as "fellow workers" (συνεργοὺς), helping to bring what was chosen to completion; nothing is said of the spindle alone being valid.
!flag the elected guardian-spirit | The Greek is ὁ δαίμων, the daemon each soul chooses in the myth of Er; "elected" is MacKenna's.
!note The myth of Er (Republic X, 617d-620e): the souls draw lots for their turn to choose a life; Lachesis gives each the daemon it has chosen, Clotho confirms the choice under the turning spindle, and Atropos makes it irreversible. Plotinus' "first of the so-called Fates" is Clotho, the spinner, whom MacKenna names.

@p
@greek τῶν δ’ ἀνθρώπων
Of human beings, some come to belong to what comes from the whole and from outside, as if bewitched, and are little or nothing themselves. Others master these things, and, rising above them, as if with their head, towards what is above and outside soul, keep safe what is best and ancient in the being of soul. For surely we must not think that soul is the kind of thing whose nature is whatever it undergoes from outside, alone of all things with no nature of its own. Rather, much more than the other things, since it has the standing of a principle, it must have many powers of its own for its natural activities: for it is not possible that, being a substance, it should not have, along with its being, desires and actions and a bent towards the good. The composite, then, comes from the composite nature, and is of this kind and has works of this kind; but if a soul is separated, it does separate works, its own, not counting the body's passions as its own, since it now sees that the one is one thing and the other another.
!flag the Soul, possessing the Idea which belongs to a Principle | The Greek ἀρχῆς λόγον ἔχουσαν is "having the standing of a principle", as in chapter 8; "the Idea" is MacKenna's.
!note "As if with their head": in the Phaedrus (248a) the best soul "lifts the head of its charioteer into the place outside" the heaven. "Ancient": see chapter 8.

@ch II.3.16 | How soul governs the All: the farmer, and the worse things
What is mixed in us and what is separable must be asked later. Does soul make each thing in a line and then only watch them clash, or is it the cause of what follows too? The forming principle holds what each thing must do or suffer, of necessity: the principles do not make it, but the soul that has them knows what follows and strings it together, and so things grow worse, as men did. Is soul a farmer for ever mending what storms have spoiled? If not, ruin and vice lie in the forming principles, though art holds no error. Yet the worse has its use, and contraries make the cosmos: the better things the principle makes, the worse are matter's, shaken by the principles; and so one thing comes from all.

@p
But what is mixed and what is not, what is separable and what inseparable, when soul is in body, and in general what the living being is, must be looked into later, taking up another beginning: for not everyone has held the same opinion about it. For now let us say further in what sense we said that soul governs the All according to a forming principle. Does it make each thing as if in a straight line, a man, then a horse, and another animal, and wild beasts too, though fire and earth first; and then, seeing these fall together and destroying one another or helping one another, does it only see the interweaving that comes from them, and what always comes about afterwards, contributing nothing more to what follows, but only making again the births of the living beings that were from the beginning, and leaving them to what they undergo through one another? Or do we say that it is the cause of what comes about in this way too, since what came from it brings about what follows?
!flag For the present let us explain in what sense we have described the All as the expressed idea of the Governing Soul. | The Greek says "in what sense we said that soul governs the All according to a forming principle" (πῶς τὸ κατὰ λόγον ψυχῆς διοικούσης τὸ πᾶν εἴπομεν), in chapter 13; MacKenna makes the All the soul's "expressed idea".
!note "Must be looked into later": the next treatise Plotinus wrote, On what the living being is (I.1), the fifty-third of the fifty-four, takes up these questions.

@p
@greek ἢ καὶ τὸ τόδε τι παθεῖν
Or does the forming principle hold this thing's being affected or acting in this way too, not at random, nor by chance, nor as things merely happen, but of necessity so? Do the forming principles make these things, then? Or do the forming principles exist, not as making them but as knowing them; or rather, does the soul that has the generative forming principles know what follows from all its works? For when the same things fall together and surround a thing, the same things must in every way be brought about; and the soul, taking these up or foreseeing them, carries through and strings together what follows on them. So there are things that go before and things that follow, in every way, and again what follows goes before what comes after it, from what is present. Hence perhaps what follows is always worse: as men were different long ago and are different now, by the interval and by the always necessary yielding of the forming principles to the affections of matter.
!note "Men were different long ago": as in Homer, where a hero lifts a stone that two men, "such as mortals are now", could not carry (Iliad 5.302-304).

@p
@greek συνορῶσα οὖν ἀεὶ ἄλλα
So, seeing now these things and now those, and following the affections of its own works, it has a life of this kind, and is never freed from care for its work, as it would be if it had put the finish on what it made and had contrived once for all that it should be well for ever; but it is like a farmer who has sown or even planted, and is always setting right what rainy winters, or long frosts, or blasts of wind have harmed. But if this is absurd, we must say that ruin, and the works that come from vice, are already known, or even lie, in the forming principles. But if so, we shall say that the forming principles make the vices too; and yet in the arts and their principles there is no error, nor what is against the art, nor the ruin of what is made by art.
!flag ever labouring to bring about perfection, planning to lead all to an unending state of excellence | The Greek says the soul is never freed from care "as one who had put the finish on the work and contrived once for all that it should be well for ever" (τέλος ἐπιθεῖσα τῷ ποιήματι καὶ ὅπως ἕξει καλῶς καὶ εἰς ἀεὶ ἅπαξ μηχανησαμένη): that is what it has not done. MacKenna turns it into the soul's plan.
!flag the ruin and all the consequences of flaw | The Greek says "the works that come from vice" (τὰ ἀπὸ κακίας ἔργα): vice, not a flaw.

@p
@greek ἀλλ’ ἐνταῦθά τις ἐρεῖ
But here someone will say that there is nothing against nature, and nothing bad, for the whole. Still, the worse and the better will be granted. What then, if the worse too works with the whole, and not everything has to be beautiful? For contraries too contribute, and there is no cosmos without them; and so it is with the individual living beings too. The forming principle compels and shapes the better things; those that are not so lie in the forming principles potentially, and in what comes to be actually; for soul can no longer make them, or stir up the forming principles, when matter, by the shaking that comes from the forming principles that went before, is already making worse things of its own, and yet is overcome no less towards the better: so that there is one thing out of all, which come to be in one way from each side, and in another way again in the forming principles.
!flag since, amid the clash of these antecedent Principles, Matter had already from its own stock produced the less good | The Greek speaks of matter "shaken" (τῷ σεισμῷ) by the forming principles that went before, as the receptacle of the Timaeus is shaken (52e-53a): not of a clash among the principles.
!note "There is no cosmos without contraries": so Plato's Theaetetus (176a), "evils cannot perish, for there must always be something opposed to the good".

@ch II.3.17 | Are the forming principles thoughts? The figure in water
If the forming principles are thoughts, how do they make? What makes in matter does not think or see: it is a power that turns matter, as a figure is made in water when something else starts it. So the leading part of the soul makes by turning the soul in matter; not by reasoning, but by what holds the forming principles, and so by forms. Intellect gives to the soul of the All, that soul to the one after it, and this one makes as if under orders, sometimes hindered. Since its principles are not the first, something of its own comes in, worse: a living thing, but discontented, savage, made of matter that is the bitter sediment of what went before.

@p
Are these forming principles in the soul thoughts? But then how will it make according to its thoughts? For the forming principle makes in matter naturally, and what makes naturally is not thinking or seeing, but a power that turns matter, not knowing but only doing, like a print or a figure in water, a circle say, when something else has given it the power to make this. If so, the leading part of the soul will make by turning the soul that is in matter and generative. Will this, then, turn it after reasoning? But if after reasoning, it will refer to something. To something else, or to what is in itself? But if to what is in itself, there is no need of reasoning: for it is not reasoning that will turn it, but the part of the soul that holds the forming principles; for this is both stronger and able to make in the soul.
!flag where the formation of the ring is conditioned by something distinct from that force itself (i.e. by the existence and nature of the water) | The Greek says "something else having given it the power to make this" (ἄλλου ἐνδόντος εἰς τοῦτο τὸ ποιεῖν): whatever started the ring, as a stone dropped in. MacKenna's words in brackets, the water's nature, are his.

@p
@greek κατ’ εἴδη ἄρα ποιεῖ
So it makes according to forms. So it too must give what it has from intellect. Intellect gives to the soul of the All, and soul, the one after intellect, gives from itself to the one after it, shining on it and imprinting it; and this one, as if under orders, at once makes. It makes some things unhindered, others hindered. Since it has received power for making, and has been filled with forming principles that are not the first ones, it will not only make according to what it received, but something will come from itself too, and this is plainly worse: a living being, yes, but a less complete one, and discontented with its own life, as being the worst, and ill-tempered and savage, made of a worse matter, a kind of sediment of what went before, bitter and making things bitter. And this too the soul will supply to the whole.
!flag hindering its own life | The Greek δυσχεραῖνον τὴν αὑτοῦ ζωήν is "discontented with its own life", finding it hard to bear; not hindering it.
!note "Sediment" (ὑποστάθμη) is the word of Plato's Phaedo (109c) for the dregs that settle in the hollows of the earth where we live.

@ch II.3.18 | Why evils are needed; an image always being imaged
Evils are needed not only because they follow on what came before, but because without them the All would be incomplete: most have uses, like the poisons of snakes, and vice itself makes beautiful things and wakes us to wisdom. The soul of the All contemplates the best and, filled to overflowing, sends out an image, its lowest part, which makes; above it the soul filled from intellect; above all intellect the craftsman. So the cosmos is an image always being imaged, and the forming principles will flow as long as intellect and soul are, as light from the sun.

@p
Are the evils in the All necessary, then, because they follow on what went before? Or rather because, if they did not exist, the All would be incomplete. For most of them, or even all, provide some use to the whole, like those of the poisonous creatures, though why is hidden in most cases; since even vice itself has many uses, and makes many beautiful things, all the beauty made by art, for instance, and moves us to wisdom, not letting us sleep in security.
!flag Are the evils in the Universe necessary because it is of later origin than the Higher Sphere ? | The Greek asks whether they are necessary "because they follow on what went before" (ὅτι ἕπεται τοῖς προηγουμένοις), the things that came first in the chain of chapter 16; it says nothing of the All's being later than the higher sphere.
!flag it stirs us to thoughtful living | The Greek says vice moves us "to wisdom" (εἰς φρόνησιν).
!note "Not letting us sleep in security": Chrysippus said that bedbugs are useful for waking us and mice for making us careful (Plutarch, On the self-contradictions of the Stoics 21, 1044d).

@p
@greek εἰ δὴ ταῦτα ὀρθῶς εἴρηται
If this has been said rightly, the soul of the All must contemplate the best things, always reaching towards the intelligible nature and God; and as it is filled, and has been filled, as it were brimming over, the image that comes from it, its last part, towards what is below, is what makes. This, then, is the last maker; above it is the part of the soul that is first filled from intellect; and above all is intellect, the craftsman, who gives to the soul after him too the things whose traces are in the third. Rightly, then, is this cosmos called an image always being imaged: the first and the second stand still, and the third stands still too, but is moved in matter and incidentally. For as long as there are intellect and soul, the forming principles will flow into this form of soul, as, as long as there is a sun, all the lights that come from it.
!flag the divine Thought-Forms will pour forth into that phase of the Soul | The Greek says "the forming principles will flow" (ῥεύσονται οἱ λόγοι), the principles that chapter 17 says are not thoughts.
!note The Workshop's Greek of this chapter has a damaged word, "δἑ??ʼ", at the start of this sentence; the word is δεῖ, "must", which the modern version follows.
!note "The craftsman" (δημιουργός) is the maker of the cosmos in the Timaeus (28a-29a), who makes it after the eternal model; for Plotinus he is intellect. "An image always being imaged" recalls the Timaeus (37d), where time is "a moving image of eternity".

# Milestone E9: II.5 to II.8, the short treatises on nature (II.4, On matter, is Taylor's, read beside his English).
# MacKenna's chapters of all four are Volkmann's (vol. II, 1921, pp. 196-214).

@treatise II.5 | On what is potential and what is actual
Things are said to be potentially and actually, and there is also activity among the things that are. What is potential is always potentially something else, as bronze is potentially a statue; it is clearer to set the potential against the actual, and power against activity. Where matter takes a form, what is actual is the compound, and the form is the activity of this thing. In the intelligible world nothing waits for a future: everything there is actual, and is also activity and life. Matter alone is only potential: none of the beings, not even an image of a forming principle, actually a phantom and a falsehood, truly non-being; to make it actual would destroy it, so it must be kept potential if it is to last.

@ch II.5.1 | What "potentially" means
Is being actual the same as being an activity? Is the potential in the intelligible world too? First, what is potential is always potentially something: bronze is potentially a statue, being already bronze; and it either stays itself while the other comes to be, or gives itself up and perishes, as water becomes bronze and air fire. It is clearer to set the potential against the actual, and power against activity. What is potential is like a substrate for the forms it is going to receive.

@p
Some things are said to be potentially, others actually; and something among the things that are is also called activity. So we must look at what the potential is and what the actual is. Is activity the same as being actually, so that whatever is an activity is also actual? Or is each different from the other, so that what is actually need not also be an activity? That there is the potential among the things perceived by the senses is clear. Whether it is also among the intelligibles must be looked into, or whether there is only the actual there; and if the potential is there, whether it is only potential for ever, and, if for ever, whether it would never come into activity because it is shut off from time. But first we must say what the potential is, since the potential must not be spoken of simply: nothing can be potential without being potentially something. Bronze, for example, is potentially a statue. For if nothing were going to come from it or upon it, if nothing were going to be after what it was, and nothing could come to be, it would be only what it was. And what it was was already present, and not still to come: what else, then, could it have been able to be after what was present? It would not, then, be potentially anything.
!flag Actuality (an absolute, abstract Principle) | The Greek asks only whether activity (ἐνέργεια) is the same as being actually (τὸ ἐνεργείᾳ εἶναι); "an absolute, abstract Principle" is MacKenna's.
!note "Potentially" (δυνάμει) and "actually" (ἐνεργείᾳ), "power" (δύναμις) and "activity" (ἐνέργεια) are Aristotle's pairs (Metaphysics IX.1-9). The modern version keeps "activity" for ἐνέργεια, where MacKenna writes "Actuality" or "Act".

@p
@greek δεῖ τοίνυν τὸ δυνάμει τι ὂν
So what is potentially something else must already be something, and be said to be potentially through being able to be something else after itself: either staying itself while it makes that other, or giving itself up to what it is able to be and perishing itself. For bronze is potentially a statue in one way, and water is potentially bronze, and air fire, in another. Being potential in this way, could it also be called a power towards what will be, as if the bronze were the power of the statue? Not at all, if the power is taken as the power to make: for the power taken as the power to make could not be called potential. But if the potential is said not only in relation to the actual but also in relation to activity, the potential would be a power too. It is better and clearer, though, to set the potential against the actual, and power against activity. What is potential, then, is something like a substrate for affections, shapes and forms, which it is going to receive and is of a nature to receive, or even hastens to take: some of them for the better, others for the worse, things that ruin it, while each such substrate is also actually something else.
!flag water (= primalliquid) as potentially bronze | The Greek says only "water" (τὸ ὕδωρ); "primal liquid" is MacKenna's gloss.
!flag the Principle or Abstract in which potentiality | The Greek says that if the potential is spoken of in relation to activity, and not only to the actual, it would be a power too; the "Principle or Abstract" in MacKenna's brackets is his.
!flag in regard to the process of Actualisation | The Greek pairs "the potential" with "the actual" (τὸ ἐνεργείᾳ) and "power" with "activity" (ἐνέργεια): two pairs of words, not a process and a principle.
!note Water as potentially bronze: in Plato's Timaeus (58d-59c) gold, bronze and the other metals are kinds of water, "fusible" water. Air becoming fire is the change of the elements into one another (Timaeus 49b-c).

@ch II.5.2 | Matter, the compound, and the form as activity
Is matter actually something else? Does the potential become the actual, or does the actual come after it, as the statue, a compound, comes from the bronze? When a man potentially a grammarian becomes one, the same man is potential and actual, for his soul was fit to know. The form on the bronze may be called the activity of this thing; but activity more properly is what is set against a power that brings it on, as courage and acting bravely.

@p
We must look into matter: whether, being actually something else, it is potentially the things it is shaped into, or actually nothing; and in general whether the other things we call potential, when they take the form and stay themselves, become actual; or whether "actually" will be said of the statue only by setting the actual statue against the potential statue, and not by saying "actually" of that of which "potentially a statue" was said. If so, it is not the potential that becomes actual; rather, from what was potential before, the actual came to be afterwards. For what is actually is the compound of both, not the matter; and the form is what is upon it. This is so when a different substance comes to be, as a statue from bronze: for the statue, as the compound, is a different substance. And with things that do not stay at all it is plain that the potential was altogether different. But when the man who is potentially a grammarian becomes one actually, how is the potential here not the same as the actual? For Socrates potentially wise is the same as Socrates actually wise.
!flag a person with a capacity for education becomes in fact educated | The Greek is γραμματικός, one who knows letters, a grammarian: Aristotle's stock example of the learner.
!note The man who is potentially a grammarian, and so a knower in two senses, is Aristotle's (On the Soul II.5, 417a21-b2; Physics VIII.4, 255a33-b5).

@p
@greek ἆρα οὖν καὶ ὁ ἀνεπιστήμων
Is the man without knowledge, then, a knower, since he was potentially a knower? Or is it the man who is ignorant only by accident who is a knower? For it is not as ignorant that he is potentially a knower: it happened to him to be ignorant, but his soul, fit for it in itself, was what was potential, the very thing by which he is also a knower. So he still keeps the potential, and the man potentially a grammarian keeps it while already being a grammarian. Or nothing prevents it being so in another way too: in the first case only potentially, in this case the power having the form. If, then, the potential is the substrate, and the actual is the compound, the statue, what should we call the form on the bronze? It is not absurd to call the shape and the form the activity by which it is actually and not only potentially: not activity simply, but the activity of this thing. For we might perhaps more properly call something else activity, the one set against the power that brings on activity. For what is potential has its being actual from another; but a power has from itself the activity of what it can do: as a settled state and the activity named after it, courage and acting bravely. So much for that.
!flag but by virtue of Actuality (the abstract) | The Greek says the power has the activity "from itself" (παρ’ αὐτῆς): it does not act by virtue of an abstract Actuality.
!flag between a temperament and its expression in act | The Greek is ἕξις, a settled state, Aristotle's word for the state a virtue is, not a temperament.
!note A settled state (ἕξις) and its activity: so Aristotle, Nicomachean Ethics II.1 (1103a31-b2), "by doing brave acts we become brave".

@ch II.5.3 | In the intelligible world all is actual, and activity
There is no matter in the intelligible world in which the potential could be, and nothing there is waiting to become what it is not: so there is no potential there. Matter there is form, and its form is never separate but in thought; soul is not potentially what it becomes but a power of it. Intellect does not go from power to the activity of thinking: all the first things have from themselves, always, what they must have. If that nature is sleepless life, the best life, all there are actual and are activities, and the place there is the place of life.

@p
Now we must say why all this was said first: how "actually" is said in the intelligibles, and whether each and all there are only actual, or each is also activity, and whether the potential is there too. If there is no matter there in which the potential is, and nothing there is going to be what it is not already, nor does anything there change into something else, or, staying, beget something other, or, standing out of itself, give way to another to be in its place, then the potential would not be there, where all things are really, things that have eternity and not time. If, then, someone asked those who set matter in the intelligibles too whether there is not the potential there as well, after the matter there (for even if matter is there in another way, still in each thing there will be one part as matter, one as form, and the compound of both), what will they say? That what is as matter there is form too; since soul, being a form, would also be matter in relation to another. Is it not, then, potentially that other? No: for the form was its own, and does not come later, and is not separated except in thought; and so it has matter in that it is thought of as double, but both are one nature: as Aristotle too says that the fifth body is without matter.
!flag who (with us) assert Matter in the Intellectual Realm | "With us" is MacKenna's; it is true to Plotinus, who set out an intelligible matter in II.4 (chapters 2-5), but the Greek says only "those who set matter there too".
!note The fifth body is Aristotle's heavenly body (On the Heavens I.2-3); Aristotle allows it matter only for movement in place, not for coming to be (Metaphysics VIII.4, 1044b7-8; XII.2, 1069b24-26).

@p
@greek περὶ δὲ ψυχῆς πῶς ἐροῦμεν
But what shall we say about soul? It is potentially a living being, when it is not yet one but is going to be; and potentially musical, and all the other things it becomes, not being them always: so the potential is in the intelligibles too. Or rather it is not potentially these things: soul is the power of them. How, then, is the actual there? Is it as the statue, the compound, is actual, because each thing has received its form? Or because each is a form, and complete as what it is. For intellect does not go from a power of being able to think to the activity of thinking; for then it would need another, prior to it, which did not come from a power; the whole is in it. For what is potential wants something else to come upon it and bring it into activity, so that it may become something actually; but what has from itself what is always so, that would be actual. All the first things, then, are actual: for they have what they must have, from themselves and always. And so is soul, the soul that is not in matter but in the intelligible; and the other soul too, the one in matter, is actual, the soul of growth, for instance: for this one too is actually what it is.
!flag the Form-Idea has mastered each separate constituent | The Greek asks whether it is actual "because each has received its form" (ὅτι τὸ εἶδος ἕκαστον ἀπείληφεν); "mastered" is MacKenna's.
!flag (and not, like material things, merely in image) | MacKenna's words in brackets are his; the Greek says only that the soul of growth "too is actually what it is".

@p
@greek ἀλλ’ ἐνεργείᾳ μὲν πάντα
But if all are actual, are all of them in this way also activity? Or how? If that nature is rightly said to be sleepless, and life, and the best life, the most beautiful activities would be there. All, then, are both actual and activity, all are lives, and the place there is a place of life, and truly the principle and spring of soul and intellect.
!note "Sleepless" and "the best life" are said of Aristotle's god, whose life is "best and eternal" (Metaphysics XII.7, 1072b26-30) and who, if it thought nothing, would be like one asleep (XII.9, 1074b17-18). The "spring" (πηγή) of soul echoes Plato's Phaedrus (245c), where soul is "the spring and principle of motion".

@ch II.5.4 | Matter: actually none of the beings
Everything else that is potentially something is actually something else; but matter is potentially all beings, so it cannot be actually any of them. It is none of the things that come to be upon it, and as formless it is not one of the forms either: so it is non-being twice over. It has fled the true beings and cannot reach even the false ones, since it is not even a phantom of a forming principle: what could it be actually?

@p
All the other things, then, that are something potentially, also have being actually something else, which, already being, is said to be potentially in relation to another. But what of what is called matter, which we say is potentially all the beings: how can it be said to be actually any of the beings? Otherwise not all the beings would be potentially in it. If, then, it is none of the beings, it must necessarily not be a being itself. How, then, could it be actually anything, being none of the beings? Still, it would be none of these beings that come to be upon it; but nothing prevents it being something else, if indeed not all the beings are upon matter. But if it is none of the things upon it, and these are beings, it would be non-being. Nor indeed, imagined as something formless, would it be a form: so it could not be counted among those either. So in this way too it will be non-being. Being non-being on both counts, then, it will be non-being all the more. If it has fled the nature of what truly is, and cannot reach even the things falsely said to be, because it is not even a phantom of a forming principle as they are, in what kind of being could it be caught? And if in none, what could it be actually?
!flag this potentiality of the future mode of being is an existing mode | The Greek says the thing that is potential is already actually something else, and is said to be potentially in relation to another; it says nothing of a "future mode of being".
!flag it is not even a phantasm of Reason | The Greek is ἴνδαλμα λόγου, a phantom of a forming principle: the bodies that are "falsely said to be" are images of forming principles, and matter is not even that.
!note "What truly is" and what is falsely said to be: Plato's Timaeus (27d-28a, 52a-c) sets what always is against what comes to be and never is, and makes the receptacle a third kind, grasped "by a bastard reasoning".

@ch II.5.5 | Only potential, a dim phantom: matter must stay so
