Matter is only potential: its being is a promise of what is to come. It is non-being not as motion is different from being but as cast out from all being, never coloured by the forms it takes, caught at the end of both worlds. It is actually a phantom and a falsehood, and so truly non-being. To make it actual would destroy it, as taking away the falsehood takes away the being of false things: so, if matter is to last, it must be called only potential.

@p
How, then, shall we speak of it? How is it the matter of the beings? Because it is potentially. Then, because it is potentially, is it already what it is going to be? No: its being is only the promise of what is going to be; its being, as it were, is put off to what will be. What is potential, then, is not something, but potentially all things; being nothing by itself, but being what it is, matter, it is not actually either. For if it is actually something, that which it is actually will not be matter; it will not be matter in every way, but only as the bronze is. It would be non-being, then, not as different from being, as motion is: for motion rides upon being, coming as it were from it and being in it; but matter is as if cast out and wholly separated, unable to change itself, but always as it was from the beginning; and it was non-being. It was not actually anything from the beginning, standing away from all the beings, nor did it become anything: it has not even been able to take a colour from the things it wished to put on; but staying, being potentially towards something else, towards what came next, it appeared when those beings had already come to an end, and, caught by the things that came to be after it, it settled as the last of these too.
!flag annex for itself even a visible outline | The Greek says it could not even "be coloured" (χρωσθῆναι) by the things it wished to slip into: a colour, not an outline.
!flag it is remoter (from Real-Being) even than they | The Greek says that, caught by the things that came to be after it, it "settled as the last of these too" (ἔσχατον καὶ τούτων κατέστη): last in the order, not further from real being.
!note Non-being as "the different" from being, and motion as different from being and yet partaking in it, are the Eleatic Stranger's in Plato's Sophist (255e-256d, 257b, 258b).

@p
@greek ὑπ’ ἀμφοτέρων οὖν καταληφθεῖσα
Caught by both, then, it would be actually neither, but is left to be only potentially, something weak and dim, an image unable to be shaped. It is actually, then, an image; it is actually, then, a falsehood. And this is the same as being truly a falsehood, and this is really non-being. If, then, it is actually non-being, it is non-being more, and so really non-being. So it is far from being actually any of the beings, since its truth lies in non-being. If, then, it must be, it must not be actually, so that, having stepped out of true being, it may have its being in non-being; since, with things that are falsely, if you take away their falsehood, you take away whatever being they had; and if you bring activity into things that have their being and their substance in being potential, you have destroyed the cause of their existence, because their being was in potency. If, then, we must keep matter indestructible, we must keep it as matter: so we must say, it seems, that it is only potentially, so that it may be what it is; or else these arguments must be refuted.
!flag kept as the unchanging substratum | The Greek is ἀνώλεθρον, "indestructible": matter must not perish, which it would if it became actual.
!note Matter as a weak and dim image, truly false, is Plotinus' account in III.6 (chapter 7), where it is a "phantom" (φάντασμα) that holds nothing and promises everything.

@treatise II.6 | On substance, or on quality
Is being different from substance, substance being being together with motion, rest, sameness and difference? In the intelligible world all is substance, because all are one; here the images are divided, as the parts of a body are one in the seed and apart in the grown living thing. Some qualities seem to complete a substance, as white completes white lead and heat fire, and others are only added, as white is in a man. Plotinus answers that what completes a substance is not a quality at all but its activity or form, coming from its forming principles; quality is a disposition added to a substance already there, a trace, a shadow and an image of an activity that has left its substance behind. Nothing is both quality and not: what is cut off from substance is quality, what is with it is form or activity.

@ch II.6.1 | Being and substance; the qualities that seem to complete a substance
Is substance being with motion, rest, sameness and difference? There, all are substance because all are one; here the images are separated, as head and hand are together in the seed. Some qualities seem to be differences that complete a substance, as two-footed, or white in white lead and heat in fire; others are only qualities, added to a substance already complete. But it is absurd that one nature should be quality in one place and not in another. Perhaps the forming principles are wholly of substance, and what they bring about is a "something" there but only qualified here, so that we slip from the "something" to the qualified when we look at fire.

@p
Is being different from substance? Is being stripped of the others, while substance is being together with the others, motion, rest, sameness and difference, and these are its elements? The whole, then, is substance, and of those each is being, or motion, or something else. Motion, then, is a being by accident: is it, then, a substance by accident? Or does it complete substance? Or is it too a substance, and all the things there are substance? Why not here too, then? Because there all are one, but here, the images being divided, one is one thing and another another; as in the seed all things are together, and each is all, and hand is not apart and head apart, while here they are separated from each other: for they are images, and not true.
!flag envisage Being as the substance stripped of all else | The Greek says only "being stripped of the others" (τὸ μὲν ὂν ἀπηρημωμένον τῶν ἄλλων). MacKenna's "Reality" in this treatise stands for οὐσία, which the modern version calls substance.
!note Being, motion, rest, sameness and difference are the five greatest kinds of Plato's Sophist (254b-255e); Plotinus takes them as the kinds of the intelligible world in VI.2 (chapters 7-8). The seed in which all the parts are together is the Stoics' "seminal principle"; Plotinus uses it again in III.2.2 and V.9.6.

@p
@greek τὰς οὖν ποιότητας ἐκεῖ
Shall we say, then, that the qualities there are differences of substance, about substance or about being, differences that make substances different from one another, and substances on the whole? That is not absurd; but there is the question of the qualities here, some of which are differences of substances, as two-footed and four-footed, while others, not being differences, are called qualities for that very reason. And yet the same thing becomes a difference when it completes a substance, and is not a difference in something else, where it does not complete the substance but is an accident: as white completes a swan or white lead, but is an accident in you. Or rather the white in the forming principle completes it and is not a quality, while the white on the surface is a quality. Or we must divide quality: one kind belongs to the substance, being something proper to it; the other is only quality, by which a substance is qualified, the quality making no change into the substance or out of it, but, the substance being already there and completed, making a disposition from outside, an addition after the substance of the thing, whether it comes to be about a soul or about a body.
!flag elaborates the Reality into the particular thing | The Greek says the quality is "an addition after the substance of the thing" (μετὰ τὴν οὐσίαν τοῦ πράγματος προσθήκην); it says nothing of making the particular thing.
!note Two-footed and four-footed, the differences that divide the kind "animal", are the stock examples of Aristotle (Metaphysics VII.12).

@p
@greek ἀλλ’ εἰ καὶ τὸ ὁρώμενον λευκὸν
But what if the visible white on white lead completes it? On the swan it does not, since a swan could come to be not white; but on white lead it does. And heat does on fire. But what if someone said that fieriness is the substance, and something answering to it on the white lead? Still, the fieriness or heat of the visible fire completes it, and the whiteness the other. The same things, then, will complete and not be qualities, and will not complete and be qualities; and it is absurd to say that they are one thing where they complete and another where they do not, the nature being the same.
!note White lead (ψιμμύθιον) is lead carbonate, ground for a white paint and for women's face powder; its whiteness is what it is.

@p
@greek ἀλλ’ ἄρα τοὺς μὲν λόγους
Or are the forming principles that made them wholly of substance, while the things they bring about are there a "something", but here qualified, not a "something"? Hence we always go wrong about the "something": in seeking it we slip away and are carried down into the qualified. For fire is not what we call it when we look to the quality: fire is a substance; but what we see now, and look to when we speak, leads us away from the "something" and defines the qualified. And with things perceived by the senses this is reasonable: none of them is a substance, but affections of it. Hence that question too: how does substance come from what are not substances? It was said that what comes to be need not be the same as what it comes from; but now we must say that what comes to be is not a substance either. But how do we say that what we called substance there comes from what is not substance? We shall say that substance there, which has being more properly and more unmixed, is substance as being among the differences of being; or rather, it is called substance with the addition of activities: it seems to be the completion of being, but is perhaps more lacking by the addition and by not being simple, standing already away from being.
!flag (not a combination of material phenomena) | The words in brackets are MacKenna's; the Greek says only "fire is a substance".
!flag from the pure Being which is above Reality | MacKenna's gloss in brackets; the Greek asks only how substance there comes "from what is not substance" (ἐξ οὐκ οὐσίας), being and its activities.
!flag though in the truth it is a diminution | The Greek hedges: substance is "perhaps" (τάχα) more lacking than being; MacKenna makes it certain.
!note The "something" (τὸ τί) and the "qualified" (τὸ ποιόν) are Aristotle's categories of substance and quality (Categories 4, 1b25-27); the Stoics too named the substrate and the qualified as two of their kinds.

@ch II.6.2 | What quality is: a disposition added to a substance
If a quality completes not a substance but a qualified substance, what is the substance of fire before it is qualified? Not body, nor matter left when heat and brightness are taken away; the form, but the form is a forming principle, not a quality. What completes substances, like burning and whitening, are activities from the forming principles. Qualities are what come after substance: virtues and vices, beauty, health, being shaped, the arts. Quality is a disposition on substances already there, without which the substance would lack nothing, either easily moved or lasting.

@p
But we must look into what quality is on the whole: for once we know what it is, the difficulties may rather stop. First, then, we must ask whether the same thing is to be set down sometimes as only a quality and sometimes as completing a substance, without being troubled that a quality completes not a substance but rather a qualified substance. In the qualified substance, then, the substance, and what it is, must come before its being qualified. In fire, then, what is the substance, before the qualified substance? Is it the body? Then body will be the substance, and fire a hot body, and the whole not a substance; and the hot will be in it as the snub nose is in you. Take away, then, heat, brightness and lightness, which do seem to be qualities, and resistance, and what is left is what is extended three ways, and matter is the substance. But that does not seem right: the form is rather the substance. But the form is a quality. No: the form is not a quality but a forming principle.
!flag body being the Reality, fire is a warmed body | The Greek says "a hot body" (σῶμα θερμόν): heat that is in it, as the snub nose is in you, not heat given to it.
!note The snub nose, a quality that belongs only to a nose, is Aristotle's example (Metaphysics VII.5, 1030b16-1031a1); Socrates was snub-nosed (Plato, Theaetetus 143e). Body as what is extended three ways, with matter under it, is Aristotle's too (Metaphysics VII.3, 1029a11-26).

@p
@greek τὰ οὖν ἐκ τοῦ λόγου
What, then, is what comes from the forming principle and the substrate? Not what is seen and burns: that is a qualified thing. Unless someone says that burning is an activity from the forming principle; and then heating and whitening and the rest are makings, so that we shall have nowhere to leave quality. Or rather, those that are said to complete substances are not to be called qualities, since they are their activities, coming from the forming principles and from the powers of the substance; qualities are what are outside all substance, not appearing as qualities in some places and not qualities in others, but holding what is over and above, after the substance: such as virtues and vices, uglinesses and beauties, healths, and being shaped in a certain way. Triangle and square by themselves are not a quality; but being made triangular, in so far as it has been shaped, is to be called a quality: not the triangularity, but the shaping. So too the arts and the aptitudes. So quality is a disposition upon substances that already are, whether brought in or there from the beginning, such that if it were not there the substance would have nothing less. And it can be easily moved or hard to move: so it has two forms, one easily moved and one lasting.
!flag that is the something in which these qualities inhere | The Greek says "this is a qualified thing" (τοῦτο δὲ ποιόν): what is seen and burns is already qualified, not the subject in which the qualities are.
!flag the warming and lighting and other effects of fire | The Greek is τὸ λευκαίνειν, "whitening", not lighting.
!flag by which human beings take the quality | The words in brackets are MacKenna's; the Greek names only "the arts and the aptitudes".
!note Quality as a disposition easily moved, or a lasting state, is Aristotle's first kind of quality (Categories 8, 8b26-9a13), with shape his fourth (10a11-16).

@ch II.6.3 | Activity there, its trace here: quality as a shadow
The white on you is an activity from a power of whitening; the so-called qualities there are activities, which only look like qualities to us because each marks one substance off from another. Reason, taking what shows on the surface of a substance, begets a quality. Heat is the form and activity of fire, but in something else only a quality, a trace, shadow and image of its substance. Nothing is quality and not quality: what is stripped of substance is quality, what is with it is form or activity; what is never the form of anything is pure quality.

@p
The white on you, then, is to be set down not as a quality but plainly as an activity, from a power of whitening; and there all the so-called qualities are activities, which take on being qualities from our opinion, because each is a mark of its own, as it were marking the substances off from one another and having a character proper to themselves. How, then, will quality there differ, since these too are activities? Because they do not show what kind a thing is, nor a change in the substrates, nor a character, but only what is called quality there, being activity. So when it has the distinctiveness of a substance, it is plain at once that it is not a quality; but when reason separates what is distinctive in them, not taking it away from there but rather taking it and begetting something else, it begets a quality, taking as a part of substance what appeared to it on the surface. If so, nothing prevents heat too, being inborn in fire, from being a form of fire and an activity, and not its quality; and again, taken otherwise, in something else, from being only a quality, no longer the shape of a substance but a trace only, a shadow and an image, which has left behind its own substance, whose activity it is.
!flag (as do the corresponding Activities here) | The words in brackets are MacKenna's; the Greek sets no activities here beside them.
!note "Shadow" and "image" (σκιά, εἰκών) are the words of Plato's line in the Republic (VI, 509e-510a) for what lies below the things themselves; for Plotinus the qualities of bodies stand so to the activities of the intelligible.

@p
@greek ὅσα οὖν συμβέβηκε
Whatever is accidental, then, and not the activities and forms of substances, while providing some shapes, is qualified: so the states and the other dispositions of the substrates are to be called qualities, but their archetypes, in which they first are, are the activities of those things. And the same thing does not become quality and not quality: what is stripped of substance is quality, what is with it is form or activity; for nothing is the same when it stays in itself and when it has fallen into another, out of being form and activity. But what is never the form of anything else, but always an accident, is purely quality, and only that.
!flag other than those constituting the substratum of a thing | The Greek says the states and dispositions "of the substrates" (τῶν ὑποκειμένων) are qualities: they are in the substrates, not set against what makes them.

@treatise II.7 | On complete blending
Can two bodies, two liquids above all, be blended whole through whole, as the Stoics held? Some say the bodies only lie side by side, their matter apart, while their qualities mix: a blend through and through would cut the bodies into nothing, and the blend takes more room than either. Their opponents answer with sweat, with liquids soaking thin solids, and with the small quantity spread through the great. Plotinus' own view starts from water soaking wool and papyrus: the water's matter goes everywhere with its quality, and its size is added, not lost, as a quality is dimmed. Matter is bodiless, like the qualities; what stops bodies passing through one another is a quality, density. Last, corporeity is a forming principle which, coming to be in matter, makes a body.

@ch II.7.1 | The arguments on either side
Those who set bodies only side by side mix and do not blend. Those who blend only the qualities say that a blend through and through would cut the bodies to pieces, and that blends fill more room than either body, or else air goes out. Those who hold complete blending answer that sweat passes through the body without cutting it, and that size is brought along like the other qualities, so the blend has a size from both. But the others say a blend through and through would be like line laid on line, with no increase; and the small spread through the great is plain, but stretching it so far is not persuasive.

@p
We must look into what is called the complete blending of bodies, through and through. Can a liquid mixed with a liquid go whole through whole, each through the other, or one through the other? It makes no difference which, if it happens. Those who allow only setting side by side may be let go, as mixing rather than blending, if blending must make the whole of like parts, each smallest part made of the things said to be blended. Those who blend only the qualities, and set the matter of each body side by side, bringing on to it the qualities from each, might be persuasive in attacking complete blending: because the sizes of the masses would come to be cut into pieces, if there is no gap left in either body and the division is continuous, the one sinking into the other everywhere; and further, because the blended things take up a greater place than either one, as much as the places of both together. Yet if whole had gone through whole, they say, the place of the one into which the other was thrown should stay the same. And where the place does not become greater, they blame some going out of air, in place of which the other sank in. And how could the small, stretched out in the greater, pass through the whole of it? And they say many other things.
!flag must be whittled away if there is to be mixture without any gap | The Greek says the masses would be cut "into pieces" (εἰς τομάς), divided without end, not whittled.
!note "Complete blending" (κρᾶσις δι’ ὅλων) is the Stoics': two bodies, wine and water, wholly through each other, so that a drop of wine could blend with the whole sea (Diogenes Laertius VII.151; Plutarch, On common conceptions 37, 1078e). Setting side by side (παράθεσις), as of grains, the Stoics called mixing, not blending. Blending only the qualities is the Peripatetic answer: Aristotle says a drop of wine in ten thousand measures of water does not blend but turns into water (On Generation and Corruption I.10, 328a23-28), and Alexander of Aphrodisias argued against the Stoics in his On mixture.

@p
@greek οἱ δ’ αὖ τὴν δἰ ὅλων κρᾶσιν εἰσάγοντες
Those who bring in complete blending, on the other hand, could say that the bodies are cut but not used up in the cuts, even when the blending is through and through; for they will say that sweat too does not make cuts in the body, nor is the body pierced with holes. And if someone says that nothing prevents nature having made it so, so that sweat may pass through, still with things made by craft, when they are thin and continuous, a liquid is seen to wet them through and flow through to the other side. "But since they are bodies, how can this happen? For it is not easy to conceive of passing through without cutting; and cutting everywhere, they will plainly destroy one another." When they say that increases often do not happen, they give the others the going out of air to blame. As to the increase in place, it is hard; but still, what prevents saying that, since each body brings its size along with its other qualities, the increase comes of necessity? For size is not destroyed either, any more than the other qualities; and as there a different form of quality comes, mixed from both, so a different size, the size from both that the mixture makes.
!note Sweat passing through the skin, which has no holes we can see, was used on both sides; the Stoics took it as a body through a body.

@p
@greek ἀλλ’ ἐνταῦθ̓ ἂν πρὸς αὐτοὺς
But here the others might say to them: "If matter lies beside matter, and mass beside mass, with which the size goes, you would be saying what we say; but if matter too goes through and through, with the size that is first upon it, it would not happen as when line lies next to line, joining at the ends by their points, where there would be an increase, but as when line is laid on line, so that there is no increase." But the less goes through the whole of the greater, and the smallest through the greatest, where it is plain that it is blended. With things not seen it is possible to say that it does not reach everywhere; but where it plainly happens, it could not be said. And when they speak of the stretching of masses, they would not say very persuasive things, stretching the smallest mass so far: for they do not give the body a greater size by changing it, as if air came to be from water.
!flag one substance sinking its original magnitude in the other | The Greek says "if matter too goes through and through, with the size that is first upon it" (μετὰ τοῦ ἐπ’ αὐτῇ πρώτως μεγέθους): the matter goes with its size, rather than sinking it.

@ch II.7.2 | Plotinus' own view: water in wool and papyrus
When water runs through wool or drips from papyrus, the whole wet body passes through, and the papyrus is wet everywhere: so the water's matter is everywhere, with its quality. The papyrus swells: the water's size is added, not swallowed up, for size joined to size does not vanish as a quality mixed with another is dimmed. Why should body passing through body cut it, when bodiless qualities do not? Matter too is bodiless. What stops solids is a quality, density, or the number of qualities: so things mix or fail to mix not as qualities or as matter, but as having such qualities.

@p
This itself must be looked into on its own, what happens when the mass of water that was becomes air, and how the greater is in what has come to be; for now let these things be said, among many others said by each side. Let us look for ourselves at what we should say about this: what opinion agrees with what is said, or what other will appear beside those now said. When water runs through wool, then, or papyrus lets the water in it drip out, how does the whole watery body not pass through it? Or even when it does not run, how shall we join matter to matter and mass to mass, and make only the qualities blend? For the water's matter will not lie outside the papyrus, nor in some gaps in it: for all of it is wet, and nowhere is matter empty of the watery quality. And if the matter is everywhere with the quality, the water is everywhere in the papyrus.
!flag without regard to the agreement or disagreement of that theory | The Greek asks "what opinion agrees with what is said, or what other will appear" (τίς δόξα σύμφωνος τοῖς λεγομένοις): the agreement is what Plotinus looks for.
!flag when papyrus-pulp gives up its moisture | The Greek is ἡ βίβλος, papyrus, the reed or a sheet of it; the "pulp" is MacKenna's.

@p
@greek ἢ οὐ τὸ ὕδωρ
"Not the water, but the water's quality." But where is the water, then? And how is the mass not the same? Or what was added stretched the papyrus: for it took size from what came in. But if it took it, some mass was added; and if added, it was not swallowed up in the other; so the matter must be in one place and in another. Or what prevents that, as one body gives some of its quality to the other and takes from it, so too with size? For a quality coming together with a quality is not that quality but with another, and in being with another, not being pure, is not wholly that, but has been dimmed; but a size coming together with another size does not vanish.
!flag communicate quality and receive matter | The Greek says "one body gives of the quality and takes from the other" (δίδωσι τῆς ποιότητος καὶ λαμβάνει σῶμα θάτερον παρὰ θατέρου): "body" is what gives and takes, not matter received.

@p
@greek τὸ δὲ σῶμα χωροῦν διὰ σώματος
How it is said that body passing through body must make cuts, one might stop and ask: for we too say that the qualities pass through bodies and make no cuts. Because they are bodiless? But if matter itself is bodiless too, why, matter being bodiless and the qualities too, if they were such as to be few, do they not pass through with the matter in the same way? Do solids not pass through because they have qualities such that they are stopped from passing through, or because many together cannot do this with matter? If, then, the number of qualities makes what is called a dense body, the number would be the cause; but if density is a quality of its own, like what they call corporeity, the cause is that quality. So qualities will not make the mixing as qualities, but as qualities of a certain kind; nor again will matter fail to mix as matter, but as matter with a certain quality; and most of all if it has no size of its own, except that it does not throw off size. Let these things, then, be gone through in this way too, as difficulties.
!flag how we came to say that body passing through | The Greek says "one might stop and ask how it is said" (πῶς λέγεται): the claim that body cuts body is the opponents', not Plotinus' own.
!note That matter is bodiless, and has no size of its own but takes it, is argued in II.4 (chapters 8-12), Taylor's On matter.

@ch II.7.3 | What makes a body: corporeity
Is corporeity all the qualities together with matter, or a form and forming principle that, coming to be in matter, makes a body? If a forming principle, it holds all the qualities in itself; and, if it is not a definition but makes the thing, it does not contain matter but is about it, so that body is matter with the principle in it, while the principle is a form, bare, though never apart. The separable principle is another, the one in intellect.

@p
Since we have mentioned corporeity, we must look into whether corporeity is what is made up of all the qualities together with matter, or a form and a forming principle which, coming to be in matter, makes a body. If body is what is made up of all the qualities together with matter, corporeity would be that same thing. And if it is a forming principle which, coming on, makes the body, plainly the principle holds all the qualities embraced in itself. But this principle, if it is not merely like a definition showing what the thing is, but a principle making a thing, must not take in matter along with itself, but be about matter, and, coming to be in it, complete the body; and the body is matter and the principle in it, while the principle itself, being a form, is seen bare, without matter, even if it is as inseparable as can be. For the separable principle is another, the one in intellect; and it is in intellect because it is itself intellect. But these things belong elsewhere.
!flag that other Principle in man | The Greek speaks of "the separable" forming principle, "the one in intellect" (ὁ ἐν νῷ); nothing is said of man.
!note A definition (ὁρισμός) that shows what a thing is, against a principle that makes it: Aristotle's formula (λόγος) is both the definition and the form (Metaphysics VII.10-11).

@treatise II.8 | On sight, or why distant things look small
Why do distant things look smaller, and close together? Perhaps because the light is drawn together to the size of the pupil, and the form arrives without its size; or because we know a size by going through it part by part; or because we see colour first and size only by accident, so that as colours come faint, sizes come small, as sounds do. It is plainest with varied things, mountains with houses and trees: where the forms of the parts do not reach us, sight cannot measure the whole; and things of one colour deceive too. The usual answer, the smaller angle of sight, fails: a mountain may fill the whole sight and still look smaller, and so does the sky.

@ch II.8.1 | Why distant things look small
Distant things look small because the light is drawn together to the pupil and only the form arrives; or because size is known by going through the parts; or because colour is seen first and size by accident, so that as colour fades size shrinks, as sound grows less. Hearing knows a sound's strength directly, its size only by accident. Varied things show it best: where the forms of houses and trees on a mountain do not reach us, sight cannot measure the whole. Things of one colour deceive too, and the distant looks near because the stretch between is not gone through.

@p
Do distant things look smaller, and things far apart seem to have little between them, while things near look as large as they are, and as far apart as they are? Distant things seem smaller to those who see them because the light wants to be drawn together towards the sight and to the size of the pupil; and the further off the matter of what is seen is, the more the form arrives as if alone, stripped of the form of its size and of its quality too, so that only its forming principle arrives. Or else because we perceive how great a size is by going through it and passing over it part by part: so it must be present and near to be known for how great it is. Or else because size is seen by accident, colour being seen first. Near, then, we know how much is coloured; far off, only that it is coloured; and the parts, divided with it by quantity, do not give an exact discerning of the quantity, since the colours themselves arrive faint. What wonder, then, if sizes too, like sounds, are smaller as their form comes faint? For there too hearing seeks the form, and perceives the size by accident.
!flag stripped, so to speak, of magnitude as of all other quality | The Greek goes on: "so that only its forming principle arrives" (ὡς τὸν λόγον αὐτοῦ ἀφικνεῖσθαι μόνον); MacKenna leaves it out.
!flag by observing the salience and recession of its several parts | The Greek says we know a size "by going through it and passing over it" part by part (ἐν διεξόδῳ καὶ ἐπελεύσει): sight travels over the parts; nothing is said of parts standing out or falling back.
!note Size is one of Aristotle's "common sensibles", perceived by more than one sense, and colour the "proper sensible" of sight (On the Soul II.6, 418a7-20; III.1, 425a14-20).

@p
@greek ἀλλὰ περὶ τῆς ἀκοῆς
But as for hearing, does it perceive size by accident? For to what does the size in a sound belong first, as the size of what is seen seems to belong to touch? Or rather hearing perceives the seeming size not by quantity but by more and less, not by accident, as the strength of it, just as taste perceives the strength of the sweet; but the size of a sound in the proper sense is how far it reaches; and this it would signify by accident from the strength, but not exactly. For the strength is the same at each point, while the amount goes over all the place the sound has covered. "But colours are not small but faint, while sizes are small." Or rather what is common to both is the less. In colour, the less is faint; in size, the less is small; and the size, following the colour, is lessened in proportion.
!flag exactly as the palate perceives by no indirect knowledge | The Greek of the Workshop ends the comparison with "by accident" (κατὰ συμβεβηκός); the modern version, like MacKenna, takes the sense to be that taste, like hearing, knows the strength directly, and leaves the words out.

@p
@greek σαφέστερον δὲ ἐπὶ τῶν ποικίλων
The experience is clearer with things that are varied: mountains, say, with many houses, a host of trees and many other things; each of these, if seen, lets one measure the whole from the things seen one by one; but when the form does not come for each, sight, which measures by each, is deprived of knowing how great the whole underlying size is. For even things near, when they are varied and one's glance falls on them all at once and not all the forms are seen, would look smaller in proportion to how much of each is stolen from view; but when all are seen, they are measured exactly and known for how great they are. And sizes of one look and one colour deceive about their quantity too, since sight cannot measure them part by part at all, because, measuring part by part, it slips off, for it has no difference at which to stop at each part. And the distant looks near because the stretch between is drawn together, how great it is, for the same reason. For how great the near is does not escape us, for the same reasons; but not going through the distant part of the interval, what it is like in form, sight could not say how great it is in size either.
!flag It was the detail that prevented a near object deceiving | The Greek says only that "how great the near is does not escape us, for the same reasons" (διὰ τὰ αὐτά): seeing the forms of its parts.

@ch II.8.2 | Not the smaller angle: the mountain and the sky
The smaller angle of sight does not explain it. Whoever says so leaves part of the sight seeing something else outside the object, air at least. But a mountain may fill the whole sight, or stretch beyond it, and still look much smaller than it is; and the sky, even if the whole sight took in the whole hemisphere, looks many times smaller than it is.

@p
That things look smaller because the angles of sight are smaller has been said elsewhere not to be so; and now this must be said: whoever says that a thing looks smaller under a smaller angle leaves the sight seeing something outside it, or something else outside altogether, such as air. When, then, it leaves nothing, because the mountain is large, but either the mountain is equal to the sight and the sight can no longer see anything else, its extent fitting what is seen, or what is seen even stretches beyond the sight's reach on either side, what would one say here, when the thing looks much smaller than it is, and yet is seen with the whole sight? And if one looked at the heaven, one would learn it beyond dispute. For no one could see the whole hemisphere with one glance, nor could the sight be poured out so far, stretching up to it. But if anyone wishes, let it be granted. If, then, the whole sight took in the whole, and the size of the heaven is many times that of what appears, how could one make the lessening of the angle the cause of its looking much less than it is?
!flag No part of the eye is unoccupied | The Greek speaks of the sight (ἡ ὄψις) that the mountain leaves nothing of, not the eye.
!flag the eye directed to it could not cover so vast an expanse | The Greek says the sight could not "be poured out" (χυθῆναι) so far, "stretching up to it": sight goes out from the eye, as in Plato's Timaeus.
!note The angle of sight is the geometers' account: in Euclid's Optics (definitions 1-4) sight goes out from the eye in straight rays that make a cone, and things seen under a greater angle look greater. The ray of sight going out from the eye is also Plato's (Timaeus 45b-d). Which treatise "elsewhere" means is not known.

# Ennead III (milestone E10 onward). MacKenna's vol. II (1921) prints the Third Ennead first; his chapters of III.1 are
# Volkmann's (vol. II, pp. 1-10).

@treatise III.1 | On destiny
Everything that comes to be has a cause, and the near causes are easy to find; but why do the same causes give different results? Some answer with bodies, atoms or elements; some with one soul, the Stoics' destiny, running through the whole; some with the motion of the stars; some with a chain of causes strung on one another. Plotinus answers each in turn. Atoms could give no order and no foretelling, and nothing that soul does. One soul doing everything would leave nothing to be ours, and would do away with causes. The stars give heat and cold to bodies, but not character or skill, and they signify rather than make, like letters that can be read. The chain of causes leaves us only a name of freedom. What saves order, foretelling and ourselves is soul, each one's along with the soul of the whole: a first cause, free without body, partly mastered by what it meets in body. What it does by its own pure reason is up to us; what it suffers from outside may be called destiny.

@ch III.1.1 | Everything has a cause; the near causes
Either all things have causes, or none, or some, among the things that come to be and the things that always are. The first eternal things have no cause beyond them; their activities are traced to their substances. But all that comes to be has a cause: no empty swerve, no sudden motion, no impulse of a soul with nothing moving it. The near causes are easy to find: a reason for going to the market, medicine for health, a treasure for wealth, a father and a fit wife for a child; and, in general, nature.

@p
Everything that comes to be, and everything that is, either comes to be and is through a cause, or both are without a cause. Or, in both, some things are without a cause and some with one. Or all that comes to be has a cause, while of the things that are, some have a cause and some do not, or none has. Or, the other way round, all the things that are have a cause, while of the things that come to be, some have one and some do not, or none of them has. Now among eternal things, the first cannot be traced back to other causes, since they are first; but those that hang from the first may have their being from them. And whoever gives the activities of each should trace them back to their substances: for this is their being, to give out such an activity.

@p
@greek περὶ δὲ τῶν γιγνομένων ἢ ὄντων μὲν ἀεί
As for things that come to be, or things that always are but do not always have the same activity, we must say that all of them come to be through causes. What has no cause is not to be admitted. We must give no room to empty swerves, nor to a sudden movement of bodies that arose with nothing going before it, nor to a senseless impulse of a soul, with nothing moving it to do something it did not do before. By this very thing a greater necessity would hold the soul: that it would not be its own, but would be carried by such movements, unwilled and without cause. For either something willed moved it, outside it or inside, or something desired; or, if nothing it reached for moved it, it would not have moved at all.
!flag we can make no place here for unwarranted | The Greek says "empty swerves" (παρεγκλίσεσι κεναῖς): the swerve of Epicurus' atoms, named by its own word; MacKenna's "unwarranted slantings" hides whose doctrine is meant.
!note The swerve (παρέγκλισις, Lucretius' clinamen, On the nature of things II.216-293) is the small motion without cause by which Epicurus let atoms meet and left room for free will. Both Plotinus and the Stoics reject motion without a cause (Cicero, On fate 18-25).

@p
@greek γιγνομένων δὲ πάντων κατ’ αἰτίας
Since everything comes to be through causes, it is easy to grasp the near causes of each thing and trace it back to them. For going to the market, say: thinking one must see someone or collect a debt, and in general choosing this or that and setting out for it, and then its seeming right to each to do this. Some things can be traced back to the crafts: the cause of getting well is medicine and the doctor. Of getting rich, a treasure found, or a gift from someone, or making money from labour or a craft. Of a child, the father, and any other help towards begetting that came from outside, one thing from one place and another from another: such and such foods, say, or, a little further off, seed that flows well for begetting, or a wife fit for bearing. And in general, we trace things back to nature.
!flag by manual or intellectual labour | The Greek says "from labours or a craft" (ἐκ πόνων ἢ τέχνης); "intellectual" is MacKenna's.
!flag or, more immediately, a special organic aptitude | The Greek says "a little further off" (ὀλίγῳ προσώτερα), not more immediately, and names "a good flow towards begetting" (εὔροια εἰς παιδοποιίαν), not an aptitude of the organs.

@ch III.1.2 | Going further back: the answers of the schools
To stop at the near causes is lazy: the same moon shines, and one man steals and another does not; the same air, and one falls sick. So men went further back. Some set down bodies, atoms or others, and made everything, our impulses too, slaves to their necessity. Others, the Stoics, run one cause, destiny, through all things, so that even our thoughts move as the ruling part moves an animal's limbs. Others make the revolution of the heavens and the figures of the stars the cause of all. Others string all causes on one chain from above. Plotinus will take them in order, the first first.

@p
To come this far and stop, and not want to go higher, is perhaps the mark of a lazy man, one who does not listen to those who climb to the first causes and to the causes beyond. For why, when the same things happen, when the moon shines, say, does one man steal and another not? And when like things come from the surrounding air, why does one fall ill and another not? And why is one rich and another poor from the same work? Ways of life, characters and fortunes that differ call on us to go further back. And so, never standing still, some set down bodily principles, such as atoms: by their motion, their blows and their tangling with one another, they make each thing be and come to be as it is, as those atoms are put together and act and are acted on; and our impulses and dispositions too, they say, are as the atoms make them. So they bring this necessity, the one that comes from the atoms, into the things that are. And if someone gives other bodies as principles, and has everything come from them, he makes the things that are slaves to the necessity that comes from those bodies.
!flag even upon Real Being | The Greek says only "into the things that are" (εἰς τὰ ὄντα), the things of this world; MacKenna's capitals make it the intelligible world.

@p
@greek οἱ δὲ ἐπὶ τὴν τοῦ παντὸς ἀρχὴν ἐλθόντες
Others go up to the principle of the universe and bring everything down from it. They say a cause runs through all things, and that it not only moves each thing but makes it; they set it down as destiny, and as the most sovereign cause. And not only all else that comes to be, but our own thoughts too, they say, come from its movements, as the parts of an animal each move not from themselves but from the ruling part in each animal. Others say that the revolution of the universe holds all things and makes all things by its motion, and by the relations and figures of the planets and the fixed stars towards one another; trusting in the foretelling that comes from these, they hold that each thing comes to be from there.
!flag the leading principle which animal life presupposes | The Greek says "the ruling part in each animal" (τοῦ ἡγεμονοῦντος ἐν ἑκάστῳ τῶν ζῴων), the Stoics' word for the commanding part of the soul; "which animal life presupposes" is MacKenna's.
!note The Stoics called destiny (εἱμαρμένη) a cause running through all things, the reason of the cosmos (Chrysippus in Stobaeus, Eclogues I.79; Cicero, On divination I.125-126), and the ruling part (ἡγεμονικόν) the part of the soul that moves the rest, as a spider in the middle of its web.

@p
@greek καὶ μὴν καὶ τὴν τῶν αἰτίων ἐπιπλοκὴν
And if someone speaks of the weaving together of causes with one another, and of the chain from above, the later things always following the earlier and going back to them, since they came to be through them and would not have come to be without them, and the later being slaves to what is before them, he will plainly be bringing in destiny in another way. One would not miss the truth if he set these too down as two kinds: for some hang everything from some one thing, and others do not. We shall speak about these. But now the argument must go to the first; then, in order, we must look at the others.
!flag Of this last opinion we will have something to say | The Greek says "we shall speak about these" (λεχθήσεται δὲ περὶ τούτων), "but now we must go to the first" (ἐπὶ τοὺς πρώτους), "then look at the others in order": the first named, the atoms, are taken first, in chapter 3.
!note "The chain" (εἱρμός) is the Stoic word: destiny, they said, is the chain of causes (Aetius I.28.4; Cicero, On fate 20).

@ch III.1.3 | Against the atoms
To hand everything to bodies is absurd, and atoms most of all. Even granting them, there would be no order, and so no foretelling by craft or by inspiration. Atoms may push bodies, but no blow of an atom makes a reasoning, an impulse or a thought, or makes the soul resist the body, or makes one man a geometer and another wise. The same holds against other bodies: they warm and chill, but the works of soul come from another principle.

@p
To hand all things over to bodies, whether atoms or what are called elements, and to have their disorderly motion beget order, reason and the soul that leads, is absurd and impossible both ways; but more impossible, if one can say so, is the one from atoms. Many true arguments have been given about these. But even if someone did set down such principles, it would not follow of necessity, either, that there is necessity over all things, or destiny in some other way. Suppose, first, that there are atoms. They will move, some downwards (let there be a down), others sideways, as it happens, each one differently. Nothing, then, will be in order, since there is no order; and what comes to be, once it has come to be, will simply be. So there would be no foretelling or prophecy at all, neither by a craft (for how could there be a craft of things without order?), nor by inspiration and possession by a god: for here too what is to be must be fixed.
!flag A good deal of truth has resulted | The Greek says "many true arguments have been given about these" (πολλοὶ εἴρηνται λόγοι ἀληθεῖς), that is, against the atoms; MacKenna makes the theory itself yield truth.
!flag admitting a down and an up | The Greek has only "let there be a down" (ἔστω γάρ τι κάτω); the "up", and MacKenna's words in brackets on the universe, are his.
!flag this Universe, when it achieves existence, is all order | The Greek says "what has come to be, when it has come to be, is wholly so" (τὸ δὲ γενόμενον τοῦτο, ὅτε γέγονε, πάντως): it is settled only after the event, which is why nothing can be foretold. Nothing is said of the universe or of order.

@p
@greek καὶ σώμασι μὲν ἔσται
Bodies, struck by the atoms, will have to suffer of necessity whatever the atoms bring. But to what motions of atoms will anyone trace the works and experiences of the soul? What blow of an atom, carried down or bumping in from anywhere, will make reasonings of this kind or impulses of that kind, or reasonings, impulses or thoughts at all, be necessary, or be at all? And what of when the soul opposes the experiences of the body? By what motions of atoms will one man be forced to be a geometer, another look into arithmetic and astronomy, and another be wise? For our own work, and our being living things, will be lost altogether, if we are carried where the bodies lead, pushing us like soulless bodies. The same can be said to those who set down other bodies as the causes of all things: these can warm and chill us, and destroy what is weaker; but none of the works that soul does could come from them. These must come from another principle.
!flag lead a third to the philosophic life | The Greek says "another will be wise" (ὁ δὲ σοφὸς ἔσται): wisdom, not a way of life.

@ch III.1.4 | Against one soul doing all things
Perhaps one soul runs through the whole and does everything, each thing moving as a part, as a plant's parts all move from its root: that would be destiny. But so much necessity does away with destiny and the chain of causes: as our limbs move by our ruling part, not by destiny, so all would be one, and nothing would come from causes. We would not be we; our plans would be another's reasoning. Each must be itself, with acts and thoughts of its own, and the shameful must not be laid on the universe.

@p
Or is it that one soul, reaching through the whole, accomplishes all things, each thing being moved by it as a part, as the whole leads; and that, as the causes are carried from there, their continuous succession and weaving together must follow, and that is destiny? As if one were to say of a plant, which has its principle from the root, that the weaving together reaching from there to all its parts and to one another, the acting and being acted on, is one management and, as it were, the destiny of the plant. But first, this very excess of necessity and of such a destiny does away with destiny itself, and with the chain and weaving together of causes.
!note "One management" (διοίκησις) is the Stoics' word for the soul or reason that runs the cosmos as a household; the cosmos as one living thing, all its parts in sympathy, is theirs (Diogenes Laertius VII.138-143).

@p
@greek ὡς γὰρ ἐν τοῖς ἡμετέροις μέρεσι
For with our own parts, which move as the ruling part moves them, it is unreasonable to say they move by destiny: what gave the movement is not one thing, and what received it and used the impulse from it another; what first moved the leg is the ruling part itself. In the same way, if in the universe too what acts and is acted on in everything is one, and things do not come one from another by causes that always lead back to something else, then it is not true that all things come to be by causes: all things will be one. So we are not we, and no work is ours. We do not reason ourselves; our plans are someone else's reasoning. Nor do we act, any more than our feet kick; it is we who kick, through parts of ourselves. But each thing must be each thing; there must be acts and thoughts that are ours; each one's fine and shameful acts must come from each one himself, and we must not lay the doing of shameful things, at least, on the universe.
!flag dictated by the mind and will | The Greek says the parts move "as the ruling part" moves them (κατὰ τὸ ἡγεμονοῦν), the Stoics' word; nothing is said of mind or will.
!flag the mind itself (and not destiny) is the prime mover | The Greek says "what first moved the leg is that" (ἐκεῖνό ἐστι πρῶτον τὸ κινῆσαν τὸ σκέλος): the ruling part both gives and is the movement, and what receives it is the leg, not the mind, as MacKenna has it.

@ch III.1.5 | Against the stars as makers
Perhaps the stars, by their positions, set out each thing: astrologers foretell from them, plants and animals grow and wane in sympathy with them, and places differ in their looks and tempers by their relation to the sun. But this too gives what is ours to the stars and leaves us stones. Climate and parents give the body its blend and likeness; yet men alike in looks differ most in character and thought. If the stars make what they foretell, birds make what they signify. And the astrologers tell of good birth, of the fortunes of parents from children and of brothers from brothers, things already settled before the birth; they say likeness and beauty come from home; and men and beasts born under the same figures differ.

@p
But perhaps it is not in this way that each thing is accomplished, but the revolution that manages all things, and the motion of the stars, sets out each thing as the stars stand towards one another, at the midheaven and at rising, at setting and in conjunction. At any rate, from these men divine and foretell about what will be in the universe and about each man, how he will fare in fortune and, not least, in mind. And one sees, they say, the other animals and plants growing and waning from their sympathy with the stars, and suffering other things from them; and the places on earth differ from one another by their relation to the universe and above all to the sun; and on the places follow not only the other plants and animals, but men's looks and heights and colours, their tempers and desires, their pursuits and characters. So the revolution of the universe is lord of all things.
!flag show a different produce according to their situation | The Greek says the places on earth "differ from one another" (διαφέροντας ἀλλήλων εἶναι) by their relation to the universe and the sun; the "produce" is MacKenna's.
!note The midheaven, rising and setting are three of the astrologers' four "centres" (κέντρα) of a nativity; that places and climates shape men's looks and tempers is the argument of the Hippocratic Airs, waters, places 12-24 and of Ptolemy, Tetrabiblos II.2. Plotinus has argued against the stars as causes at length in II.3 (milestone E8).

@p
@greek πρὸς δὴ ταῦτα πρῶτον μὲν ἐκεῖνο ῥητέον
To this we must say, first, that this man too, in another way, lays what is ours on the stars: our plans and passions, our vices and impulses. He gives us nothing, and leaves us to be rolling stones, not men who have a work from themselves and from their own nature. We must give what is ours to us, while some things come from the universe into what is already ours and our own. We must distinguish what we do from what we suffer of necessity, and not lay everything on the stars. From places and the difference of the surrounding air come heat or cold, say, into our blend; and things come from parents too: we are like our parents, mostly in looks, and in some of the soul's unreasoning passions. Yet even among those alike in looks because of their places, the greatest difference is seen in character and in thought, as if such things came from another principle. And here too the soul's struggles against the blends of the body and against desires could fitly be named.
!flag our entire personality | The Greek has "our vices and impulses" (κακίας τε καὶ ὁρμάς); "our entire personality" is MacKenna's.
!flag A further confirmation is found in the efforts we make to correct | The Greek speaks of "the struggles against the blends of the bodies and the desires" (αἱ ... ἐναντιώσεις): resisting them, not correcting them.
!note The blend (κρᾶσις) is the body's mixture of hot, cold, wet and dry, what later medicine called temperament.

@p
@greek εἰ δ’ ὅτι εἰς τὴν τῶν ἄστρων σχέσιν
If, because they look at the relation of the stars and tell what comes to be in each case, they infer that it is made by the stars, then likewise birds would make what they signify, and so would everything the diviners look at when they foretell. One could look into this more exactly from the following. What someone foretells by looking at the relation the stars had when each man was born, they say also comes to be from the stars, the stars not only signifying it but making it. When, then, they speak of good birth, as being from famous fathers and mothers, how can one say that these things are made by the stars, when they belong to the parents before the relation of the stars from which they foretell? And they tell the fortunes of parents from the birth of their children, and from the parents what the children's dispositions will be and what fortunes they will meet, about those not yet born; and from brothers the deaths of brothers, and from wives what concerns their husbands, and the other way round. How then could the relation of the stars at each man's birth make what is already said, from his fathers, to be going to be so? For either those earlier relations will be what make it, or, if they do not make it, neither do these.
!flag a girl's fate includes that of a future husband | The Greek says "from wives what concerns their husbands, and the other way round" (ἐκ γυναικῶν τὰ περὶ τοὺς ἄνδρας): husbands and wives already married; the "future" husband is MacKenna's.

@p
@greek καὶ μὴν καὶ ἡ ὁμοιότης
And they themselves say that likeness in looks to the parents, and beauty and ugliness, come from the home, and not from the motion of the stars. And it is reasonable that at the same times all kinds of other animals and men are born together; all of them should have the same lot, if they have the same relation of the stars. How, then, do men come at the same time, and the other things at the same time, from the same figures?
!flag this implies that ugliness and beauty are so caused | The Greek says the astrologers themselves say that likeness, "and beauty and ugliness", come from home (οἴκοθέν φασι καὶ κάλλος καὶ αἶσχος ἰέναι); it is their own word, not an inference.

@ch III.1.6 | What the stars give, and what they signify
Each thing comes to be by its own nature, a horse from a horse, a man from a man. Let the revolution of the heavens help, and the stars give heat and cold to bodies. But how could they give characters and pursuits, a grammarian, a geometer, a dice-player? How could stars that are gods give wickedness, or do harm because they set, as if setting from our view changed them? They move to keep the whole safe; and they serve besides as letters, from which those who know the grammar read the future, as a high-flying bird signifies high deeds.

@p
But in fact each thing comes to be according to its own nature: a horse because it is from a horse, a man because he is from a man, and such a man because he is from such a man. Let the revolution of the universe help as well, granting much to what comes to be; let the stars give many things bodily to the body, heats and colds and the blends of bodies that follow from them. How then do they give characters and pursuits, and above all those that do not seem to be slaves to the blend of bodies: a grammarian, say, a geometer, a dice-player, and the inventor of any of these? And how could wickedness of character be given by stars that are gods? And in general, how could they give all the evils they are said to give when they are afflicted, because they set and go under the earth? As if they suffered something different when they set from our view, and were not always carried on the heavenly sphere and keeping the same relation to the earth.
!flag wreak vengeance, as for a wrong | The Greek says the stars give evils "when afflicted" (κακούμενοι), the astrologers' word for a planet in a bad place; vengeance and wrong are MacKenna's.

@p
@greek οὐδὲ λεκτέον, ὡς ἄλλος ἄλλον ἰδὼν
Nor must we say that one god becomes worse or better by seeing another in this or that position, so that when they are well off they do us good, and harm us if the opposite. Rather, they are carried round for the preservation of the whole, and they also give another service: those who know such a grammar look at them as at letters, and read the future from their figures, working out what is signified by analogy; as if one were to say that, since the bird is high-flying, it signifies some high deeds.
!flag on which the augur, acquainted with that alphabet | The Greek says "those who know such a grammar" (τοὺς τὴν τοιαύτην γραμματικὴν εἰδότας), readers of the stars; the augur, who reads birds, comes only in the comparison.
!note The stars as letters written in the heavens are taken up again in II.3.7 (milestone E8).

@ch III.1.7 | Against the one chain of causes
Last, the one principle that strings all things together and accomplishes everything by seed-principles. It is close to the view that all comes from the soul of the whole, though it would grant us something. But it holds every necessity: with all the causes taken in, nothing can stop anything. Our impressions follow what went before, our impulses follow them, and "what is up to us" is only a name: we would be like animals, infants and madmen, and fire too has its impulses. Everyone sees this, and looks for other causes.

@p
It remains to look at the principle that weaves all things together and, as it were, strings them on one another, and brings upon each thing how it shall be; it is set down as one, and from it everything is accomplished according to seed-principles. This opinion too is close to the one that says every state and movement, ours and every one of all things, comes from the soul of the whole, even if it wishes to grant us, and each thing, something towards doing something of our own. But it holds the necessity of all things in every way; and when all the causes are taken in, each thing cannot fail to come to be: for there is nothing any more to stop it or to make it come out otherwise, if all things have been taken in by destiny.
!note Seed-principles (λόγοι σπερματικοί) are the Stoics': the reason of the cosmos holds the principles of all things as seeds, and unfolds them in order (Diogenes Laertius VII.136; Aetius I.7.33).

@p
@greek τοιαῦτα δὲ ὄντα ὡς ἀπὸ μιᾶς ἀρχῆς
Such causes, set going as from one principle, will leave us nothing but to be carried wherever they push. For our impressions will follow what went before, and our impulses will follow them, and "what is up to us" will be only a name. It will not be anything more because it is we who have the impulse, when the impulse comes about according to those causes. What is ours will be like that of the other animals, and of infants, who go by blind impulses, and of madmen: for these have impulses too. Yes, by Zeus, and fire has impulses, and so has everything that is a slave to its own make-up and is carried along by it. Everyone sees this and does not dispute it; but they look for other causes of this impulse, and do not stop at it as at a principle.
!flag personal action becomes a mere word | The Greek says "what is up to us will be only a name" (ὄνομά τε μόνον τὸ ἐφ̓ ἡμῖν ἔσται): "what is up to us" is the standing term for freedom in Aristotle and the Stoics.
!flag we seek for other explanations of our action | The Greek says "everyone sees this and does not dispute it, but looking for other causes of this impulse, they do not stop" at it as at a principle (τῆς ὁρμῆς ταύτης ἄλλας αἰτίας ζητοῦντες): all men look past impulse for its causes.
!note Impressions (φαντασίαι) and impulse (ὁρμή) are the Stoics' account of action: an impression, assented to, sets off an impulse. Chrysippus answered that assent is still ours, as a cylinder rolls by its own shape once pushed (Cicero, On fate 42-43).

@ch III.1.8 | Soul, the other principle
What cause leaves nothing uncaused, keeps order, lets us be something, and saves foretelling? Soul: not only the soul of the whole but each one's, a first cause, not born of seeds. Without a body it is master of itself and free; in a body it is ranked with other things, and the chances it meets lead much of what it does. The better soul masters more, the worse less: the worse gives way to the body's blend, and is humbled by poverty or made a tyrant by power; the good soul changes things rather than being changed.

@p
What other cause, then, coming in beside these, will leave nothing without a cause, keep sequence and order, allow us to be something, and not do away with foretelling and prophecy? We must bring soul into the things that are as another principle: not only the soul of the universe, but each one's soul along with it, as a principle of no small account, to weave all things together. It does not itself come to be, like other things, from seeds, but is a cause that acts first. Without a body, then, it is most of all its own master, free, and outside the cause that rules the cosmos. But when it is brought into a body, it is no longer master of everything, since it has been ranked with other things.
!flag we must place at the crest of the world of beings | The Greek says we must "bring in soul into the things that are, as another principle" (ἀρχὴν οὖσαν ἄλλην ἐπεισφέροντας εἰς τὰ ὄντα); the "crest" is MacKenna's.
!flag bodiless and therefore supreme over itself | The Greek says "being without body, it is most master of itself" (ἄνευ μὲν οὖν σώματος οὖσα): while it is without a body, set against when it is brought into one; MacKenna makes it a soul that is bodiless.

@p
@greek τύχαι δὲ τὰ κύκλῳ πάντα
Chances, all the things around it, among which it fell when it came into the midst, led much of what it does; so that some things it does because of them, while in others it masters them and leads them where it wishes. The better soul masters more, the worse less. For the soul that gives way somewhat to the body's blend is forced to desire or to be angry, and is humbled by poverty, or puffed up by wealth, or a tyrant with power. But the other, the soul that is good by nature, holds out even in these same things, and changes them rather than being changed by them, so that it alters some, and gives way to others without vice.
!flag largely led by secondary causes | The Greek says "chances" (τύχαι), "all the things around it": what it happens to meet, not secondary causes; MacKenna gives the right word only in brackets.

@ch III.1.9 | What is up to us
What comes of choice and chance blended is necessary: given all the causes, all things happen. When the soul acts as moved from outside, blindly, or uses wrong impulses because it is worse of itself, the act is not voluntary. Only when it moves by its own reason, pure and unaffected, is the impulse up to us: our own work, from within, from the pure soul, not from a soul led astray by ignorance or beaten by desires.

@p
Necessary, then, are the things that come about from choice and chances blended: for what else could there still be? When all the causes are taken in, all things happen entirely; and among the causes outside is whatever is brought about by the revolution of the heavens too. When, then, the soul, altered by things outside, acts and has an impulse as if using a blind motion, its act and its disposition must not be called voluntary; nor when, being worse of itself, it uses impulses that are not right in everything, and not leading.
!flag this compromise between evil and accidental circumstance | The Greek says "choice and chances blended" (προαιρέσει καὶ τύχαις κραθέντα): choice (προαίρεσις), Aristotle's word for deliberate decision, not evil.

@p
@greek λόγον δὲ ὅταν
But when it has its own reason as leader, pure and unaffected, and has its impulse from that, only this impulse must be said to be up to us and voluntary. This is our own work, which did not come from elsewhere but from within, from the soul when it is pure, from a first principle that leads and is master; not from a soul that has gone astray from ignorance or been beaten by the force of desires, which come upon it and lead it and drag it, and no longer let there be works of ours, but things we suffer.
!note "Voluntary" (ἑκούσιον) and "up to us" (ἐφ’ ἡμῖν) are Aristotle's terms (Nicomachean Ethics III.1-5); for Plotinus only the act of the pure soul, ruled by reason, fully deserves them, as he argues again in VI.8, On free will and the will of the One.

@ch III.1.10 | The sum
All things are signified and come to be by causes of two kinds: some by soul, some by the things around. Souls that act by right reason act of themselves; when hindered, they suffer more than they act. So the causes of not being wise lie elsewhere, and these acts may be called destiny's; but the best come from us, as we are when we are alone. The good do fine things, and it is up to them; the others, when they are let breathe, not taking their wisdom from elsewhere, but only not hindered.

@p
In the end, then, the argument says that all things are signified, and come to be, by causes, but that these are of two kinds: some things come to be by soul, others through other causes, the things around. Souls, when they act by right reason, do what they do of themselves; in other things they are hindered from doing their own, and suffer rather than act. So the causes of not being wise are other things; and it is perhaps right to say that these acts are done by destiny, for those at least who think destiny is a cause from outside. But the best things come from us. For we are of this nature when we are alone. And good men do fine things, and it is up to them; the others do fine things insofar as they are let breathe again, not taking their wisdom from elsewhere when they are wise, but only not being hindered.
!flag Unwisdom, then, is not due to the Soul | The Greek says "the causes of not being wise are other things" (τοῦ μὲν μὴ φρονεῖν ἄλλα αἴτια εἶναι): the things around, which hinder the soul.
!flag when the passions are in abeyance | The Greek says only "insofar as they are let breathe again" (καθ̓ ὅσον ἄν ἀναπνεύσωσι συγχωρηθέντες); the passions are MacKenna's.
!note "When we are alone" (ὅταν μόνοι ὦμεν): the soul by itself, without what the body and the chances add; the same thought ends the Enneads, "the flight of the alone to the alone" (VI.9.11).
